simply follows the top line in
W^{n}
since
W^{n}_{i}(0^{-})=0
for all
i
and
W^{n}
is ordered, Proposition
2.4
(ii). Therefore it is disjoint from
\pi_{n}(x,y)
if
\pi_{n}(x,y)
has its final jump after time
y
. The final jump of
\pi_{n}(x,y)
occurs at the time
Z^{n}_{2}(x,y)
, so the probability in (
4.17
) is bounded above by

\mathbb{P}(Z^{n}_{2}(x,y)>y-\epsilon).

Lemma
4.9
implies that the sequence

(Z^{n}_{i}(x,y),i=2,3,\dots)

(4.18)

is tight in
n
. Moreover,
W_{k}^{n}(\cdot)=A^{0}_{n,k}(\cdot)
, where
A^{0}_{n,k}
is as in the statement of Theorem
4.4
. Therefore by the assumption in Theorem
4.4
, the pair
(W^{n}_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N},Z^{n}_{i}(x,y),i\geq 2)
is jointly tight, where the underlying topology is the Skorokhod topology for the paths
W^{n}_{k}
. Subsequential limits are of the form
(\mathfrak{A}_{k},k\in\mathbb{N},Z_{i},i\geq 2)
, where
\mathfrak{A}
is a parabolic Airy line ensemble.
To complete the proof of the lemma, it suffices to show that in such a subsequential limit,
Z_{2}<y
almost surely.

Consider such a subsequential limit. By Skorokhod’s representation theorem, we can find a coupling of the environments such that along some subsequence
(W^{n}_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N},Z^{n}_{i}(x,y),i\geq 2)\to(\mathfrak{A}_{k},k\in\mathbb{N},Z_{i},i\geq 2)
almost surely. Since Skorokhod convergence implies uniform convergence on compact sets when the limit is continuous, along this subsequence
W^{n}_{k}\to\mathfrak{A}_{k}
compactly for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, almost surely

W^{n}[(Z^{n}_{k}(x,y),k)\to(y,1)]\to\mathfrak{A}[(Z_{k},k)\to(y,1)].

In particular, since the points
Z^{n}_{i}(x,y),i=2,\dots,k
are jump times on a geodesic in
W^{n}
from
(Z^{n}_{k}(x,y),k)
to
(y,1)
, the points
\{Z_{i}:i\in[2,k]\}
are the jump times on a geodesic in
\mathfrak{A}
from
(Z_{k},k)
to
(y,1)
.

The asymptotics of
Z^{n}_{k}(x,y)
from Lemma
4.9
imply that almost surely, the infimum

K=\inf\{k\in\{2,3,\dots\}:Z_{k}<y-1\}

is finite. If
K=2
, then
Z_{2}<y
as desired. If not, then the points
\{Z_{i}:i\in\{2,\dots K-1\}\}
are jump times on a geodesic in
\mathfrak{A}
from
(y-1,K-1)
to
(y,1)
. Now, by Proposition
3.1
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
the top
k
lines of
\mathfrak{A}
restricted to the interval
[y-1,y]
are absolutely continuous with respect to
k
independent Brownian motions. Therefore for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, almost surely all jump times on any geodesic in
\mathfrak{A}
from
(y-1,k-1)
to
(y,1)
are contained in the open interval
(y-1,y)
. In particular,
Z_{2}<y
almost surely on the event
K>2
.
∎

Lemma 4.11
.

For every
x>0
and
y<z\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\pi_{n}(x,y)\text{ and }\pi_{n}(x+\epsilon,z)\text{ are essentially disjoint})=0.

Proof.

There exist essentially disjoint geodesics from
p_{1}\to q_{1}
and
p_{2}\to q_{2}
in any environment
f\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{Z}}
if and only if

f[p_{1}\to q_{1}]+f[p_{2}\to q_{2}]=f[(p_{1},p_{2})\to(q_{1},q_{2})].

Since last passage values for arbitrary disjoint paths from line
n
to line
1
are preserved by
W
by Proposition
2.4
(i), this implies that there exist essentially disjoint geodesics from
(a_{n}+x,n)\to(y,1)
and
(a_{n}+x+\epsilon,n)\to(z,1)
in
W^{n}
if and only if this holds in
L^{n}
. By the same argument, this holds if and only if there exist essentially disjoint geodesics in the reverse melon
W^{*}_{z}L^{n}
from
(z-y,n)\to(-a_{n}+z-x,1)
and
(0,n)\to(-a_{n}+z-x-\epsilon,1)
.

Since the environment reversed at
z
satisfies the same assumptions as the original environment after a shift by
a_{n}
, we can apply Lemma
4.10
to
W^{*}_{z}L^{n}
to complete the proof.
∎

Lemma
4.11
and Lemma
4.9
can be combined to give bounds on the branching points of geodesics in
W^{n}
.

Corollary 4.12
.

Define

K_{n}(x;y,z)=\inf\{k\in\mathbb{N}:Z^{n}_{k}(x,y)=Z^{n}_{k}(x,z)\}.

For every triple
x>0,y<z
, the sequence
K_{n}(x;y,z)
is tight.

Proof.

Recall the notation
\pi(x,y)
for the rightmost geodesic in
W^{n}
from
(a_{n}+x,n)
to
(y,1)
. We also let
\pi^{*}_{n}(x,y;x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\pi(x,y)\cap\pi(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
. This intersection is a (possibly empty) path by Lemma
2.2
.

Let
\epsilon>0
. Observe that
\pi^{*}_{n}(x,z;x+\epsilon,y)=\pi^{*}_{n}(x,y;x+\epsilon,z)
whenever the rightmost geodesics
\pi_{n}(x,y)
and
\pi_{n}(x+\epsilon,z)
are not disjoint. Moreover, on this event,
\pi^{*}_{n}(x,y;x,z)
is given by the union of
\pi^{*}_{n}(x,z;x+\epsilon,y)
with the initial segment of the geodesic
\pi_{n}(x,z)
. That is

\pi^{*}_{n}(x,y;x,z)=\pi^{*}_{n}(x,z;x+\epsilon,y)\cup\{(r,m)\in\pi_{n}(x,z):\exists(r^{\prime},m^{\prime})\in\pi^{*}_{n}(x,z;x+\epsilon,y)\text{ s.t. }r\leq r^{\prime}\}.

This uses both the monotonicity and tree structure of rightmost geodesics established in Lemma
2.2
.
Therefore
K_{n}(x,y,z)\leq k
on the event where

•

\pi^{*}_{n}(x,z;x+\epsilon,y)
intersects the top
k-1
lines in
W^{n}
, and

•

\pi_{n}(x,y)
and
\pi_{n}(x+\epsilon,z)
are not disjoint.

Now fix
\delta>0
. The second condition holds with probability at least
1-\delta
for all large enough
n
as long as
\epsilon
is small enough by Lemma
4.11
. The first condition holds whenever

Z^{n}_{k-1}(x,z)<Z^{n}_{k}(x+\epsilon,y),

(4.19)

as this implies that the paths
\pi(x,z)
and
\pi(x+\epsilon,y)
must cross in the top
k
lines of
W^{n}
. For any fixed
\epsilon>0
, the asymptotics in Lemma
4.9
imply that (
4.19
) happens with probability at least
1-\delta
for all large enough
n
as long as
k
is large enough. Since
\delta>0
was arbitrary,
K_{n}(x,y,z)
is tight.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
4.4
.

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

The assumptions of the theorem guarantee that

S_{n}(a,\cdot)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{A}_{1}(\cdot-a)\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad S_{n}(\cdot,a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{A}_{1}(a-\cdot)

for every
a\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Since the process
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
is continuous, Lemma
4.3
then implies that
S_{n}|_{[-r,r]^{2}}
is tight in
\operatorname{CDF}_{r}
for every
r\in\mathbb{N}
, and all distributional limits of
S_{n}|_{[-r,r]^{2}}
are continuous. Consider any joint distributional subsequential limit of the sequence
(S_{n}|_{[-r,r]^{2}},r\in\mathbb{N}),n\in\mathbb{N}
. This limit must be of the form
(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}|_{[-r,r]^{2}},r\in\mathbb{N})
for some continuous function
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
.
We will show that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is an Airy sheet. The compact convergence in Theorem
4.4
then follows from Skorokhod’s representation theorem, Lemma
4.2
and continuity of the Airy sheet.

To prove that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
is an Airy sheet, we just need to show that

\tilde{\mathcal{S}}|_{[z,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}_{[z,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}}

for every
z\in\mathbb{R}
where
\mathcal{S}
is an Airy sheet. Since the assumptions of Theorem
4.4
are invariant with respect to shifting
z
, and since
\mathcal{S}
is shift invariant by definition, it is enough to prove this for
z=0
.

By Skorokhod’s representation theorem, we can find a subsequence of
\{S_{n}\}
and a coupling of the corresponding environments
L_{n}
for which the following convergences all hold almost surely:

•

The functions
A^{0}_{n,k}(\cdot)=L_{n}[(a_{n},n)\to_{\Delta_{k}}(\cdot,1)]
converge compactly to the Airy line ensemble
\mathfrak{A}_{k}(\cdot)
.

•

S_{n}\to\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
compactly and
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x,x+\cdot)
is a parabolic Airy process for rational
x
.

•

For every
x\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}
and
y,z\in\mathbb{Q}
, each of the jump times
Z^{n}_{k}(x,y)
converges to a limit
Z_{k}(x,y)
and
K_{n}(x;y,z)
converges to a limit
K(x;y,z)
. Moreover,

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{Z_{k}(x,y)}{\sqrt{k}}=\frac{-1}{\sqrt{2x}}.

(4.20)

This uses Lemma
4.9
and Corollary
4.12
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be an Airy sheet coupled to
\mathfrak{A}
by the relationship (
3.1
). By continuity of
\tilde{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{S}
, it is enough to show that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x,y)=\mathcal{S}(x,y)
for all
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}
. For
x\in\mathbb{Q}^{+},y<z\in\mathbb{Q}
the third condition above guarantees that for all
k\geq K(x;y,z)
we have

\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x,y)-\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x,z)=\mathfrak{A}[(Z_{k}(x,z),k)\to(z,1)]-\mathfrak{A}[(Z_{k}(x,z),k)\to(y,1)].

By the asymptotics in (
4.20
), for any
\epsilon>0
, for all large enough
k

we can apply the quadrangle inequality, Lemma
4.1
, to the points
(Z_{k}(x,z),k),(-\sqrt{k/(2(x+\epsilon))},k)
and
(z,1)
,
(y,1)
in the environment
\mathfrak{A}
. This gives the lower bound

\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x,y)-\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x,z)\geq\mathfrak{A}[(-\sqrt{k/(2(x+\epsilon))},k)\to(z,1)]-\mathfrak{A}[(-\sqrt{k/(2(x+\epsilon))},k)\to(y,1)].

Formula (
3.1
) for
\mathcal{S}
then implies that

\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x,y)-\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x,z)\geq\mathcal{S}(x+\epsilon,y)-\mathcal{S}(x+\epsilon,z)

for all
\epsilon>0
. By the same reasoning, the opposite inequality holds for all
\epsilon<0
. The continuity of
\mathcal{S},\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
then implies that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x,y)-\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x,z)=\mathcal{S}(x,y)-\mathcal{S}(x,z)
for rational
x,y,z
. This extends to all
x\in\mathbb{Q}^{+},y,z\in\mathbb{R}
, since both
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x,\cdot)
and
\mathcal{S}(x,\cdot)
are continuous for rational
x
, and hence

\mathcal{S}(x,\cdot)=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x,\cdot)+C_{x}

for random constants
C_{x},x\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}
. Since
\mathcal{S}(x,\cdot)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x,\cdot)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathfrak{A}_{1}(\;\cdot\;-x)
, ergodicity of the Airy process
\mathfrak{A}_{1}(t)+t^{2}
(equation (5.15) in
Prähofer and Spohn 2002
), implies that
\mathcal{S}(x,\cdot)=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x,\cdot)
for rational
x
, as desired.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

The proof of Theorem
4.4
actually shows something stronger than just convergence of
S_{n}
to
\mathcal{S}
. Namely, letting
A_{n,k}(\cdot)=L_{n}[(a_{n},n)\to_{\Delta_{k}}(\cdot,1)]
, it shows that there is a coupling of the environments
L_{n}
such that the functions
(S_{n},A_{n})
converge compactly to
(\mathcal{S},\mathfrak{A})
, where
\mathcal{S}
is an Airy sheet and
\mathfrak{A}
is a parabolic Airy line ensemble, coupled via the relationship in Definition
3.3
.

5
Directed metrics

In Sections
5
to
11
, we develop an abstract framework for deciding when a sequence of last passage percolation models that converges to Airy sheet also converges to the directed landscape. To study this question, we introduce directed metric spaces, which generalize metric spaces. In contrast with the metric property, the directed metric property is preserved under certain natural scaling operations. The directed landscape and all last passage percolation models are random directed metrics.

Definition 5.1
.

A
directed metric of positive sign
on a set
S
is a function
d:S\times S\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
satisfying:

•

d(x,x)=0
for all
x\in S
,

•

(Triangle Inequality)
d(x,z)\leq d(x,y)+d(y,z)
for all
x,y,z\in S
.

We call the pair
(S,d)
a
directed metric space
.

A metric is always a directed metric of positive sign. A directed metric of positive sign is a generalization of a metric without the symmetry condition
d(x,y)=d(y,x)
and the positivity condition
d(x,y)>0
whenever
x\neq y.

A
directed metric of negative sign
is a function
d:S\times S\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
satisfying
d(x,x)=0
for all
x\in S
and the reverse triangle inequality
d(x,z)\geq d(x,y)+d(y,z)
for all
x,y,z\in S
. Equivalently, a function
d
is a directed metric of negative sign if
-d
is a directed metric of positive sign.

Directed metrics of negative sign will be important to us later on because last passage models can be viewed in this way. However, for the remainder of this section, we restrict our attention to directed metrics of positive sign since the two notions are equivalent up to a sign change, and directed metrics of positive sign can more naturally be thought of as distance functions.

There are a few standard methods for building new directed metrics from old ones. These methods are summarized by the following lemma, whose proof we leave as a straightforward exercise.

Lemma 5.2
.

1.

Let
g
be a function from a set
R
to a directed metric space
(S,d)
. Then the
pullback
of
d
, defined by

d^{\prime}(x,y)=d(g(x),g(y))

is a directed metric on
R
.

2.

If
d_{1},\dots d_{k}
are directed metrics on a space
S
and
c_{1},\dots,c_{k}\geq 0
, then
c_{1}d_{1}+\dots+c_{k}d_{k}
is also a directed metric on
S
.

3.

If
d_{1},d_{2}
are directed metrics on
S
, then
\max(d_{1},d_{2})
is also a directed metric on
S
.

4.

If
\{d_{n}\}
is a sequence of directed metrics on
S
with a pointwise limit
d:S\times S\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
, then
d
is a directed metric on
S
.

5.

If
I
is a collection of directed metrics on
S
then the function
d:S\times S\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
given by
d(u)=\sup\{e(u):e\in I\}
is also a directed metric.

6.

For any function
h:S\to\mathbb{R}
, the function
d_{h}(x,y)=h(x)-h(y)
is a directed metric of both positive and negative sign.

We will combine points 2, 4, and 6 later to take limits of directed metrics after centering and rescaling.

The method of building directed metrics in Lemma
5.2
.5 allows us to associate a canonical directed metric to any function from
S\times S
to
\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 5.3
.

Let
S
be a set,
A\subset S\times S
, and
d_{0}:A\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
. If there is a directed metric
d
so that
d|_{A}\leq d_{0}
, then we can define a directed metric on
S
by

e(d_{0})(u)=\sup\{d(u)\}

where the supremum is over all directed metrics satisfying
d|_{A}\leq d_{0}
.
This
induced directed metric

e(d_{0})
is the maximal directed metric on
S
that is bounded above by
d_{0}
on
A
.

We will also want to study short paths in directed metric spaces. To do this, we introduce an abstract definition of geodesics. This definition has the advantage that it does not require any extra structure on the space
(S,d)
.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
(S,d)
be a directed metric space and let
x,y\in S
be such that
d(x,y)
is finite. A subset
A\subset S
is a
geodesic set
from
x
to
y
if there exists a total order
\preceq
on
A
such that

•

x\preceq z\preceq y
for all
z\in A
. In other words,
x
and
y
are minimal and maximal elements in
(A,\prec)
.

•

d(a,c)=d(a,b)+d(b,c)
for all triples
a\preceq b\preceq c\in A
.

We call
\preceq
a compatible total order on
A
.
We can put a partial order on all geodesic sets from
x
to
y
by inclusion. Maximal elements in this partial order are called
geodesics
.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
(S,d)
be a directed metric space, and let
x,y\in S
be points with
d(x,y)<\infty
. Let
A
be a geodesic set from
x
to
y
in
(S,d)
. Then
A
is contained in a geodesic
\pi
from
x
to
y
.

Since
\{x,y\}
is always a geodesic set from
x
to
y
in
(S,d)
, this implies that there is a geodesic between every pair of points
x,y\in S
with
d(x,y)<\infty
.

To prove Proposition
5.5
, we need to investigate how different compatible total orders on geodesic sets are related. For this, we introduce a fundamental equivalence relation.

Definition 5.6
.

Let
(S,d)
be a directed metric space. We say that
x
and
y
are
d
-equivalent and write
x\sim y
if
d(x,y)+d(y,x)=0
.

By the triangle inequality,
d(x,y)+d(y,x)\leq d(x,x)=0
for every pair
x,y
. If two points
x
and
y
are
d
-equivalent then this is an equality, and we can move back and forth between
x
and
y
at zero cost. Note that if
x\sim y
, then both
d(x,y)
and
d(y,x)
are finite.
If
d
is a true metric, then
d
-equivalence is a trivial relation. The next lemma gives two important properties of
d
-equivalence.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
(S,d)
be a directed metric space.

1.

If
x,y,z\in S
and
y\sim z
, then

d(x,z)=d(x,y)+d(y,z)\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad d(z,x)=d(z,y)+d(y,x).

(5.1)

2.

The relation
\sim
is an equivalence relation.

Proof.

For any points
x,y,
and
z
, by two triangle inequalities we have that

d(x,z)\leq d(x,y)+d(y,z)\leq d(x,z)+d(z,y)+d(y,z).

(5.2)

If
y\sim z
, then the right and left hand sides of (
5.2
) are equal. Hence all inequalities in (
5.2
) are in fact equalities, yielding the first equation in (
5.1
). The second equation follows by symmetric reasoning.

All parts of checking that
\sim
is an equivalence relation are self-evident except for transitivity. For transitivity, if
x\sim y
and
y\sim z
, then both equalities in (
5.1
) hold. Adding these two equations together and using that
x\sim y
and
y\sim z
gives that
d(x,z)+d(z,x)=0
.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
(S,d)
be a directed metric space, and let
x,y\in S
with
d(x,y)<\infty
.
Let
A
be a geodesic set from
x
to
y
and let
q
be the quotient map from
A
to
{\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$A$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\sim$}\right.}
. Then there exists a unique total order
\preceq
on
{\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$A$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\sim$}\right.}
such that
\leq
is a compatible order on
A
if and only if
x\leq z\leq y
for all
z\in A
and

a\leq b\qquad\text{ implies }\qquad qa\preceq qb.

(5.3)

In other words, Lemma
5.8
says that compatible total orders on a geodesic set
A
from
x
to
y
are all equivalent up to rearranging points in the same equivalence class.

Proof.

Consider points
a,b,c\in A
, and
a^{\prime}\sim a,b^{\prime}\sim b,c^{\prime}\sim c
. By repeated applications of Lemma
5.7
.1, we have

\displaystyle d(a,c)

\displaystyle=d(a,a^{\prime})+d(a^{\prime},c^{\prime})+d(c^{\prime},c),\qquad\;\text{and}\;

(5.4)

\displaystyle d(a,b)+d(b,c)

\displaystyle=d(a,a^{\prime})+d(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})+d(b^{\prime},c^{\prime})+d(c^{\prime},c).

(5.5)

Moreover, since
a\sim a^{\prime}
and
c\sim c^{\prime}
, both
d(a,a^{\prime})
and
d(c,c^{\prime})
are finite. Therefore

\displaystyle d(a,c)=d(a,b)+d(b,c)\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad d(a^{\prime},c^{\prime})=d(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})+d(b^{\prime},c^{\prime}).

(5.6)

We use this to prove the lemma.
Let
\leq
be a compatible total order on
A
. We can construct an induced order
\preceq
on
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$A$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sim$}\right.}
by setting
w\prec z
for
w\neq z
whenever
a<b
for some
a\in q^{-1}w,b\in q^{-1}z
. We first check that
\prec
is well-defined. For this, suppose that
a,a^{\prime},b,b^{\prime}\in A
with
a<b,a^{\prime}>b^{\prime}
with
a\sim a^{\prime}
and
b\sim b^{\prime}
. We just need to check that
a^{\prime}\sim b^{\prime}
. Since
a<b
and
a^{\prime}>b^{\prime}
, we have

d(x,b)=d(x,a)+d(a,b)\qquad\;\text{and}\;\qquad d(x,a^{\prime})=d(x,b^{\prime})+d(b^{\prime},a^{\prime}).

(5.7)

Applying (
5.6
) with the points
x,b,a
and
x,b^{\prime},a^{\prime}
to the first equation above, we get that

d(x,b^{\prime})=d(x,a^{\prime})+d(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}).

Combining this with the second equation in (
5.7
) gives that
d(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})=-d(b^{\prime},a^{\prime})
, as long as the distances
d(x,b^{\prime}),d(x,a^{\prime})
are both finite. This is guaranteed by the assumption that
d(x,y)<\infty
and the second condition on geodesic sets applied to the triples
x,a^{\prime},y
and
x,b^{\prime},y
.

We now show that
\preceq
satisfies the if and only if statement in the theorem. First suppose that
\leq^{\prime}
is a total order on
A
with
x\leq^{\prime}y\leq^{\prime}z
for all
z
, satisfying (
5.3
). We show that
\leq^{\prime}
is compatible.
Let
a\leq^{\prime}b\leq^{\prime}c
. By the construction of
\preceq
, we can find
\hat{a}\in q^{-1}qa,\hat{b}\in q^{-1}qb,\hat{c}\in q^{-1}qc
such that
\hat{a}\leq\hat{b}\leq\hat{c}
, and hence

d(\hat{a},\hat{c})=d(\hat{a},\hat{b})+d(\hat{b},\hat{c}).

Applying (
5.6
) to the points
a,b,c
and
\hat{a},\hat{b},\hat{c}
then shows that
\leq^{\prime}
is compatible.

Now, suppose that
\leq^{\prime}
is any total order on
A
. For
\leq^{\prime}
to be compatible, we need
x
and
y
to be minimal and maximal elements in
\leq^{\prime}
. We check that
\leq^{\prime}
must satisfy (
5.3
) by contradiction. Suppose that (
5.3
) fails. Then there exists
a<^{\prime}b\in A
with
qb\prec qa
. This implies that
b<a
in the original order on
A
, and so

d(x,a)=d(x,b)+d(b,a).

Moreover, since
qb\prec qa
, we have
a\not\sim b
and hence
d(a,b)>-d(b,a)
. Therefore using that
d(x,a)
and
d(x,b)
are finite, we get that
d(x,b)<d(x,a)+d(a,b)
. Thus
<^{\prime}
is not compatible.
∎

We can use this structure of compatible orders in geodesic sets to find geodesics in directed metric spaces.

Proof of Proposition
5.5
.

Let
I
be the set of all geodesic sets from
x
to
y
containing
A
, and let

I^{\prime}=\{(A,\prec):A\in I,\prec\text{ compatible}\}.

We can put a partial order on
I^{\prime}
by saying that
(A,\prec)\leq(A^{\prime},\prec^{\prime})
if
A\subset A^{\prime}
and if
\prec^{\prime}|_{A\times A}=\;\prec
. Any totally ordered subset
J\subset I^{\prime}
has an upper bound in
I^{\prime}
given by the union of all sets in
J
with the union of all the total orders. Hence by Zorn’s lemma,
I^{\prime}
contains a maximal element. We will show that for any two maximal elements
(A,\prec),(A^{\prime},\prec^{\prime})
in
I^{\prime}
, that if
A\subset A^{\prime}
or
A^{\prime}\subset A
, then
A=A^{\prime}
. This will imply that
A
and
A^{\prime}
are maximal elements in the original set
I
, and are therefore geodesics.

For this, suppose
A\subset A^{\prime}
.
Let
\prec^{\prime}_{*}
be the total order induced on
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$A^{\prime}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sim$}\right.}
by
\prec^{\prime}
via the quotient map
q:A^{\prime}\to{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$A^{\prime}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sim$}\right.}
and consider an arbitrary total order
\triangleleft
on
A^{\prime}
extending
\prec
. Define a new total order
\prec^{\prime\prime}
on
A^{\prime}
where
a\prec^{\prime\prime}b
if
qa\prec^{\prime}_{*}qb
and
a\triangleleft b
. The ‘only if’ part of Lemma
5.8
implies that
\prec^{\prime\prime}
is an extension of
\prec
, and the ‘if’ part of Lemma
5.8
, implies that
\prec^{\prime\prime}
is compatible on
A^{\prime}
. Hence
(A,\prec)\leq(A^{\prime},\prec^{\prime\prime\prime})
so by maximality,
A=A^{\prime}
. By symmetry, if
A^{\prime}\subset A
then
A^{\prime}=A
as well.
∎

By Proposition
5.5
, for any pair of points
x,y\in S
for which
d(x,y)
is finite, there is at least one geodesic from
x
to
y
since
\{x,y\}
is always a geodesic set from
x
to
y
. Note that geodesics may not always yield any interesting information about the space (i.e. the pair
\{x,y\}
could be the only geodesic from
x
to
y
, or the entire space
S
could be a geodesic between any pair of points).

We can use Proposition
5.5
to show that geodesics behave well under pullbacks.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
g
be a surjective function from a set
R
to a directed metric space
(S,d)
, and let
e
be the pullback metric on
R
. Let
x,y\in R
be such that
e(x,y)<\infty
. Then

\{A:A\subset R\text{ is an $e$-geodesic from $x$ to $y$}\}=\{g^{-1}B:B\subset S\text{ is a $d$-geodesic from $gx$ to $gy$}\}.

Proof.

The inverse image of a geodesic set
B
in
(S,d)
is a geodesic set in
(R,e)
. To see this, observe that if
\prec
is a compatible total order on
B
, then by breaking ties in an arbitrary way we can find a total order
\preceq^{\prime}
on
g^{-1}B
such that
x\prec^{\prime}y
whenever
gx\prec gy.
The order
\prec^{\prime}
makes
g^{-1}B
a geodesic set.

On the other hand, if
A
is a geodesic set from
x
to
y
, then we claim that
gA
is a geodesic set from
gx
to
gy
. To do this, we just need to find a compatible total order on
A
whose pushforward onto
gA
is also a well-defined total order.
Let
q:S\to{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$S$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sim$}\right.}
be the quotient map on
S
for
d
-equivalence. Then
q^{\prime}=qg
is the quotient map on
R
for
e
-equivalence. Let
\preceq
be the total order on
q^{\prime}A
identified via Lemma
5.8
. Let
\preceq_{S}
be any partial order on
gA
such that
a\preceq_{S}b
implies
qa\preceq qb
, and let
\preceq_{R}
be any partial order on
A
such that
a\preceq_{R}b
implies
ga\preceq_{S}gb
. Then by Lemma
5.8
,
\preceq_{R}
is a compatible order on
A
. Its pushforward onto
S
is then well-defined: it is simply
\preceq_{S}
.

Now, let
A
be a geodesic from
x
to
y
in
R
. Then
gA
is a geodesic set from
gx
to
gy
. Since
e(x,y)=d(gx,gy)
, we have that
d(gx,gy)<\infty
. Therefore by Proposition
5.5
, there is a geodesic
B
from
gx
to
gy
so that
gA\subset B
. We want to show that
A=g^{-1}B
. For this, observe that
g^{-1}B
is a geodesic set from
x
to
y
in
S
. Since
A\subset g^{-1}B
, maximality implies
A=g^{-1}B.

On the other hand, let
B
be a geodesic from
gx
to
gy
in
S
, and let
A
be a geodesic between points
x
to
y
containing the geodesic set
g^{-1}B
. Again,
A
exists by Proposition
5.5
. Then
B\subset gA
, and
gA
is a geodesic set from
gx
to
gy
. Therefore
gA=B
by maximality and thus
g^{-1}B=A
.
∎

We end this section by noting that certain subsets of geodesics are geodesics. The proof of this lemma is straightforward and left for the reader.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
(S,d)
be a directed metric and let
A
be a geodesic in
(S,d)
with compatible order
\leq
. Let
[a,b]\subset A
be an interval in the total order
\leq
. Then
[a,b]
is a geodesic from
a
to
b
.

6
Examples of directed metrics

In this section, we give some important examples of directed metrics, elaborating on Section
1.3.1
. We start with general constructions, then move to constructions related to last passage percolation. Our most important and complex example is left until the end: the directed landscape.

Example 6.1
.

Let
S
be a set, and consider a function
h:S\to\mathbb{R}
. The
additive metric of
h
is the directed metric
d_{h}(x,y)=h(x)-h(y)
. Note that
d_{h}
is a directed metric of both positive and negative sign. We will frequently just write
h
in place of
d_{h}
to refer to the additive metric when there is no ambiguity.

Additive metrics are trivial in a certain sense. For example, any two points in
S
are
d_{h}
-equivalent, so the whole space
S
is a geodesic between any pair of points. Moreover, if
d
is another directed metric on
S
(of either sign), then
d+d_{h}
is a directed metric with the same geodesics as
d
, and
d
-equivalence is the same as
(d+d_{h})
-equivalence.

Our next set of examples comes from generalizing the notion of graph distance.

Example 6.2
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a directed graph. We can construct the following directed metrics of positive sign from data associated to
G
.

(i)

Define
d_{0}(x,y)=1
for all
(x,y)\in E
. The induced directed metric
e(d_{0})
can be thought of as a directed graph distance on
V
. If
G
is a directed version of an undirected graph (i.e
(x,y)\in E
whenever
(y,x)\in E
), then this gives the ordinary graph distance. This construction can also be applied to weighted finite graphs, where we set
d_{0}(x,y)
to be the weight of the edge
(x,y)
.

(ii)

Consider a function
h:V\to\mathbb{R}
. We can consider a directed metric on the edge set
E
induced by the function
d_{0}((x,y),(y,z))=h(y)
for all
(x,y),(y,z)\in E
. Note that this induced metric may not be defined for certain graphs. If
G
is a directed version of an undirected graph and
h>0
, then this defines an ordinary metric on the edges of the undirected graph.

(iii)

In the setting of (ii), we can also consider the metric on
V\cup E
induced by
d_{0}((x,y),y)=h(y)
and
d_{0}(x,(x,y))=0
for all
(x,y)\in E
.

All of the constructions in Example
6.2
have clear analogues for directed metrics with negative sign. In particular, last passage percolation on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
can be interpreted in the context of (iii) above.

Example 6.3
.

Consider a directed graph on the vertex set
V=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
where
(x,y)\in E
whenever
y-x\in\{(0,-1),(1,0)\}
. Then the negative-sign version of the construction in Example
6.2
(iii) gives a directed metric
d
on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cup E
corresponding to last passage percolation. We can explicitly write out distances as follows. Let
u,v\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
u_{1}\leq v_{1}
and
u_{2}\geq v_{2}
, and let

u^{-}=u-(1,0),u_{-}=u-(0,-1)
, and
v^{+}=v+(1,0),v_{+}=v+(0,-1)
. Then

\displaystyle d(u,x)+h(u)=d(y,x)=\max_{\pi}\sum_{w\in\pi}h(w),

\displaystyle\quad\text{ for }\quad x\in\{v,(v,v^{+}),(v,v_{+})\},\quad y\in\{(u^{-},u),(u_{-},u)\}.

Here the maximum is over all up-right lattice paths
\pi
from
u
to
v
. In the language of Section
2.3
, the right hand side above is equal to the last passage value
h[u\to v]
. We call
d
the
lattice last passage metric
defined by
h
.

We have defined the lattice last passage metric on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cup E
, rather than just
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, in order to make it easier to embed into the plane later on.
We can similarly relate last passage across cadlag paths to an induced metric on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
. We define this metric on
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
, where
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}+1/2)
will play the role of an edge set making this directed metric easier to embed into the plane.

Example 6.4
.

Consider
f\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, and let
d
be the directed metric of negative sign on
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
induced by the function

\begin{array}[]{ll}d_{0}((x,n),(y,n))&=f_{n}(y)-f_{n}(x)\qquad\mbox{for }\\
d_{0}((x,n),(x,n-1/2))&=0,\\
d_{0}((x,n+1/2),(x,n))&=f_{n}(x)-f_{n}(x^{-}),\end{array}

(6.1)

for
x<y\in\mathbb{R}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The directed metric
d
corresponds to last passage across functions as defined in Section
2
. Namely,

\displaystyle d((x,n+1/2),u)=f_{n}(x)-f_{n}(x^{-})+d((x,n),u)

\displaystyle=f[(x,n)\to(y,m)],

\displaystyle\qquad\text{ for }\qquad u

\displaystyle\in\{(y,m),(y,m-1/2)\}.

We call
d
the
line last passage metric
defined by
f
.

Important pullbacks:
When taking limits, we will have to embed last passage percolation on the lattice and across lines into a continuous setting. This can be done by looking at pullbacks of last passage metrics into the plane. One nice feature of this pullback approach is that we can easily pass between geodesics in the two models via Lemma
5.9
.

We first embed the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
into
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
.
Define
f_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}:\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cup E
by

f_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}(x,n)=\begin{cases}(x,n),\quad&x,n\in\mathbb{Z},\\
(({\left\lfloor x\right\rfloor},n),({\left\lceil x\right\rceil},n)),\quad&x\notin\mathbb{Z},n\in\mathbb{Z},\\
((x,{\left\lceil n\right\rceil}),(x,{\left\lfloor n\right\rfloor})),\quad&x\in\mathbb{Z},n\in\mathbb{Z}+1/2,\\
(({\left\lceil x\right\rceil},{\left\lceil n\right\rceil}),({\left\lceil x\right\rceil},{\left\lfloor n\right\rfloor})),\quad&x\notin\mathbb{Z},n\in\mathbb{Z}+1/2.\end{cases}

(6.2)

If
d
is a metric defined from a function
h
as in Example
6.3
, then the pullback onto
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
of
h
is simply the line last passage metric defined by the cadlag functions
h_{n}
given by the translation (
2.9
):

h_{n}(0)=0,\quad h_{n}(x)-h_{n}(y)=\sum_{u\in(y,x]\times\{n\}}h(u).

Next, we embed
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
into the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Define
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
by

f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}}(x,y)=\begin{cases}(x,-y),\quad&y\in\mathbb{Z},\\
(x,{\left\lfloor-y\right\rfloor}+1/2),\quad&y\notin\mathbb{Z}.\end{cases}

(6.3)

Note the sign change in (
6.3
). Our convention for last passage across lines means that finite distances can only be assigned for pairs of points
(u,v)
where
u
has a higher line index than
v
. However, in the directed landscape finite distances can only be assigned to a pair of points
(x,s;y,t)
when
s<t
. Since line index corresponds to time in the directed landscape, we need to reverse the line ordering to prove convergence.
For future use in Section
12
, we write out the composition

f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}:=f_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\circ f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}}

(6.4)

explicitly.

f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}(x,y)=\begin{cases}(x,-y),\quad&x,y\in\mathbb{Z},\\
(({\left\lfloor x\right\rfloor},-y),({\left\lceil x\right\rceil},-y)),\quad&x\notin\mathbb{Z},y\in\mathbb{Z},\\
((x,{\left\lceil-y\right\rceil}),(x,{\left\lfloor-y\right\rfloor})),\quad&x\in\mathbb{Z},y\notin\mathbb{Z},\\
(({\left\lceil x\right\rceil},{\left\lceil-y\right\rceil}),({\left\lceil x\right\rceil},{\left\lfloor-y\right\rfloor})),\quad&x\notin\mathbb{Z},y\notin\mathbb{Z}.\end{cases}

(6.5)

Remark 6.5
.

It may be helpful for the reader to keep the following construction in mind when thinking about lattice last passage metrics.
If
d
is a lattice last passage metric generated from a
nonnegative
weight function
h:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, and
d^{\prime}
is the pullback of
d
under the map
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
, then we can alternately describe
d^{\prime}
similarly to Example
1.4
, as the negative sign metric on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
induced by the function

d_{0}(a,c)=h(c)\mathbf{1}(c\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}),

where
d_{0}
is defined for all
a,c\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
a\neq c,a_{1}\leq c_{1},a_{2}\leq c_{2}
.

The directed landscape

Our most important example of a (random) directed metric is the directed landscape. To define the directed landscape, let
\mathcal{S}
be an Airy sheet as in Definition
3.3
. The rescaled function

\mathcal{S}_{s}(x,y)=s\mathcal{S}(x/s^{2},y/s^{2})

(6.6)

is an
Airy sheet of scale
s
. We refer to the process
\mathcal{S}(0,\cdot)
as a
parabolic Airy process
of scale
s
. The directed landscape is built out of independent Airy sheets of different scales.

Definition 6.6
.

The directed landscape is a random function
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
satisfying the following properties:

I.

(Domain) Let
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}=\{(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:s<t\}
and
\Delta=\{(u,u)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\}
. The function
\mathcal{L}
is continuous and finite on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, equal to zero on the diagonal
\Delta=\{(u,u)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\}
, and equal to
-\infty
on
\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus(\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}\cup\Delta)
.

II.

(Independent Airy sheet increments) For any disjoint time intervals
\{(t_{i},s_{i}):i\in\{1,\dots k\}\}
, the random functions

\mathcal{L}(\cdot,t_{i};\cdot,s_{i}),\quad i\in\{1,\dots,k\}

are independent Airy sheets of scale
(t_{i}-s_{i})^{3}
.

III.

(Metric composition law) Almost surely, for any
r<s<t
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\mathcal{L}(x,r;y,t)=\max_{z\in\mathbb{R}}[\mathcal{L}(x,r;z,s)+\mathcal{L}(z,s;y,t)].

(6.7)

Formally, we need to define a space of functions for
\mathcal{L}
to take values in. We will address this issue in detail in Section
7
. For now,
\mathcal{L}
can be viewed as a random element in the space of continuous functions from
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
to
\mathbb{R}
with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets, since
\mathcal{L}
is deterministic off of
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
. The directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
is unique in law by Theorem 10.9 in
Dauvergne et al. 2018
, and properties I and III imply that it is almost surely a directed metric of negative sign on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

We record two properties of
\mathcal{L}
that will be necessary for establishing convergence.

Proposition 6.7
.

Almost surely,
\mathcal{L}
satisfies the following.

1.

\mathcal{L}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}:=\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus\Delta
.

2.

For any
(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, there exists a (random) compact set
[A,B]\subset\mathbb{R}
such that all geodesics from
(x,s)
to
(y,t)
are contained in
[A,B]\times[s,t]
.

Proof.

Both of these results follow straightforwardly from the estimate on
\mathcal{L}
in Corollary 10.7 in
Dauvergne et al. 2018
. This corollary states that there exists a random constant
C>0
such that almost surely, for all
u=(x,s;y,t+s)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
we have

\left|\mathcal{L}(u)+\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{s}\right|\leq Cs^{1/3}\log^{4/3}\left(\frac{2(||u||+2)^{3/2}}{s}\right)\log^{2/3}(||u||+2).\qed

(6.8)

We will also use well-known tail bounds on the one-point distributions of
\mathcal{L}
.

Theorem 6.8

(
Tracy and Widom 1994
)
.

Let
u=(x,t;y,t+s^{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
. Then the random variable

Y=\mathcal{L}(u)/s+(x-y)^{2}/s^{3}

is a standard GUE Tracy-Widom random variable. In particular, it satisfies the tail bound

\mathbb{P}\left(|Y|\geq m\right)\leq ce^{-dm^{3/2}}

for constants
c,d>0
.

7
Topologies for planar directed metrics

The goal of this section is to define topologies which are suitable for proving convergence of lattice and line last passage metrics to the directed landscape.

In the previous section we saw that last passage metrics can be embedded in the plane in a natural way. This embedding yields discontinuous metrics. On the other hand, the directed landscape itself is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}=\{(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:u\neq v\}
. One approach to proving convergence would be to convert all directed metrics into continuous ones, and then appeal to a standard topology on a space of continuous functions.

We do not pursue this approach, since the necessary modifications of discontinuous last passage metrics do not necessarily preserve the triangle inequality or the geodesic structure. Instead we will study directed metrics up to different notions of equivalence.

For a given function
f:S\to R
between two metric spaces define the
closed graph
of
f
as

\mathfrak{g}f=\text{closure of }\{(x,f(x)):x\in S\}\subset S\times R.

(7.1)

If
R
has total order, then the
closed hypograph

\mathfrak{h}f
of
f
is the closure of the set
\{(x,y):x\leq f(y)\}
in
S\times R
.

We will study functions from
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
to
\bar{\mathbb{R}}:=\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
up to equivalence of closed graphs or hypographs. The reason for working on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
, rather than all of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, is twofold. First, planar directed metrics are equal to
0
on the diagonal
\Delta=\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
, so removing
\Delta
does not change what information we have about the metrics. Second, the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
is discontinuous on
\Delta
and so notions of convergence become unnecessarily complicated if we start considering what happens there. Define sets

\mathcal{E}_{g}=\{\mathfrak{g}f:f:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}\},\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad\mathcal{E}_{h}=\{\mathfrak{h}f:f:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}\}.

To put topologies on
\mathcal{E}_{h}
and
\mathcal{E}_{g}
, we embed them in a larger space
\mathcal{E}_{*}
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{*}
be the set of all closed subsets
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\times\bar{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\Gamma\cap(\{x\}\times\bar{\mathbb{R}})
is nonempty for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
. For
(r,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\times\bar{\mathbb{R}}
with
r=(u,v)
, define

E(r,s)=\left(r,\frac{|u-v|e^{-|r|}s}{1+|s|}\right).

(7.2)

Here we use the convention that
E(r,\pm\infty)=(r,\pm|u-v|e^{-|r|})
. The image of
E
is contained in the set
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\times[-1,1]
.
For two sets
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathcal{E}_{*}
, let
d(e_{1},e_{2})
be the Hausdorff distance between
E(e_{1})
and
E(e_{2})
. That is,

\begin{split}d(e_{1},e_{2})&=\inf\{r>0:E(e_{1})\subset E(e_{2})_{+r},\;E(e_{2})\subset E(e_{1})_{+r}\}\qquad\text{where}\\
\quad A_{+r}&=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\times[-1,1]:\inf_{a\in A}|a-x|<r\}.\end{split}

(7.3)

The function
E
maps closed sets to closed sets since it has a continuous inverse.

Lemma 7.1
.

The metric space
(\mathcal{E}_{*},d)
is a compact. In particular, it is a Polish space.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma_{n}\in\mathcal{E}_{*}
be a sequence. Since the Hausdorff topology on compact subsets of any compact space is compact, for any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
, the sequence
E\Gamma_{n}\cap(K\times[-1,1])
is precompact. Therefore letting

O_{i}=\{r=(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}:|u-v|>1/i,|r|<i\}\times[-1,1],

by a diagonalization argument we can find a subsequence
N
such that
\{E\Gamma_{n}\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muO_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu:n\in N\}
converges to a limit
\Lambda^{i}
for all
i
. Define

\Lambda=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\Lambda^{i}\cap O_{i}.

Note that
\Lambda=\Lambda^{i}
on
O_{i}
.
To complete the proof, we show that
E^{-1}\Lambda
is the limit of

\{\Gamma_{n}:n\in N\}
. Recalling the fattening notation
\Lambda_{+r}
from (
7.3
), for any fixed
i
we have

E\Gamma_{n}\subset\Lambda_{+r}\qquad\text{ if and only if }\qquad E\Gamma_{n}\cap O_{i}\subset\Lambda_{+r}\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad E\Gamma_{n}\cap O_{i}^{c}\subset\Lambda_{+r}.

(7.4)

The second containment on the right hand side of (
7.4
) holds for all
n
whenever
r\geq 2/i
since
\Lambda_{+r}
intersects every slice
\{x\}\times\bar{\mathbb{R}}
and

EO_{i}^{c}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\times(-1/i,1/i).

We now analyze the first containment on the right hand side in (
7.4
). Hausdorff convergence of
E\Gamma_{n}\cap\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muO_{i}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
to
\Lambda^{i}
implies that for any
r>0
, for all large enough
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

E\Gamma_{n}\cap O_{i}\subset\Lambda^{i}_{+r}\subset\Lambda_{+r}.

Therefore for all large enough
n\in N
, we have

E\Gamma_{n}\subset\Lambda_{+r}\quad\text{for all}\quad r\geq 2/i.

(7.5)

By a similar argument we can also show that for all large enough
n\in N
, we have
\Lambda\subset(E\Gamma_{n})_{+r}
for all
r\geq 2/i
. Since
i
was arbitrary, combining these two containment statements, and using the definition (
7.3
), implies that
\Gamma_{n}\to E^{-1}\Lambda
along
N
.
∎

Convergence statements about graphs and hypographs can be converted into more natural convergence statements about the underlying functions when the limit is continuous.

For the first lemma, we will use a notion of uniform convergence for functions whose range is all of
\bar{\mathbb{R}}
, rather than just
\mathbb{R}
. For such functions, we say that
f_{n}\to f
uniformly if
\frac{f_{n}}{1+|f_{n}|}\to\frac{f}{1+|f|}
uniformly as functions with range in
[-1,1]
. When the limit
f
is continuous, this is equivalent to the statement that
f_{n}(x_{n})\to f(x)
for any
x_{n}\to x
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Consider a sequence of functions
f_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
and a continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
. Then
\mathfrak{g}f_{n}\to\mathfrak{g}f
in
\mathcal{E}^{*}
if and only if

f_{n}\to f

compactly as functions from
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
.

We leave the straightforward proof of Lemma
7.2
as an exercise for the reader.

Lemma 7.3
.

Consider a sequence
f_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
and a continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
.

1.

Suppose
\mathfrak{h}f_{n}\to\mathfrak{h}f
in
\mathcal{E}_{*}
. Then for any sequence
x_{n}
converging to a point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
, we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(x_{n})\leq f(x).

(7.6)

In particular, if
f
has range contained in
\mathbb{R}
on some compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
, then

(f_{n}-f)^{+}\to 0\text{ uniformly on }K.

2.

Suppose that there exists a dense set
D
such that
f_{n}(y)\to f(y)
for all
y\in D
, and that for every convergent sequence
x_{n}\to x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
, (
7.6
) holds.
Then
\mathfrak{h}f_{n}\to\mathfrak{h}f
in
\mathcal{E}_{*}
.

Proof.

We first prove 1.
Let
x_{n}\to x
in
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
. The points
(x_{n},f_{n}(x_{n}))\in\mathfrak{h}f_{n}
for all
n
, so the point
y=(x,\limsup f_{n}(x_{n}))\in\mathfrak{h}f
. Moreover, the continuity of
f
implies that

\{x\}\times\bar{\mathbb{R}}\cap\mathfrak{h}f=\{(x,z)\in\{x\}\times\bar{\mathbb{R}}:z\leq f(x)\},

so
\limsup_{n}f_{n}(x_{n})\leq f(x)
.

For 2, since
\mathcal{E}_{*}
is compact, we can pick out a subsequential limit
\Gamma
in
\mathcal{E}_{*}
. For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
, define

g(x)=\sup\{z\in\bar{\mathbb{R}}:(x,z)\in\Gamma\}.

The function
g
is upper semicontinuous since
\Gamma
is closed, and
\mathfrak{h}g=\Gamma
. We want to show that
g=f
. First, we can find a sequence
x_{n}
converging to
x
such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}f_{n}(x_{n})=g(x).

Hence
f\geq g
by our assumption that (
7.6
) holds for all convergent sequences. On the other hand, we have that
g(x)\geq\limsup f_{n}(x)
for all
x\in D
, so
g|_{D}\geq f|_{D}
. Upper semicontinuity of
g
and continuity of
f
and
g
then implies that
g\geq f
.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

In later sections, we will also consider graph convergence of functions defined on slices

S_{s,t}=\{(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:x,y\in\mathbb{R}\}

for fixed
s<t
, or on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
or
\mathbb{R}
.
We similarly embed the closed graphs/hypographs of
\bar{\mathbb{R}}
-valued functions
f
with domain
S_{s,t},\mathbb{R}^{2},
or
\mathbb{R}
into spaces
\mathcal{E}_{*}(S_{s,t}),\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\mathcal{E}_{*}(\mathbb{R})
, where
\mathcal{E}_{*}(C)
consists of all closed subsets of
C\times\bar{\mathbb{R}}
that intersect
\{x\}\times\bar{\mathbb{R}}
for all
x\in C
. We say that
\Gamma_{n}\to\Gamma
in one of these three spaces
\mathcal{E}_{*}(C)
if
E^{\prime}\Gamma_{n}\to E^{\prime}\Gamma
in the Hausdorff topology, where
E^{\prime}:C\times\bar{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R}\times[-1,1]
is defined similarly to
E
:

E^{\prime}(x,s)=(x,e^{-|x|}s/(1+|s|)
.
It is easy to check that Lemmas
7.1
,
7.2
, and
7.3
still hold in this context.

8
Convergence of geodesics

Convergence of hypographs, and hence also convergence of graphs, is a strong enough notion to yield convergence of geodesics for planar directed metrics with a small amount of natural additional structure.

We start with a proposition showing convergence of geodesic sets. While this proposition is stated for planar metrics, the proof does not require anything about the underlying set being
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We work with directed metrics of negative sign with an eye to proving convergence of geodesics in last passage percolation; an analogous statement holds for directed metrics of positive sign.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
d_{n}
be a sequence of directed metrics of negative sign on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
whose hypographs converge in
\mathcal{E}_{*}
to a limiting directed metric
d
which is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
. Let
(x_{n},y_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
be a sequence converging to
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
such that

d_{n}(x_{n},y_{n})\to d(x,y)>-\infty.

Let
A_{n}
be a sequence of bounded
d_{n}
-geodesic sets from
x_{n}
to
y_{n}
. Then all subsequential limits of
\bar{A}_{n}
in the Hausdorff topology on closed subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
are
d
-geodesic sets from
x
to
y
.

In Proposition
8.1
, we work with closures of geodesic sets rather than geodesic sets themselves so that convergence in the Hausdorff topology is well-defined. In the sequel, we will frequently make this exchange between sets and their closures.

Proof.

Let
A
be any subsequential limit of the sequence of closures
\bar{A}_{n}
. We will show that for every pair of points
a,b\in A
, that either

d(x,y)=d(x,a)+d(a,b)+d(b,y),

(8.1)

or else (
8.1
) holds with the roles of
a
and
b
reversed.

For this, fix
a,b\in A\setminus\{x,y\}
. First assume
a\neq b
. There exists
a_{n},b_{n}\in A_{n}
such that
a_{n}\to a,b_{n}\to b
. For each
n
, either
a_{n}\leq_{n}b_{n}
or
b_{n}\leq_{n}a_{n}
, where
\leq_{n}
is a compatible order on
A_{n}
. By possibly passing to a subsequence and relabelling the points, we may assume
a_{n}\leq b_{n}
for all
n
. Then Lemma
7.3
implies

\limsup_{n\to\infty}d_{n}(x_{n},a_{n})+d_{n}(a_{n},b_{n})+d_{n}(b_{n},y_{n})\leq d(x,a)+d(a,b)+d(b,y)\leq d(x,y).

(8.2)

The second inequality in (
8.2
) follows from the triangle inequality for
d
.
The same inequality chain holds when
a=b
when we take
a_{n}=b_{n}
since then
d(a_{n},b_{n})=d(a,b)=0
. On the other hand, since
A_{n}
was a geodesic set, the left hand side of (
8.2
) equals

\limsup_{n\to\infty}d_{n}(x_{n},y_{n}).

This equals
d(x,y)
by the assumption of the lemma, so the inequalities in (
8.2
) must all be equalities. This proves (
8.1
) when
a,b\notin\{x,y\}
. If
\{a,b\}=\{x,y\}
, then (
8.1
) is trivially true. If only one of either
a
or
b
is in the set
\{x,y\}
, say,
a
, then (
8.1
) for the pair
a,b
follows from (
8.1
) applied to the pair
b,b
.

Now let
\preceq
be the relation on
A
where we say that
a\preceq b
if (
8.1
) holds. We want to modify
\preceq
to create a compatible total order on
A
. For this, let
\leq
be an arbitrary total order on
A
with least element
x
and greatest element
y
. Define a new relation
\preceq^{\prime}
given by

a\preceq^{\prime}b\text{ if }\begin{cases}&a\preceq b\;\text{and}\;a\not\preceq b,\text{ or}\\
&a\preceq b\;\text{and}\;b\preceq a,\text{ and }a\leq b.\end{cases}

(8.3)

We claim that the new relation
\preceq^{\prime}
is a compatible total order on
A
. Since for any
a,b
, (
8.1
) either holds for
a,b
or for
b,a
, we have
a\preceq^{\prime}b
or
b\preceq^{\prime}a
for any
a,b
. Moreover, the construction in (
8.3
) ensures that if
a\preceq^{\prime}b
and
b\preceq^{\prime}a
then
a=b
. Next, we check transitivity. Suppose
a\preceq^{\prime}b\preceq^{\prime}c
. By (
8.1
) for the pair
a,b
and two triangle inequalities, we have that

d(x,y)=d(x,a)+d(a,b)+d(b,y)\leq d(x,b)+d(b,y)\leq d(x,y).

(8.4)

All the inequalities above must be equalities, implying that
d(x,a)+d(a,b)=d(x,b)
. Combining this with (
8.1
) for the pair
b,c
gives that

d(x,y)=d(x,a)+d(a,b)+d(b,c)+d(c,y).

Again, by appealing to the triangle inequality as in (
8.4
), this implies that (
8.1
) holds for the pair
a,c
, so
\preceq^{\prime}
is transitive, and hence a total order. This reasoning also implies that
d(a,c)=d(a,b)+d(b,c)
, so the total order satisfies the second condition of Definition
5.4
. Finally, for any
a
, (
8.1
) holds for the pairs
x,a
and
a,y
, so the construction (
8.3
) implies that
x\preceq^{\prime}a\preceq^{\prime}y
. Hence
\preceq^{\prime}
is a compatible order on
A
and so
A
is a geodesic set.
∎

If we impose some structure on the limiting metric
d
then we can improve Proposition
8.1
to give convergence of geodesics.
The following structure is natural in light of the fact that we are interested in proving convergence to the directed landscape.

Definition 8.2
.

Let
d
be a directed metric of negative sign on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We say that
d
is a
spacetime metric
if
d
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
and satisfies

1.

(Domain)
d>-\infty
on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
,
d=0
on
\Delta
, and
d=-\infty
everywhere else.

2.

(Metric composition) For all
s<r<t
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

d(x,s;y,t)=\max_{z\in\mathbb{R}}d(x,s;z,r)+d(z,r;y,t).

3.

For any
(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, there exists a compact set
K=[a,b]\times[s,t]\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that all geodesics from
(x,s)
to
(y,t)
are contained in
K
.

By Proposition
6.7
and Definition
6.6
, the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
is almost surely a spacetime metric. We start by proving two basic properties of spacetime metrics.

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
d
be a spacetime metric on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

(i)

For every
(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, all geodesics from
(x,s)
to
(y,t)
are of the form

\Gamma(\pi)=\{(\pi(r),r):r\in[s,t]\}

for some continuous function
\pi:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\pi(s)=x,\pi(t)=y
. Moreover, the only compatible order on each geodesic is the order induced by the time coordinate
r\in[s,t]
.

(ii)

For any
(u;v)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, a geodesic set from
u
to
v
is a geodesic if and only if it is a connected subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Proof.

We first prove (i). Let
A
be a geodesic from
u=(x,s)
to
v=(y,t)
. Since
d=-\infty
off of
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}\cup\Delta
,
A
must be the graph of a function
\pi
from some subset
D\subset[s,t]
to
\mathbb{R}
and the only compatible order on
A
is the order induced by the time coordinate. Next,
D
is closed by continuity of
d
on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
and maximality of
A
. Finally, suppose
(a,r_{1}),(b,r_{2})\in A
and
(r_{1},r_{2})\cap D=\emptyset
. Then by metric composition, there is some
r_{3}\in(r_{1},r_{2})
and some
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that

d(a,r_{1};b,r_{2})=d(a,r_{1};c,r_{3})+d(c,r_{3};b,r_{2}).

We can add the point
(c,r_{3})
to the geodesic
A
, contradicting maximality. Therefore
D=[s,t]
.

Next, we show that
\pi
is right continuous. For this, we let
\bar{\pi}(r)=(\pi(r),r)
. Let
r_{n}\in[s,t)
be any nonincreasing sequence converging to
r\in[s,t)
. By Definition
8.2
.3,
\bar{\pi}(r_{n})
is necessarily a bounded sequence in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
so it has a subsequential limit
y
. Suppose that
y\neq\bar{\pi}(r)
. By continuity of
d
on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
, we have

d(u,v)=d(u,\bar{\pi}(r))+d(\bar{\pi}(r),\bar{\pi}(r_{n}))+d(\bar{\pi}(r_{n}),v)\to d(u,\bar{\pi}(r))+d(\bar{\pi}(r),y)+d(y,v).

Since
d
is finite on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
and equal to
-\infty
on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, we have
d(\bar{\pi}(r),y)=-\infty
, whereas all other terms above are finite. This is a contradiction, so
y=\bar{\pi}(r)
and therefore
\pi(r_{n})\to\pi(r)
. We can similarly show that
\pi
is left continuous.

Part (ii) follows from part (i), and the fact that any geodesic set is contained in a geodesic (Proposition
5.5
).
∎

Remark 8.4
.

In
Dauvergne et al. 2018
, geodesics in
\mathcal{L}
are defined differently. Namely, instead of a geodesic being a subset
\Gamma(\pi)=\{(\pi(r),r):r\in[s,t]\}
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, a geodesic is simply the function
\pi
itself. By Proposition
8.3
(i) and the fact that
\mathcal{L}
is almost surely a spacetime landscape, the two notions are equivalent, so all geodesic results from
Dauvergne et al. 2018
apply here. For example, for any
(u;v)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, there is almost surely a unique
\mathcal{L}
-geodesic
\Gamma
from
u
to
v
by
Dauvergne et al. 2018
, Theorem 1.7.

We can use Proposition
8.3
to give our first geodesic convergence theorem.

Theorem 8.5
.

Let
d_{n}
be a sequence of directed metrics of negative sign whose hypographs converge in
\mathcal{E}_{*}
to the hypograph of a spacetime metric
d
. Let
(u_{n};v_{n})\to(u;v)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
. Suppose that
\Gamma_{n}
is a sequence of
d_{n}
-geodesics from
u_{n}
to
v_{n}
which are connected subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and that

d_{n}(u_{n},v_{n})\to d_{n}(u,v).

Then the sequence of closures
\bar{\Gamma}_{n}
is precompact in the Hausdorff topology and all subsequential limits of
\bar{\Gamma}_{n}
are
d
-geodesics from
u
to
v
.

Proof.

Let
K
be a compact set in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
containing all
u_{n}
and
v_{n}
, and all
d
-geodesic sets from
u
to
v
in its interior. Such a
K
exists by Definition
8.2
.3. The sequence
\bar{\Gamma}_{n}\cap K
is a precompact sequence of sets in the Hausdorff topology. All subsequential limits of
\bar{\Gamma}_{n}\cap K
are geodesic sets from
u
to
v
by Proposition
8.1
. In particular, any such limit is contained in
\Int(K)
, so
\bar{\Gamma}_{n}\cap\partial K=\emptyset
for all large enough
n
. By the connectedness of
\bar{\Gamma}_{n}
, for such
n
this implies that either

\bar{\Gamma}_{n}\cap\Int(K)\qquad\text{ or }\qquad\bar{\Gamma}_{n}\cap K^{c}

is empty. Since
(u_{n},v_{n})\in\bar{\Gamma}_{n}\cap\Int(K)
for large enough
n
, this implies that

\bar{\Gamma}_{n}\cap K^{c}=\emptyset

for all large enough
n
. Therefore
\bar{\Gamma}_{n}
is a sequence of bounded
d_{n}
-geodesic sets, so all subsequential limits are
d
-geodesic sets by Proposition
8.1
. Connectedness of the sets
\bar{\Gamma}_{n}
implies that all subsequential limits are connected, and hence are geodesics by Proposition
8.3
(ii).
∎

We have the following straightforward corollary of Theorem
8.5
in the
d_{n}=d
case.

Corollary 8.6
.

Let
d
be a spacetime metric on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
If
(u_{n};v_{n})\to(u;v)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
and
\Gamma_{n}
is a sequence of geodesics from
u_{n}
to
v_{n}
, then
\Gamma_{n}
is precompact in the Hausdorff topology, and all subsequential limits of
\Gamma_{n}
are geodesics from
u
to
v
.

Proof.

Continuity of
d
on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
implies that
d(u_{n},v_{n})\to d(u,v)
, and Proposition
8.3
implies that each
\Gamma_{n}
is closed and connected. Therefore we can apply Theorem
8.5
to complete the proof.
∎

In some cases we will not be able to check the condition that
d_{n}(u_{n},v_{n})\to d_{n}(u,v)
. We can loosen this condition by introducing a bit more structure on the prelimiting directed metrics
d_{n}
. This structure will always be satisfied by our rescaled planar embeddings of last passage metrics.

Theorem 8.7
.

Let
d_{n}
be a sequences of directed metrics of negative sign in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying the following conditions.

1.

Let
\leq
be the total order on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
given by
(x,s)\leq(y,t)
if
s<t
or if
s=t
and
x<y
. Then
d_{n}(u;v)=-\infty
for
v<u
. This means that the only compatible order on any
d_{n}
-geodesic is the order
\leq
.

2.

Any
d_{n}
-geodesic is the image of a continuous curve
\pi:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying
\pi(s)\leq\pi(t)
for
s\leq t
.

3.

The sequence
\mathfrak{h}d_{n}
converges to the closed hypograph of a spacetime metric
d
in
\mathcal{E}_{*}
.

Suppose that
(u_{n},v_{n})\to(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
and that
d_{n}(u_{n},v_{n})>-\infty
for all large enough
n
. Then any sequence of geodesic closures
\bar{\Gamma}_{n}
is precompact in the Hausdorff topology. Moreover, if there is a unique
d
-geodesic
\Gamma
from
u
to
v
then
\bar{\Gamma}_{n}\to\Gamma
.

To prove Theorem
8.7
, we first show the following lemma about geodesic ordering.

Lemma 8.8
.

Let all notation and assumptions be as in Theorem
8.7
.
Let
(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})=(x^{\prime},s^{\prime};y^{\prime},t^{\prime}),(u,v)=(x,s;y,t)
and suppose that
s^{\prime}\leq s<t\leq t^{\prime}
. Suppose also that there is a geodesic
\Lambda
from
u^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime}
and points
x^{-}<x,y^{-}<y
such that
(x^{\prime},s),(y^{\prime},t)\in\Lambda
.

Then for any
d_{n}
-geodesic
\Gamma
from
u
to
v
and any
(w,r)\in\Gamma
, there exists a geodesic
\Gamma^{-}
from
u^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime}
with
(w^{-},r)\in\Gamma^{-}
for some
w^{-}\leq w
.

Proof.

Since
\Lambda
is connected by assumption
2
of Theorem
8.7
, there must exist a point
(\lambda,r)\in\Lambda
. If
\lambda\leq w
, then we’re done. Suppose
\lambda>w
. Since
x^{-}<x
and
y^{-}<y
, Condition
2
in Theorem
8.7
implies that there must exist points
p,q\in\Gamma\cap\Lambda
with
p\leq(w,r)\leq q
in the order in Theorem
8.7
.1.

Letting
[a,b]
denote an interval in the order
\leq
, let
\Lambda^{\prime}
denote the union of the three sets
\Lambda\cap[u^{\prime},p],\Gamma\cap[p,q]
and
\Lambda\cap[q,v^{\prime}]
. We claim that
\Lambda^{\prime}
is a geodesic set from
u^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime}
. Let
a\leq b\leq c\in\Lambda^{\prime}
.
Let
e_{0}\leq\dots\leq e_{6}
be the order statistics of the set
\{u^{\prime},a,b,c,p,q,v^{\prime}\}
. Then we have

d_{n}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})=d_{n}(u^{\prime},p)+d_{n}(p,q)+d_{n}(q,v^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{6}d_{n}(e_{i-1},e_{i}).

(8.5)

The first equality follows by the geodesic property of
\Lambda
, and the second equality follows by applying the geodesic property of
\Lambda
and
\Gamma
to each of the intervals
[u^{\prime},p],[p,q],
and
[q,v^{\prime}]
. Equation (
8.5
) and the triangle inequality for
d_{n}
implies that
d_{n}(a,c)=d_{n}(a,b)+d_{n}(b,c)
. Hence
\Lambda^{\prime}
is a geodesic set. Moreover,
(w,r)\in\Lambda^{\prime}
since
(w,r)\in\Gamma\cap[p,q]
. Since every geodesic set is contained in a geodesic (Proposition
5.5
), there exists some geodesic
\Gamma^{-}
from
u^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime}
with
(w,r)\in\Gamma^{-}
, as desired.
∎

Proof of Theorem
8.7
.

Let
(u_{n},v_{n})=(x_{n},s_{n};y_{n},t_{n}),(u,v)=(x,s;y,t)
be as in the statement of the theorem. We will construct a sequence
(u_{n}^{-},v_{n}^{-})=(x^{-}_{n},s^{-}_{n};y^{-}_{n},t^{-}_{n})
such that

1.

(u_{n}^{-},v_{n}^{-})\to(u,v)
and
d_{n}(u_{n}^{-},v_{n}^{-})\to d(u,v)
.

2.

For all large enough
n
,
[s_{n},t_{n}]\subset[s^{-}_{n},t^{-}_{n}]
.

3.

For all large enough
n
, any
d_{n}
-geodesic
\Gamma
from
u_{n}^{-}
to
v_{n}^{-}
satisfies

\Gamma\cap\mathbb{R}\times\{s_{n}\}\subset(-\infty,x_{n})\times\{s_{n}\},\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad\Gamma\cap\mathbb{R}\times\{t_{n}\}\subset(-\infty,y_{n})\times\{s_{n}\}.

Let
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\Gamma^{m}_{i}
be any sequence of
d
-geodesics from
(x-1/i-1/m,s-1/m)
to
(y-1/i-1/m,t+1/m)
.
By Corollary
8.6
and Proposition
8.3
, the sequence
\Gamma^{m}_{i},m\in\mathbb{N}
is precompact and subsequential limits are graphs of continuous paths from
(x-1/i,s)
to
(y-1/i,t)
. In particular, there exists
m_{i}\geq i
such that any
d
-geodesic
\Lambda
from
u^{i}:=(x-1/i-1/m_{i},s-1/m_{i})
to
v^{i}:=(y-1/i-1/m_{i},t+1/m_{i})
satisfies

\begin{split}&\Lambda\cap\mathbb{R}\times[s-1/(2i),s]\subset(-\infty,x-1/(2i)]\times[s-1/(2i),s]\qquad\;\text{and}\;\\
&\Lambda\cap\mathbb{R}\times[t,t+1/(2i)]\subset(-\infty,y-1/(2i)]\times[t,t+1/(2i)].\end{split}

(8.6)

Next, by hypograph convergence of
d_{n}\to d
, we can find a sequence of points
(u_{n}^{i},v_{n}^{i})\to(u^{i},v^{i})
such that
d_{n}(u_{n}^{i},v_{n}^{i})\to d(u^{i},v^{i})
as
n\to\infty
.
By Theorem
8.5
, there exists
n(i)\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq n(i)
, any
d_{n}
-geodesic
\Lambda
from
u_{n}^{i}
to
v_{n}^{i}
satisfies (
8.6
) with
x-1/(2i),y-1/(2i)
replaced by
x-1/(3i),y-1/(3i)
. We can also choose
n(i)
large enough so that for
n\geq n(i)
, we have

\displaystyle[s_{n},t_{n}]\subset[s^{i}_{n},t^{i}_{n}]\qquad\;\text{and}\;\qquad|d_{n}(u_{n}^{i},v_{n}^{i})-d(u^{i},v^{i})|\leq 1/i.

We can then set
(u^{-}_{n},v^{-}_{n})=(u^{j(n)}_{n},v^{j(n)}_{n})
for any increasing sequence
j(n)
with
n(j(n))\leq n
for all large enough
n
. To guarantee the condition (i) we have used that
d(u^{i},v^{i})\to d(u,v)
. This uses the fact that
m_{i}\geq i
and the continuity of
d
.

Now let
\Gamma_{n}
be any sequence of
d_{n}
-geodesics from
u_{n}
to
v_{n}
. We first check that
\bar{\Gamma}_{n}
is precompact in the Hausdorff topology. Consider any sequence
(w_{n},r_{n})\in\Gamma_{n}
with
r_{n}\in[s_{n},t_{n}]
converging to a point
r\in[s,t]
.

By conditions 2 and 3 satisfied by
u_{n}^{-},v_{n}^{-}
and Lemma
8.8
, for large enough
n
we can find geodesics
\Gamma_{n}^{-}
from
u_{n}^{-}
to
v_{n}^{-}
with
(w_{n}^{-},r_{n})\in\Gamma_{n}^{-}
for some
w_{n}^{-}\leq w_{n}
. By condition 1 and Theorem
8.5
, the sequence
\Gamma_{n}^{-}
converges to a geodesic
\Lambda
from
u
to
v
, so all subsequential limits of
(w_{n}^{-},r_{n})
must lie on a geodesic
\Lambda
from
u
to
v
. Therefore since
w_{n}^{-}\leq w_{n}
, all subsequential limits of
w_{n}
are bounded below by some
w\in\mathbb{R}
with
(w,r)
in a
d
-geodesic from
u
to
v
.

By a symmetric argument, all subsequential limits of
w_{n}
are bounded above by some
w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
with
(w^{\prime},r)
in a
d
-geodesic from
u
to
v
. Since all
d
-geodesics from
u
to
v
are contained in a common compact set by Definition
8.2
.3, the sequence
\bar{\Gamma}_{n}
is precompact. Now suppose there is a unique
d
-geodesic
\Gamma
from
u
to
v
. The set
\Gamma
is equal to
\{(\pi(r),r):r\in[s,t]\}
for some continuous function
\pi
by Proposition
8.3
, so the bounds above guarantee that all subsequential limits of
\bar{\Gamma}_{n}
are subsets of
\Gamma
. The connectivity of each
\bar{\Gamma}_{n}
then guarantees that
\bar{\Gamma}_{n}\to\Gamma
.
∎

The condition of uniqueness of
\Gamma
in Theorem
8.7
about the limiting geodesic being unique is almost surely satisfied for the directed landscape for any fixed
(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
by Remark
8.4
.

We finish this section with a proposition about convergence of directed metrics along geodesics. This will be used in Section
14
to prove convergence of the longest increasing subsequence in a uniform permutation.

Proposition 8.9
.

Let
d_{n}
be a sequence of directed metrics of negative sign whose graphs converge to a spacetime metric
d
in
\mathcal{E}_{*}
. Let
(u_{n};v_{n})\to(u;v)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, and consider a sequence of
d_{n}
-geodesics
\Gamma_{n}
from
u_{n}
to
v_{n}
converging to a
d
-geodesic
\Gamma
from
u
to
v
.
Then for any
x_{n}\in\Gamma_{n}
converging to a point
x\in\Gamma
we have
d_{n}(u_{n},x_{n})\to d(u,x).
Moreover,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\Gamma_{n}}|d_{n}(u_{n},x)|\leq\sup_{x\in\Gamma}|d(u_{n},x)|<\infty.

More general statements than Proposition
8.9
hold regarding convergence along geodesics, though we do not pursue these here.

Proof.

If
(u,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
, then
d_{n}(u_{n},x_{n})\to d(u,x)
by Lemma
7.2
and the continuity of
d
on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
. The only other possibility is
x=u
.
We can use the geodesic property of
\Gamma_{n}
to write

d_{n}(u_{n},x_{n})=d_{n}(u_{n},v_{n})-d_{n}(x_{n},v_{n}).

By graph convergence of
d_{n}\to d
, both terms on the right hand side converge to
d(u,v)
, again by Lemma
7.2
. Since
d
is finite on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, this implies that
d_{n}(u_{n},x_{n})\to 0=d(u,u)
.

We turn to the second part of the proposition. The first inequality is immediate from the first part of the proposition. If the second inequality fails, then we could find a convergent sequence
x_{n}\in\Gamma
such that
d(u,x_{n})
would not converge, contradicting the first part of the proposition in the special case when
d_{n}=d
for all
n
.
∎

9
Multi-time convergence to
\mathcal{L}

In the next three sections, we give criteria for random last passage metrics to converge to
\mathcal{L}
. While our main goal is to develop criteria for hypograph and graph convergence, we start with a weaker notion of multi-time convergence.

We will discretize time while keeping space continuous. Recall from Remark
7.4
the spaces
S_{s,t},\mathcal{E}_{*}(S_{s,t})
.
The results of Section
4
imply that certain rescaled last passage metrics
\mathcal{L}^{n}|_{S_{t,t+s^{3}}}
can be coupled to converge compactly a.s. to an Airy sheet of scale
s
. Since Airy sheets are continuous, this is equivalent to graph convergence in
\mathcal{E}_{*}(S_{s,t})
by Lemma
7.2
. Because of this, graph convergence in each of the spaces
\mathcal{E}_{*}(S_{s,t})
will be our main assumption in this section.

Next, for sequences of random functions
\mathcal{K}_{n},\mathcal{K}:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
, we say that
\mathcal{K}_{n}
converges to
\mathcal{K}
in the
multi-time sense
if for any set
S=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}S_{t_{i},t_{i}+s_{i}^{3}}
for
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R},s_{i}>0
, we have

\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{K}_{n}|_{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{K}|_{S}

(9.1)

in the product space
\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{E}_{*}(S_{t_{i},t_{i}+s_{i}^{3}})
.

We say that a function
W:\mathbb{R}^{4}\to\mathbb{R}
is
temporally independent
if the restrictions

W|_{(\mathbb{R}\times I_{1})^{2}},\dots,W|_{(\mathbb{R}\times I_{k})^{2}}

are independent for disjoint time intervals
I_{1},\dots,I_{k}
. The next theorem shows that Airy sheet convergence for temporally independent metrics implies multi-time convergence.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
W^{n}
be a sequence of temporally independent directed metrics on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of negative sign.
Suppose that for any
t\in\mathbb{R},s>0
, the random function
W^{n}|_{S_{t,t+s^{3}}}
converges to an Airy sheet of scale
s
in
\mathcal{E}_{*}(S_{t,t+s^{3}})
.
Then
W^{n}
converges to
\mathcal{L}
in the multi-time sense.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
S=\bigcup S_{s^{i},s^{j}}
where the union is over all
s^{i}<s^{j}
in some countable dense subset of
\mathbb{R}
. First,
\mathfrak{g}W^{n}|_{S}
is tight in the product space
\prod\mathcal{E}_{*}(S_{s^{i},s^{j}})
since it has convergent marginals. Let
\mathcal{K}
be any subsequential limit of
W^{n}
in this space.
We first show that for any
s=s^{i}<t=s^{j}
, we have

\mathcal{K}|_{S_{s,t}}=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{K}|_{S_{s_{n},t_{n}}},

(9.2)

for certain sequences
s_{n}\downarrow s,t_{n}\uparrow t
. First,
\mathcal{K}
inherits the triangle inequality from
W^{n}
, so

\mathcal{K}(x,s;y,t)\geq\mathcal{K}(x,s;x,s_{n})+\mathcal{K}(x,s_{n};y,t_{n})+\mathcal{K}(y,t_{n};y,t).

(9.3)

Now,
\mathcal{K}|_{S_{s^{i},s^{j}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}|_{S_{s^{i},s^{j}}}
for all
i,j
, so all four random variables in (
9.3
) are rescaled and shifted Tracy-Widom random variables by Theorem
6.8
.
In particular, as long as the sequences
s_{n},t_{n}
are converging to
0
quickly enough, the tail bounds on
\mathcal{K}(x,s;x,s_{n}),\mathcal{K}(y,t_{n};y,t)
in Theorem
6.8
imply that

\mathcal{K}(x,s;y,t)\geq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{K}(x,s_{n};y,t_{n})

almost surely.
However,
\mathcal{K}(x,s_{n};y,t_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(x,s_{n};y,t_{n})
, which converges in distribution with
n
to
\mathcal{K}(x,s;y,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)
, so this inequality is almost surely an equality.

Next,
\mathcal{K}|_{S}
inherits temporal independence from
W^{n}
. Equation (
9.2
) implies that this independence also holds when the intervals
I_{1},\dots,I_{k}
overlap at their endpoints. We use this to show that
\mathcal{K}|_{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}|_{S}
. By Kolmogorov’s extension theorem, it suffices to prove that
\mathcal{K}|_{S^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}|_{S^{\prime}}
for all subsets
S^{\prime}\subset S
of the form

S^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i<j\in\{1,\dots,m\}}S_{t_{i},t_{j}},

where
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots<t_{m}
. Letting

S^{*}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m-1}S_{t_{i},t_{i+1}},

we have
\mathcal{K}|_{S^{*}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}|_{S^{*}}
: both consist of
m-1
independent Airy sheets of scale
(t_{i+1}-t_{i})^{1/3}
. Next, the triangle inequality for
\mathcal{K}
gives that

\mathcal{K}(x,t_{i};y,t_{k})\geq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}[\mathcal{K}(x,t_{i};z,t_{j})+\mathcal{K}(z,t_{j};y,t_{k})]

(9.4)

for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
and
i<j<k\in\{1,\dots,m\}
. By the assumption of Airy sheet convergence for
\mathcal{K}
, and the independence of
\mathcal{K}(x,t_{i};z,t_{j})
and
\mathcal{K}(z,t_{j};y,t_{k})
, we have that

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}(x,t_{i};y,t_{k})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(x,t_{i};y,t_{k})\;\text{and}\;\\
\mathcal{K}(x,t_{i};\cdot,t_{j})+\mathcal{K}(\cdot,t_{j};y,t_{k})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(x,t_{i};\cdot,t_{j})+\mathcal{L}(\cdot,t_{j};y,t_{k}).\end{split}

(9.5)

By the metric composition law for
\mathcal{L}
, (
9.5
) implies that the right hand side of (
9.4
) is a maximum, rather than a supremum, and that the two sides of (
9.4
) are equal in distribution. Therefore by the inequality in (
9.4
), they must be equal almost surely. Continuity of both sides of (
9.4
) as functions of
x
and
y
therefore implies that almost surely,
\mathcal{K}
satisfies the metric composition law everywhere on
S^{\prime}
. This, along with
\mathcal{K}|_{S^{*}}
, uniquely determines
\mathcal{K}|_{S^{\prime}}
. The directed landscape
\mathcal{L}|_{S^{\prime}}
is determined from
\mathcal{L}|_{S^{*}}
in the same way.
∎

10
Hypograph convergence to
\mathcal{L}

The multi-time convergence in Theorem
9.1
does not immediately imply either hypograph or graph convergence. For example, we could modify any sequence of metrics
W^{n}
satisfying the conditions of Theorem
9.1
in the following way.
Let
Q
be a Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}
, and define the map
P_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

P_{n}(u)=\begin{cases}u\qquad u\notin\mathbb{R}\times Q,\\
0,\qquad u\in\mathbb{R}\times Q.\end{cases}

Let
\tilde{W}^{n}
be the pullback of
W^{n}
under
P_{n}
. Then
\tilde{W}^{n}
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
9.1
since almost surely
(\mathbb{R}\times Q)^{2}\cap S=\emptyset
for any fixed set
S=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}S_{s_{i},t_{i}}
, but
\mathfrak{h}\tilde{W}^{n}\not\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{L}
since, for example, for large enough
c
the random variable

\sup_{s<t\in[0,1],x,-y\in[c,2c]}W_{n}(x,s;y,t)

will have an atom at
0
whose size does not tend to
0
with
n
whereas the corresponding random variable for
\mathcal{L}
is nonatomic.
To work around this issue, we will need to be able to control any unusually large values of
W^{n}
on subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
. For example, in the setting above,
\tilde{W}^{n}(u,v)=0
whenever
u,v\in\mathbb{R}\times Q
, whereas
\mathcal{L}(u,v)
can be arbitrarily small anywhere. For a set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and a function
d:\mathbb{R}^{4}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
, we write

d(A)=\sup\{d(x):x\in A\}.

Typically
d
will be a directed metric of negative sign, though we will prove convergence results in greater generality.
We now give conditions for hypograph convergence of functions that arise naturally in the study of last passage percolation. For this proposition, recall that
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}=\mathbb{R}^{4}\setminus\Delta
, where
\Delta=\{(u;u)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\}
.

Proposition 10.1
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{K}_{n}
is a sequence of random functions from
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
. Suppose that the following conditions hold:

(i)

For some countable dense subset
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
we have
\mathcal{K}_{n}|_{D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{K}|_{D}
, where
\mathcal{K}:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
is a random continuous function.

(ii)

For all
n
,
\mathcal{K}_{n}(x,s;y,t)=-\infty
whenever
s>t
.

(iii)

For
p=(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},
define

B^{+}(p,r)=[x-r^{2},x+r^{2}]\times[t+r^{3},t+2r^{3}],\quad B^{-}(p,r)=[x-r^{2},x+r^{2}]\times[t-2r^{3},t-r^{3}].

For every compact
K\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
and every
\epsilon>0
we have

\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{\sup_{(p,q)\in K}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}^{n}(B^{+}(p,r)\times B^{-}(q,r))-\mathcal{K}^{n}(p,q)>\epsilon\right)}{r^{10}}=0.

(iv)

For
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
, let
A_{+r}=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:\inf_{a\in A}|a-u|<r\}
. For any
m\in\mathbb{R}
and any compact set
A\subset\{(x,s;y,s):x,y,s\in\mathbb{R},x\neq y\}
we have

\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(A_{+r})>m)=0.

Then
\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{K}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{K}
as random elements of
\mathcal{E}_{*}
. Moreover, we have the joint distributional convergence
(\mathcal{K}_{n}|_{D},\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{K}_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(\mathcal{K}|_{D},\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{K})
.

We have built the KPZ scaling into the third condition above in order to make the conditions of Proposition
10.1
easier to check in the cases we care about. Condition (iii) handles unusually large values of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
in the interior of
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, and condition (iv) ensures that
\mathcal{K}_{n}
is close to
-\infty
near the boundary of
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{J},\Gamma)
be any joint weak subsequential limit of
(\mathcal{K}_{n}|_{D},\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{K}_{n})
. By (i),
\mathcal{J}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{K}|_{D}
. By Skorokhod’s representation theorem, we can couple the functions
\mathcal{K}_{n}
so that
(\mathcal{K}_{n}|_{D},\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{K}_{n})\to(\mathcal{K}|_{D},\Gamma)
almost surely along the given subsequence.

The set
\Gamma
is the closed hypograph of a unique upper semicontinuous function
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
. We will show that
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=\mathcal{K}
. By Lemma
7.3
.1,
\mathcal{K}|_{D}\leq\mathcal{K}^{\prime}|_{D}
. Therefore upper semicontinuity of
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
and continuity of
\mathcal{K}
implies that
\mathcal{K}\leq\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
. To establish that this is in fact an equality, we first show that for any compact set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
,

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(A)=\mathcal{K}(A\cap D)=\mathcal{K}(A)

(10.1)

almost surely. In (
10.1
), the second equality follows from the continuity of
\mathcal{K}
. Next, there exists an
A
-dependent constant
c>0
such that for every small enough
r>0
, there exists a finite mesh
U_{r}\subset A_{+4r^{2}}\cap D
with
|U_{r}|\leq cr^{10}
and

A\subset\bigcup_{(p,q)\in U_{r}}B^{+}(p,r)\times B^{-}(q,r).

If

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(A)>\mathcal{K}(A\cap D)+\epsilon

(10.2)

for some
\epsilon>0
, then there exists
(p,q)\in U_{r}
so that either

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(B^{+}(p,r)\times B^{-}(q,r))>\mathcal{K}(p,q)+\epsilon/2\qquad\text{ or }\qquad\mathcal{K}(p,q)>\mathcal{K}(A\cap D)+\epsilon/2.

Thus by a union bound the probability of (
10.2
) is bounded above by

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{(p,q)\in U_{r}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(B^{+}(p,r)\times B^{-}(q,r))>\mathcal{K}(p,q)+\epsilon/2\right)\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}(A_{+r^{2}}\cap D)-\mathcal{K}(A\cap D)>\epsilon/2\right)

As
r\downarrow 0
, the first term above goes to
0
by assumption (iii) and the bound on
|U_{r}|
, and the second term goes to
0
by the continuity of
\mathcal{K}
, proving (
10.1
). Now, suppose that
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(u)>\mathcal{K}(u)
for some
u\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
. Then by the continuity of
\mathcal{K}
, for some compact set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
containing
u
, we have
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(A)\geq\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(u)>\mathcal{K}(A)
. This contradicts (
10.1
), implying that
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
.

Moreover,
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(x,s;y,t)=\mathcal{K}(x,s;y,t)=-\infty
for
s>t
by assumption (ii). Finally, assumption (iv) guarantees that
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}=-\infty
on the set
E=\{u=(x,s;y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:x\neq y\}
. Since
\mathcal{K}\leq\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
everywhere, this implies
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
on
E
. Putting all this together gives that
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
.
∎

10.1
Independent models

Conditions (iii) and (iv) of Proposition
10.1
, while technical, can be checked for sequences of last passage models with an independence structure whose one-point marginals converge to appropriately rescaled Tracy-Widom random variables.

Definition 10.2
.

A collection of random cadlag functions
F=\{F_{j}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R},j\in\mathbb{Z}\}
is an
independent environment
if for any disjoint sets
[a_{1},b_{1}]\times\{j_{1}\},\dots,[a_{k},b_{k}]\times\{j_{k}\}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
, the increments
F_{j_{i}}(b_{i})-F_{j_{i}}(a_{i})
are independent for
i=1,\dots,k
.

Any independent environment
F
gives rise to a directed metric
d_{F}
on
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
as in Example
6.4
. We will check the conditions of Proposition
10.1
for sequences of rescaled line last passage metrics arising from independent environments. Standard examples of lattice last passage percolation fit into this framework by the embedding (
6.2
).

Consider sequences of independent environments
F^{n}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
, positive real numbers
m_{n}
, continuous additive metrics
h_{n}
on
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
(see Example
6.1
), and linear maps
L_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
of the form

L_{n}(x,y)=(a_{n}x,b_{n}x+c_{n}y).

Each
L_{n}
is the composition of a horizontal shear and a diagonal transformation. Recalling the definition of
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}}
in (
6.3
), let
\mathcal{K}_{n}
be the pullback under
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}}\circ L_{n}
of the directed metric

m_{n}d_{F_{n}}+h_{n}=d_{m_{n}F_{n}}+h_{n}

on
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
.
By Lemma
5.2
, each
\mathcal{K}_{n}
is a directed metric of negative sign. Moreover, the rescaling by
m_{n}
and the augmentation by the additive metric
h_{n}
does not change the geodesic structure, so geodesics in
\mathcal{K}_{n}
are simply pullbacks under
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}}\circ L_{n}
of geodesics in
d_{F^{n}}
. In particular, if
F^{n}
itself arose from a lattice environment, then these are pullbacks of lattice last passage geodesics in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

Now, the independence structure of
F^{n}
guarantees that each
d_{F^{n}}
is a temporally independent metric in the sense of Theorem
9.1
. Since each
L_{n}
is the composition of a diagonal transformation and a horizontal shear, each
\mathcal{K}_{n}
is also temporally independent. Therefore if the two-time marginals of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
converge to Airy sheets, then
\mathcal{K}_{n}
converges to
\mathcal{L}
in the multi-time sense, implying that
\mathcal{K}_{n}
satisfies condition (i) of Proposition
10.1
where the limit is the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{K}_{n}(x,s;y,t)=0
whenever
s>t
, so
\mathcal{K}_{n}
