also satisfies condition (ii). Conditions (iii) and (iv) of Proposition
10.1
will require the following additional assumption on
\mathcal{K}_{n}
.

Mobile Tracy-Widom convergence:

A sequence of random functions
\mathcal{K}_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies
mobile Tracy-Widom convergence
if for any sequence
u_{n}\to u=(x,t;y,t+s^{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, we have

\mathcal{K}_{n}(u_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{L}(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}sT-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{s^{3}}.

Here
\mathcal{L}
is the directed landscape, and
T
is a standard GUE Tracy-Widom random variable.

We can now state the main hypograph convergence theorem for independent environments; this is a rephrasing of Theorem
1.16
.

Theorem 10.3
.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{n}
be a sequence of random directed metrics of negative sign arising from a sequence of independent environments on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
as above. Suppose that
\mathcal{K}_{n}
satisfies mobile Tracy-Widom convergence and that
\mathcal{K}_{n}|_{S_{t,t+s^{3}}}
converges in
\mathcal{E}_{*}(S_{t,t+s^{3}})
to an Airy sheet of scale
s
for all
t\in\mathbb{R},s>0
. Then
\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{K}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{L}
in
\mathcal{E}_{*}
.

Remark 10.4
.

In the setting of Theorem
10.3
, we actually have a stronger joint convergence result. For any countable union
S=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}S_{t_{i},t_{i}+s_{i}^{3}}
, the pair
(\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{K}_{n},\mathcal{K}_{n}|_{S})
converges in distribution to
(\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}|_{S})
. Here the underlying function space is

\mathcal{E}_{*}\times\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{E}_{*}(S_{t_{i},t_{i}+s_{i}^{3}})
.

10.2
A maximal inequality for last passage percolation

To pass from multi-time convergence to hypograph convergence in Theorem
10.3
, we need a maximal inequality for last passage percolation. This can be thought of as a last-passage version of Kolmogorov’s maximal inequality for sums of independent random variables. We will work within the framework of line last passage percolation set up in Example
6.4
. A similar maximal inequality could be proven for other directed metrics with an ordered independence structure, e.g. planar Poisson last passage percolation.

Through this subsection, we let
F
be an independent cadlag environment on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
, and let
d_{F}
be the corresponding directed metric of negative sign on
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
defined as in Example
6.4
.

As in Proposition
10.1
, for
A\subset(\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2))^{2}
and a directed metric
d
of negative sign on
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
, we will let

d(A)=\sup\{d(u):u\in A\}.

We use the shorthand
d(B,C)
for
d(B\times C)
.

Lemma 10.5

(Set-to-point maximal inequality)
.

Let
F
be an independent environment on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
, and let
d
be a deterministic, continuous directed metric of negative sign on
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Let
d_{F}^{*}=d_{F}+d
. Let
B\subset\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
be a finite union of intervals
[s,t]\times\{\ell\}
for
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}/2
. Then for any
p,q\in\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
and
c,\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(d_{F}^{*}(p,q)\leq c-\epsilon,c<d_{F}^{*}(p,B)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{b\in B}\;\mathbb{P}\left(d_{F}^{*}(b,q)<-\epsilon\right),

(10.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(d_{F}^{*}(p,q)\leq c-\epsilon,c<d_{F}^{*}(B,q)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{b\in B}\;\mathbb{P}\left(d_{F}^{*}(p,b)<-\epsilon\right).

(10.4)

Note that we could have considered more general sets
B
in Lemma
10.5
; we work with finite unions of intervals for simplicity. The deterministic correction
d
is necessary since we will apply Lemma
10.5
to shifted versions of line last passage metrics.

Proof of Lemma
10.5
.

We only prove (
10.3
). The inequality (
10.4
) follows by a symmetric argument. Let
\partial B
be the boundary of
B
in
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Note that
\partial B
is a finite set since
B
is a finite union of intervals.
For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, define the finite approximation

B_{k}=\{(x,\ell)\in B:2^{k}x\in\mathbb{Z}\}\cup\partial B.

For any point
(x,\ell)\in B
, that there is a nonincreasing sequence
x_{k}
with
(x_{k},\ell)\in B_{k}
such that
x_{k}\downarrow x
. Since
F
is a cadlag environment and
d
is continuous, we always have that

\limsup_{k\to\infty}d_{F}^{*}(p,(x_{k},\ell))\geq d_{F}^{*}(p,(x,\ell)).

Therefore
d_{F}^{*}(p,B_{k})\uparrow d_{F}^{*}(p,B)
as
k\to\infty
.

Now define a total order on
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
by letting
(x,n)<(y,m)
whenever
n>m
, or
n=m
and
x<y
.
For each
k
, define

\tau_{k}=\min\{b\in B_{k}:d^{*}_{F}(p,b)>c\},

where the minimum is with respect to this total order.
If the set on the right hand side above is empty, set
\tau_{k}=\infty
. The triangle inequality for
d^{*}_{F}
implies that on the event
\tau_{k}\neq\infty
, we have

d_{F}^{*}(p,\tau_{k})+d_{F}^{*}(\tau_{k},q)\leq d_{F}^{*}(p,q)

Now, using that
d_{F}^{*}(p,\tau_{k})>c
on the event
\tau_{k}\neq\infty
, we have the containment of events

E_{1,k}=\{\tau_{k}\neq\infty,d^{*}_{F}(p,q)\leq c-\epsilon\}\subset E_{2,k}=\{\tau_{k}\neq\infty,d_{F}^{*}(\tau_{k},q)<-\epsilon\}.

(10.5)

Now, since
d_{F}^{*}(p,B_{k})\uparrow d_{F}^{*}(p,B)
, we have that

E_{1,k}\to E_{1}=\{c<d^{*}_{F}(p,B),d^{*}_{F}(p,q)\leq c-\epsilon\}.

The event
E_{1}
is the event on the left hand side of (
10.3
). Therefore by (
10.5
), to complete the proof it suffices to show that

\mathbb{P}E_{2,k}\leq\sup_{b\in B}\mathbb{P}(d_{F}^{*}(b,q)<-\epsilon)

(10.6)

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We have

\mathbb{P}E_{2,k}\leq\max_{b\in B_{k}}\mathbb{P}(d_{F}^{*}(b,q)<-\epsilon\;|\;\tau_{k}=b).

(10.7)

Since
F
is an independent environment, the random variable
d_{F}^{*}(b,q)
is independent of the event
\tau_{k}=b
by the order in which we explored the set
B
. Therefore the right hand side of (
10.7
) is equal to

\max_{b\in B_{k}}\mathbb{P}(d_{F}^{*}(b,q)<-\epsilon).

This is bounded above by the right hand side of (
10.6
), as desired.
∎

The ideas behind Lemma
10.5
can be iterated to bound maximum last passage values between two sets.

Lemma 10.6

(Set-to-set maximal inequality)
.

Let
F
be an independent environment on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
, and let
d
be a deterministic continuous directed metric of negative sign on
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Let
d_{F}^{*}=d_{F}+d
.
Let
A,B\subset\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
be finite unions of sets of the form
[s,t]\times\{\ell\}
for
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}/2
and let
p,q\in\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Then

\begin{split}&\;\mathbb{P}\left(d_{F}^{*}(p,q)\leq c-\epsilon,c<d_{F}^{*}(A,B)\right)\leq\;\sup_{a\in A}\mathbb{P}(d_{F}^{*}(p,a)<-\epsilon/2)+\sup_{b\in B}\mathbb{P}\left(d_{F}^{*}(b,q)<-\epsilon/2\right).\end{split}

(10.8)

Note that we do not impose any ordering constraints on
p,q,A,B
in Lemma
10.5
. Theorem
1.15
is the special case of Lemma
10.6
when
d=0
and
A,B\subset\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

We define the same finite approximations
B_{k}\subset B
as in the proof of Lemma
10.5
so that
d^{*}_{F}(A,B_{k})\uparrow d^{*}_{F}(A,B)
as
k\to\infty
. We set

\tau_{k}=\min\{b\in B_{k}:d_{F}^{*}(A,b)>c\},

where the minimum is with respect to the total order used in the proof of Lemma
10.5
. We set
\tau_{k}=\infty
if the set on the right hand side above is empty. On the event
\tau_{k}\neq\infty
, the triangle inequality gives that

d_{F}^{*}(w,\tau_{k})+d_{F}^{*}(\tau_{k},q)\leq d_{F}^{*}(w,q)

for any
w
.
Taking a supremum in the above inequality over all points
w\in A
gives that

d_{F}^{*}(A,\tau_{k})+d_{F}^{*}(\tau_{k},q)\leq d_{F}^{*}(A,q).

Since
d_{F}^{*}(A,\tau_{k})>c
on the event
\tau_{k}\neq\infty
, this implies the containment of events

D_{1,k}=\{\tau_{k}\neq\infty,d_{F}^{*}(A,q)\leq c-\epsilon/2\}\subset D_{2,k}=\{\tau_{k}\neq\infty,d_{F}^{*}(\tau_{k},q)<-\epsilon/2\}.

(10.9)

Now set

D_{1}=\{c<d^{*}_{F}(A,B),d_{F}^{*}(A,q)\leq c-\epsilon/2\}.

Exactly as in the proof of Lemma
10.5
, we have that

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}D_{1}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{P}D_{1,k}\leq\sup_{k}\mathbb{P}D_{2,k}\leq\sup_{b\in B}\mathbb{P}\left(d_{F}^{*}(b,q)<-\epsilon/2\right).\end{split}

(10.10)

Finally, the event on the left hand side of (
10.8
) is contained in the union

D_{1}\cup\{d_{F}^{*}(p,q)\leq c-\epsilon,c-\epsilon/2<d_{F}^{*}(A,q)\}.

The probability
\mathbb{P}D_{1}
is bounded by (
10.10
). The probability of the second event above can be bounded by Lemma
10.5
. A union bound completes the proof.
∎

Remark 10.7
.

Both Lemma
10.5
and Lemma
10.6
also apply in the lattice setting, by virtue of the embedding from lattice to lines. In the lattice setting the proofs simplify since we no longer need to take a discrete approximation of the sets in the lemma.

10.3
The proof of Theorem
10.3

We can now use the maximal inequality check tightness conditions (iii) and (iv) in Proposition
10.1
about convergence of random sequences of functions to prove Theorem
10.3
.

We first translate the maximal inequality to the limiting setting.

Corollary 10.8
.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{n}
be as in Theorem
10.3
. For any compact convex sets
A,B\subset\mathbb{R}^{2},p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
c,\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\begin{split}&\;\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,q)\leq c-\epsilon,c<\mathcal{K}_{n}(A,B)\right)\\
&\leq\;\sup_{a\in A}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,a)<-\epsilon/2)+\sup_{b\in B}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{n}(b,q)<-\epsilon/2)\right).\end{split}

(10.11)

Proof.

We use the notation introduced in Section
10.1
.
Let
F^{n}
be the sequence of independent environments that give rise to
\mathcal{K}_{n}
. Then
\mathcal{K}_{n}
is the pullback under
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}}\circ L_{n}
of a metric
d_{F_{n}}^{*}=d_{m_{n}F_{n}}+h_{n}
on
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
, see the discussion after Definition
10.2
. The environments
m_{n}F_{n}
are independent, and each
h_{n}
is a deterministic continuous directed metric, so
d_{F_{n}}^{*}
satisfies all the conditions of Lemma
10.6
. Moreover, since
B
and
C
are compact and convex,
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}}L_{n}B
and
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}}L_{n}C
are finite unions of closed intervals in
\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)
. Hence we can apply Lemma
10.6
and take a pullback to prove the corollary.
∎

Proof of Theorem
10.3
and Remark
10.4
.

We check the conditions of Proposition
10.1
.
As discussed prior to the statement of Theorem
10.3
,
\mathcal{K}_{n}
satisfies conditions (i) and (ii) of that proposition. It remains to check conditions (iii) and (iv). Throughout the proof we use the shorthand

A(p,q,r)=B^{+}(p,r)\times B^{-}(q,r).

See the statement of Proposition
10.1
for the definition of the right hand side above.

We first show (iii). Fix a compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
and let
(p,q)\in K
. By a union bound, for any
m>0
we can write

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{n}(A(p,q,r))-\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,q)>\epsilon\right)\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,q)|\geq m)+\sum_{k=-{\left\lfloor 2m/\epsilon\right\rfloor}}^{{\left\lceil 2m/\epsilon\right\rceil}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,q)\leq k\epsilon/2,\;\;k\epsilon/2+\epsilon/2<\mathcal{K}_{n}(A(p,q,r)).\end{split}

(10.12)

To see the above inequality, consider two numbers
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
a-b>\epsilon
. Then either
|b|>m
, or else there exists some lattice point
x\in(\epsilon/2)\mathbb{Z}\cap[-m,m+\epsilon/2)
such that
b\leq x\leq x+\epsilon/2<a
. The above union bound uses this containment with
a=\mathcal{K}_{n}(A(p,q,r))
and
b=\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,q)
.
By Corollary
10.8
, the right hand side of (
10.12
) is bounded above by

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,q)|\geq m)+\sum_{k=-{\left\lfloor 2m/\epsilon\right\rfloor}}^{{\left\lceil 2m/\epsilon\right\rceil}}\sup_{z\in B^{+}(p,r)}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,z)<-\epsilon/2)+\sup_{z\in B^{-}(q,r)}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{n}(z,q)<-\epsilon/2)\right).

(10.13)

We can pass to the limit
\mathcal{L}
via mobile Tracy-Widom convergence as long as
r
is small enough so that
A(p,q,r)
has compact closure in
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
for all
(p,q)\in K
. This gives that the limsup of (
10.13
) is equal to

\mathbb{P}(|\mathcal{L}(p,q)|\geq m)+\sum_{k=-{\left\lfloor 2m/\epsilon\right\rfloor}}^{{\left\lceil 2m/\epsilon\right\rceil}}\sup_{z\in B^{+}(p,r)}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}(p,z)<-\epsilon/2)+\sup_{z\in B^{-}(q,r)}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}(z,q)<-\epsilon/2)\right)

(10.14)

Using the Tracy-Widom tail bounds from Theorem
6.8
, (
10.14
) is bounded above by

c_{K}e^{-d_{K}m^{3/2}}+(cm/\epsilon)e^{-d(\epsilon/(2r)-1)^{3/2}},

where
c_{K}
and
d_{K}
are
K
-dependent constants, and
c
and
d
are constants. Setting
m=1/r
, this expression is
o(r^{10})
as
r\to 0^{+}
, yielding (iii).

We now show (iv). Let
A
be a compact subset of
\{(x,s;y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:x\neq y\}
. We may assume that

A=\{(x,s;y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:x\in I,y\in J,s\in T\}

where
I,J,
and
T
are closed intervals and
I
and
J
are disjoint. Any other compact subset of
\{(x,s;y,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:x\neq y\}
can be covered by a finite union of sets of this form. For any fixed
\delta>0
, define

\mathcal{I}_{\delta}=\{(p,q)=(x,s-3\delta^{3}/2;y,s+3\delta^{3}/2):x\in I\cap(\delta^{2}/2)\mathbb{Z},y\in J\cap(\delta^{2}/2)\mathbb{Z},s\in T\cap(\delta^{3}/2)\mathbb{Z}\}.

Then for all small enough
r>0
we have that

A_{+r}\subset\bigcup_{(p,q)\in\mathcal{I}_{\delta}}A(p,q,\delta).

For all small enough
r>0
and
m>0
, by a union bound we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(A_{+r})>-m)\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathcal{I}_{\delta}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(A(p,q,\delta))>-m)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathcal{I}_{\delta}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(A(p,q,\delta))>-m,\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,q)\leq-2m)+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,q)>-2m).

(10.15)

By Corollary
10.8
, (
10.15
) is bounded above by

\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathcal{I}_{\delta}}\sup_{z\in B^{+}(p,\delta)}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,z)<-m/2)+\sup_{z\in B^{-}(q,\delta)}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{n}(z,q)<-m/2)\right)+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,q)>-2m).

(10.16)

Passing to the limit via mobile Tracy-Widom convergence gives that the
\limsup
of (
10.16
) is bounded above by

\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathcal{I}_{\delta}}\sup_{z\in B^{+}(p,\delta)}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}(p,z)<-m/2)+\sup_{z\in B^{-}(q,\delta)}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}(z,q)<-m/2)\right)+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}(p,q)>-2m).

(10.17)

Applying the Tracy-Widom tail-bounds in Theorem
6.8
and using that
|\mathcal{I}_{\delta}|\leq c_{A}\delta^{-7}
for an
A
-dependent constant
c_{A}
gives that (
10.17
) is bounded above by

c_{A}\delta^{-7}\left(\exp(-d(m/\delta)^{3/2})+\exp\left(-d[(1/(c_{A}\delta^{4})-m/\delta)^{+}]^{3/2}\right)\right),

for an
A
-dependent constant
c_{A}>0
and an absolute constant
d>0
.
Therefore for every fixed
m>0
, we have

\limsup_{r\to 0^{+}}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(A_{+r}>-m)\leq c_{A}\delta^{-7}\left(\exp(-d|m/\delta|^{3/2})+\exp\left(-d[(1/(c_{A}\delta^{4})-m/\delta)^{+}]^{3/2}\right)\right).

Now, the left hand side above is independent of
\delta
, and the right hand side above tends to
0
as
\delta\to 0
. Assumption (iv) of Proposition
10.1
follows.

Now, let
S
be as in the statement of the theorem, and let
D
be any countable dense set in
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
such that
D\cap S
is dense in
S
.
The sequence
(\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{K}_{n},\mathcal{K}|_{D},\mathcal{K}_{n}|_{S})
is tight, and by Proposition
10.1
and Theorem
9.1
any distributional subsequential limit is of the form
(\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}|_{D},\mathcal{L}^{\prime}|_{S})
, where both
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
are directed landscapes. Moreover,
\mathcal{L}|_{D\cap S}=\mathcal{L}^{\prime}|_{D\cap S}
, so by continuity
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}|_{S}=\mathcal{L}|_{S}
. This gives the joint convergence in Remark
10.4
.
∎

Remark 10.9
.

We have stated Theorem
10.3
with the assumption of mobile Tracy-Widom convergence since it is natural and often easy to check in practice. However, the reader carefully following the proof of Theorem
10.3
will note that a weaker moment assumption would suffice. For example, instead of using mobile Tracy-Widom convergence to bound (
10.13
) and (
10.16
), we could use the following moment estimate.

\sup_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{z\in B^{+}(p,r),z\in B^{-}(p,r)}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,z)^{-}]^{11}=o(r^{11}).

11
Graph convergence to
\mathcal{L}

Hypograph convergence is a strictly weaker notion than graph convergence. In fact, it is possible to construct sequences of i.i.d. lattice last passage percolation models whose hypographs converge to
\mathcal{L}
, but whose graphs do not, see Example
13.13
in Section
13
.

To move from hypograph convergence to graph convergence, we will not only need a way of controlling large values in prelimits of
\mathcal{L}
, but also a way of controlling small values. Specifically, the following will be enough. For this proposition, we work with both open balls
B(q,r)
and their closures, denoted by
\bar{B}(q,r)
.

Proposition 11.1
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{K}_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
is a sequence of random functions. Let
D
be a countable dense subset of
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
and suppose that
(\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{K}_{n},\mathcal{K}_{n}|_{D})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}|_{D})
for some random continuous function
\mathcal{K}:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\to\bar{\mathbb{R}}
with
\mathcal{K}(x,s;y,t)=-\infty
whenever
s\geq t
, and
\mathcal{K}
is finite on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
. Suppose that
for every open ball
B(q,r)\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
whose closure is also contained in
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, there exists a sequence of finite subsets
D_{1}\subset D_{2}\dots
of
D\cap B(q,r)
such that
D_{m}\to\bar{B}(q,r)
in the Hausdorff metric, and for every
\epsilon>0
we have

\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{u\in B(q,r)}\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)<\inf_{u\in D_{m}}\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)-\epsilon\right)=0.

(11.1)

Then
\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{K}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{K}
in
\mathcal{E}_{*}
.

Proof.

Let
(\Gamma,\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{K},\mathcal{K}|_{D})
be any joint subsequential distributional limit of
(\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{K}_{n},\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{K}_{n},\mathcal{K}_{n}|_{D})
. Define

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(u)=\inf\{x:(u,x)\in\Gamma\}

for all
u\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
. This is a lower semicontinuous function. Noting that

\mathcal{K}(u)=\sup\{x:(u,x)\in\Gamma\},

to prove the desired convergence it is enough to show that almost surely,
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}\geq\mathcal{K}
.
Since
\mathcal{K}=-\infty
on the set
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}=\{(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}:s\geq t\}
, this inequality holds on that set. Now suppose that
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(u)<\mathcal{K}(u)
for some
u\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
. Let
\bar{B}(q_{i},r_{i})\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
be a sequence of closed balls with rational center and rational radius
r_{i}\to 0
such that
u\in B(q_{i},r_{i})
for all
i
. By the continuity of
\mathcal{K}
, we have

\inf_{v\in B(q_{i},r_{i})}\mathcal{K}(v)\to\mathcal{K}(u).

Hence for large enough
i
, we have

\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(u)<\inf_{v\in B(q_{i},r_{i})}\mathcal{K}(v),

(11.2)

and so

\inf_{v\in B(q_{i},r_{i})}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(v)<\inf_{v\in B(q_{i},r_{i})}\mathcal{K}(v).

(11.3)

Since there are only countably many choices of
q_{i},r_{i}
, it is enough to show that the probability that (
11.3
) holds is zero for every fixed rational pair
q,r
. Letting
D_{m}
be as in the statement of the lemma for the set
B(q,r)
, by the continuity of
\mathcal{K}
the event in (
11.3
) is equal to the union over
k\in\mathbb{N}
of the events

B_{k}:=\bigcap_{m=1}^{\infty}A_{m,k},\qquad\text{ where }\qquad A_{m,k}=\left\{\inf_{v\in B(q,r)}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(v)<\inf_{v\in D_{m}}\mathcal{K}(v)-1/k\right\}.

We will show that

\mathbb{P}A_{m,k}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}A_{m,k,n},\quad\text{ where }\quad A_{m,k,n}=\left\{\inf_{v\in B(q,r)}\mathcal{K}_{n}(v)<\inf_{v\in D_{m}}\mathcal{K}_{n}(v)-1/k\right\}.

(11.4)

By the assumption of the lemma, the right hand side of the inequality in (
11.4
) goes to
0
as
m\to\infty
. Hence (
11.4
) implies that
\mathbb{P}B_{k}=0
, giving that the probability of (
11.3
) is also
0
.

It remains to show (
11.4
). It is perhaps easiest to see this with Skorokhod’s representation theorem. Consider a coupling of the
\mathcal{K}_{n}
s along a subsequence
N
such that almost surely,
\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{K}_{n}\to\Gamma
and
\mathcal{K}_{n}|_{D}\to\mathcal{K}|_{D}
. Graph convergence implies that for any
(v,\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(v))\in\Gamma
, there is a sequence
v_{n}\to v
such that
\mathcal{K}_{n}(v_{n})\to\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(v)
. Then for
v
in the open ball
B(q,r)
, we have

\limsup_{n\in N}\inf_{w\in B(q,r)}\mathcal{K}_{n}(w)\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{K}_{n}(v_{n})=\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(v).

Taking the infimum over
v\in B(q,r)
we get

\limsup_{n\in N}\inf_{v\in B(q,r)}\mathcal{K}_{n}(v)\leq\inf_{v\in B(q,r)}\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(v).

Since
D_{m}
is finite and
\mathcal{K}_{n}|_{D}\to\mathcal{K}|_{D}
almost surely, we also have

\inf_{v\in D_{m}}\mathcal{K}_{n}(v)\to\inf_{v\in D_{m}}\mathcal{K}(v)

almost surely.
Hence in this coupling, almost surely

\omega\in A_{m,k}\qquad\implies\qquad\omega\in A_{m,k,n}\text{ for all large enough $n\in N$},

and so
\mathbf{1}(A_{m,k})\leq\liminf_{n\in N}\mathbf{1}(A_{m,k,n})
almost surely. Applying Fatou’s lemma and replacing the liminf over
n\in N
with a limsup over all
n\in\mathbb{N}
yields (
11.4
).
∎

For directed metrics
\mathcal{K}_{n}
in the form of Theorem
10.3
, the conditions of Proposition
11.1
can be shown by a chaining argument as long as we have moderate deviation bounds on the one-point distributions of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
. This type of chaining argument was pioneered by
Talagrand 2006
, and was used previously in this setting by
Basu et al. 2014
to prove lower bounds on the infimum of Poisson last passage percolation. We adapt the chaining framework for general planar directed metrics. The KPZ scaling is built into our setup to give an optimal result in the cases we care about.

We first set up a lemma needed for the chaining argument.
For each
p=(p_{1},p_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we will set

p_{k}^{+}=(p_{k,1}^{+},p_{k,2}^{+})

to be a point in
2^{-2k/3}\mathbb{Z}\times 2^{-k}\mathbb{Z}
which is close to
p
, while still lying ahead of
p
in the time direction. More precisely, define
p_{k}^{+}
so that
|p-p_{k}^{+}|
is minimal subject to the condition

p_{2}+2^{-k}\leq p^{+}_{k,2}<p_{2}+2^{-k+1}.

(11.5)

Similarly let
p_{k}^{-}
denote the point in
2^{-2k/3}\mathbb{Z}\times 2^{-k}\mathbb{Z}
which minimizes
|p-p_{k}^{-}|
subject to the condition
p_{2}-2^{-k}\geq p^{-}_{k,2}>p_{2}-2^{-k+1}
. If there are multiple closest points, choose an arbitrary method of breaking ties. We note for later use that with these definitions we have

|p_{1}-p_{k,1}^{+}|\leq|p_{2}-p_{k,2}^{+}|^{2/3}\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad|p_{k,1}^{+}-p_{k-1,1}^{+}|\leq 2|p_{k,2}^{+}-p_{k-1,2}^{+}|^{2/3},

(11.6)

as well as the corresponding inequalities for
p_{k}^{-}
. Now consider a directed metric
d
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We will control the infimum of
d
on a set
A
in terms of its values on a grid. Define

\eta^{+}_{k}(d,A)=\inf_{p\in A}d(p,p_{k}^{+})\qquad\;\text{and}\;\qquad\delta^{+}_{k}(d,A)=\inf_{p\in A}d(p_{k+1}^{+},p_{k}^{+})

and similarly let

\eta^{-}_{k}(d,A)=\inf_{p\in A}d(p^{-},p)\quad\;\text{and}\;\qquad\delta^{-}_{k}(d,A)=\inf_{p\in A}d(p_{k}^{-},p_{k+1}^{-}).

Finally, for a set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we let
A_{\ell}^{+}=\{p_{\ell}^{+}:p\in A\}
and
A_{\ell}^{-}=\{p_{\ell}^{-}:p\in A\}
.

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
d
be a directed metric of negative sign and let
K=A\times B\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Then for every
\ell<k\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\inf_{u\in K}d(u)\geq\inf_{u\in A^{+}_{\ell}\times B^{-}_{\ell}}d(u)+\sum_{i=\ell}^{k-1}\left(\delta^{+}_{i}(d,A)+\delta^{-}_{i}(d,B)\right)+\eta^{+}_{k}(d,A)+\eta^{-}_{k}(d,B).

(11.7)

Proof.

We have the iterated triangle inequality

d(p,q)\geq d(p,p^{+}_{k})+\sum_{i=\ell}^{k-1}d(p^{+}_{i+1},p^{+}_{i})+d(p^{+}_{\ell},q^{-}_{\ell})+\sum_{i=\ell}^{k-1}d(q^{-}_{i},q^{-}_{i+1})+d(q^{-}_{k},q).

Taking infima over
A\times B
on both sides and using that
\inf R+T\geq\inf R+\inf T
for sets
R,T\subset\mathbb{R}
yields (
11.7
).
∎

We can use Lemma
11.2
to get a probabilistic condition for when a sequence of directed metrics satisfies the conditions of Proposition
11.1
.

Proposition 11.3
.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{n}
be a sequence of random directed metrics of negative sign on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and let
K=A\times B\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
. Suppose that there exists a sequence
k(n)\to\infty
such that for every
\epsilon>0
we have

•

\mathbb{P}(\eta_{k(n)}^{+}(\mathcal{K}_{n},A)<-\epsilon),\mathbb{P}(\eta_{k(n)}^{-}(\mathcal{K}_{n},B)<-\epsilon)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

•

There exist positive numbers
\beta_{i,n}(\epsilon)
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{p\in A}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(p^{+}_{i+1},p^{+}_{i})<-\epsilon/i^{2})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-5i/3}\beta_{i,n}(\epsilon),

\displaystyle\sup_{q\in B}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(q^{-}_{i},q^{-}_{i+1})<-\epsilon/i^{2})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-5i/3}\beta_{i,n}(\epsilon),\qquad\;\text{and}\;

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=\ell}^{k(n)}\beta_{i,n}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=0.

(11.8)

Then for any
\epsilon>0
,

\lim_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{u\in K}\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)<\inf_{u\in A^{+}_{m}\times B^{-}_{m}}\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)-\epsilon\right)=0.

Proof.

By Lemma
11.2
, we just need to show that

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=\ell}^{k(n)-1}\left(\delta^{+}_{i}(\mathcal{K}_{n},A)+\delta^{-}_{i}(\mathcal{K}_{n},B)\right)+\eta^{+}_{k(n)}(\mathcal{K}_{n},A)+\eta^{-}_{k(n)}(\mathcal{K}_{n},B)<-\epsilon\right)=0

(11.9)

for all
\epsilon>0
. First, for
\ell\geq 2
we have the union bound

\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=\ell}^{k(n)-1}\delta_{i}^{+}(\mathcal{K}_{n},A)<-\epsilon/4\right)\leq\sum_{i=\ell}^{k(n)-1}\mathbb{P}\left(\delta_{i}^{+}(\mathcal{K}_{n},A)<-\epsilon/(4i^{2})\right)

(11.10)

By the construction of the points
p_{i}^{+}
, there is a constant
c_{A}>0
such that the cardinality of the set

\{(p_{i+1}^{+},p_{i}^{+}):p\in A\}

is bounded above by
c_{A}2^{5i/3}
for all
i
. Therefore by a second union bound, the right hand side of (
11.10
) is bounded above by

c_{A}\sum_{i=\ell}^{k(n)}\beta_{i,n}(\epsilon/4).

The probability that
\sum\delta_{i}^{-}(\mathcal{K}_{n},B)
is greater than
\epsilon/4
can be similarly bounded.
Finally,
\mathbb{P}(\eta_{k(n)}^{+}(\mathcal{K}_{n},A)>-\epsilon/4)
and
\mathbb{P}(\eta_{k(n)}^{-}(\mathcal{K}_{n},B)>-\epsilon/4)
tend to
0
with
n
by assumption. Putting these four bounds together and using the asymptotics (
11.8
) on
\beta_{i,n}(\epsilon/4)
proves (
11.9
).
∎

We also record a quantitative version of Proposition
11.3
. This will be used later on when establishing tightness of exponential moments of last passage models.

Corollary 11.4
.

In the setting of Proposition
11.3
, for
m\geq 2,n\in\mathbb{N},
and
\epsilon>0
, we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\inf_{u\in K}\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)&<\inf_{u\in A^{+}_{m}\times B^{-}_{m}}\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)-\epsilon\Bigg)\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}(\eta_{k(n)}^{+}(\mathcal{K}_{n},A)<-\epsilon/4)+\mathbb{P}(\eta_{k(n)}^{-}(\mathcal{K}_{n},B)<-\epsilon/4)+c_{K}\sum_{i=m}^{k(n)}\beta_{i,n}(\epsilon/4),\end{split}

(11.11)

where
c_{K}>0
is a
K
-dependent constant.

Corollary
11.4
follows by simply keeping track of the bound we get on the probability of the right side of (
11.11
) when proving Proposition
11.3
.

Putting Proposition
11.3
together with Proposition
11.1
, we obtain the following corollary. We state this for convergence to the directed landscape.

Corollary 11.5
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{K}_{n}
is a sequence of random directed metrics of negative sign, and let

D=\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}\cap\left(\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}(2^{-2k/3}\mathbb{Z}\times 2^{-k}\mathbb{Z})^{2}\right).

Suppose that
(\mathfrak{h}K_{n},K|_{D})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}|_{D})
, where
\mathcal{L}
is the directed landscape. Suppose that for every compact set of the form
A\times B\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, that
\mathcal{K}_{n}
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
11.3
on
A\times B
. Then
\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{K}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{L}
.

Corollary
11.5
follows from Propositions
11.1
and
11.3
. The only thing to note is that (
11.1
) holds for all compact sets, rather than just products
A\times B
, since any compact set in
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
is contained in a finite union of such products.

12
Convergence for lattice last percolation models

In this section, we combine all of the results of the prior sections to give convergence criteria for lattice last passage percolation models with independent, identically distributed weights.

To make the statements more concrete and avoid topological issues, all theorems in this section and the next are given in terms of couplings for sequences of directed metrics converging to the directed landscape. The first type of coupling we consider will satisfy the following three conditions on a set
\Omega
of probability one. To state these conditions, let
\mathcal{K}_{n}
be a sequence of random directed metrics of negative sign on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Multi-time convergence (on
S
).
Let
S
be a finite or countable union of sets of the form

S_{s,t}=\{(x,s,y,t):x,y\in\mathbb{R}\},

where
s<t
. Then
\mathcal{K}_{n}\to\mathcal{L}
compactly on
S_{s,t}
for any
S_{s,t}\subset S
.

Hypograph convergence.
The hypographs
\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{K}_{n}\to\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{L}
in
\mathcal{D}_{*}
. By Lemma
7.3
, this implies that

•

(\mathcal{K}_{n}-\mathcal{L})^{+}\to 0
compactly on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
,

•

for any
u_{n}\to u\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
, we have
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{K}_{n}(u_{n})\leq\mathcal{L}(u)
,

•

for any
u\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
there exists a random sequence
U_{n}\to u
such that
\mathcal{K}_{n}(U_{n})\to\mathcal{L}(u)
.

Geodesic convergence off holes.
Let
(p_{n},q_{n})\to(p,q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, and let
\pi_{n}
be a sequence of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
-geodesics from
p_{n}
to
q_{n}
. Then
\pi_{n}
is precompact in the Hausdorff topology. If
\omega\in\Omega
is in the set

\{\mathcal{K}_{n}(p_{n},q_{n})\to\mathcal{L}(p,q)\}

then all subsequential limits of
\pi_{n}(\omega)
are
\mathcal{L}
-geodesics from
p
to
q
. If

\omega\in\{\text{There exists a unique $\mathcal{L}$-geodesic $\pi$ from $p$ to $q$}\},

then
\pi_{n}(\omega)\to\pi
.

A second, stronger coupling encodes graph convergence and the resulting strengthened geodesic convergence. This coupling will satisfy the following two conditions almost surely.

Graph convergence.

\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{K}_{n}\to\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{L}
as graphs in
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
. In particular,
\mathcal{K}_{n}\to\mathcal{L}
compactly on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
.

Geodesic convergence everywhere.
For any sequence
(u_{n},v_{n})\to(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
and any
\mathcal{K}_{n}
-geodesics
\pi_{n}
from
u_{n}
to
v_{n}
, the sequence
\pi_{n}
is precompact in the Hausdorff topology and any subsequential limit
\pi
of
\pi_{n}
is an
\mathcal{L}
-geodesic from
u
to
v
. In particular, if there is a unique
\mathcal{L}
-geodesic
\pi
from
u
to
v
, then
\pi_{n}\to\pi
.

The first theorem in this section gives conditions for when a sequence of potentially changing i.i.d. lattice last passage models can be coupled with the directed landscape to satisfy multi-time convergence, hypograph convergence, and geodesic convergence off holes. We do not consider graph convergence or geodesic convergence everywhere in this setting. By results of the previous sections, our main input is convergence to the Airy line ensemble.

Recall that for a lattice environment
X
,

X[p^{k}\to q^{k}]=\sup_{\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{v\in\pi_{i}}X_{v},

where the supremum is over all sets of
k
disjoint minimal length lattice paths
\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k}
, where
\pi_{i}
goes from
p-(0,k-i)
to
q+(0,i-1)
. We also write

X[p\to_{\Delta_{k}}q]=X[p^{k}\to q^{k}]-X[p^{k}\to q^{k}],

where
X[p^{0}\to q^{0}]=0
.

A random function
\mathfrak{A}^{r}:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a
parabolic Airy line ensemble of scale
r
if

\mathfrak{A}^{r}(\cdot)=r\mathfrak{A}(\cdot/r^{2}),

where
\mathfrak{A}
is a parabolic Airy line ensemble. A parabolic Airy line ensemble of scale
r

is related to an Airy sheet of scale
r
in the same way that the parabolic Airy line ensemble is related to the Airy sheet.

Theorem 12.1
.

Let
X_{n}=\{X_{n}(u):u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}
be a sequence of arrays of i.i.d. random variables with distribution
\mu_{n}
supported on
[0,\infty)
. Let
m_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
be a sequence converging to
\infty
with
n
. Suppose that there exist sequences
\alpha_{n},\beta_{n},\chi_{n},\tau_{n}
such that for every sequence
r_{n}
converging to some
r>0
, the functions

A^{r_{n}}_{n,k}(y)=\frac{X_{n}[(0,{\left\lfloor r_{n}n\right\rfloor})\to_{\Delta k}({\left\lfloor r_{n}m_{n}\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor\tau_{n}y\right\rfloor},1)]-\alpha_{n}{\left\lfloor r_{n}n\right\rfloor}-\tau_{n}y\beta_{n}}{\chi_{n}}

(12.1)

converge jointly in distribution as
n\to\infty
to an Airy line ensemble of scale
r^{1/3}
in the product-of-Skorokhod topologies on functions from
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. Suppose also that as
n\to\infty
we have

\frac{\beta_{n}}{\chi_{n}}\to 0,\qquad\frac{\beta_{n}m_{n}-\alpha_{n}n}{n\chi_{n}}\to 0,\qquad\frac{1}{\tau_{n}}\to 0,\qquad\frac{m_{n}}{n\tau_{n}}\to 0.

(12.2)

Then letting
d_{X_{n}}
denote the lattice last passage metric defined from
X_{n}
, after rescaling and centering
d_{X_{n}}
can be coupled with
\mathcal{L}
so that for any finite or countable union
S=\bigcup S_{s_{i},t_{i}}
, we have multi-time convergence on
S
, hypograph convergence, and geodesic convergence off holes.
More precisely, we define a sequence of additive metrics by setting
h_{n}:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
by

h_{n}(m_{n}t/n+x,-t)=\alpha_{n}t+\beta_{n}x,

and extending
h_{n}
to the directed edge set
E
for
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by letting
h_{n}((e_{1},e_{2}))=h(e_{1})
.
Let
\mathcal{K}_{n}
be the pullback onto
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of the metric

\chi_{n}^{-1}(d_{X_{n}}-h_{n})

under the map
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\circ L_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cup E,

where
L_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the linear map with matrix

A=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\tau_{n}&m_{n}\\
0&n\end{array}\right],

and
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
is as in (
6.4
). Then for any finite or countable union
S=\bigcup S_{s_{i},t_{i}}
, there is a coupling of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}
satisfying multi-time convergence on
S
, hypograph convergence, and geodesic convergence off holes.

The proof of Theorem
12.1
is straightforward but tedious, and consists of carefully checking the assumptions of the convergence theorems in the previous sections. We remark that the scaling assumptions in (
12.2
) are quite natural. They are there purely to handle lattice effects. The first two conditions amount to the statement that as
n\to\infty
,

\frac{|h_{n}(v)-h_{n}(v^{\prime})|}{\chi_{n}}\to 0

(12.3)

for adjacent vertices
v
and
v^{\prime}
. The final two scaling conditions are equivalent to the statement that if
v
and
v^{\prime}
are adjacent vertices in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
connected in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by an edge, then
\|L_{n}^{-1}v-L_{n}^{-1}v^{\prime}\|_{2}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. In particular, the third scaling condition actually follows from the Airy line ensemble convergence, (
12.1
). Indeed, if
\tau_{n}^{-1}
did not converge to
0
, then subsequential limits of the line ensembles
A^{r_{n}}
would be piecewise linear.

Proof.

Step 1: Converting to a line environment.
In order to apply the theorems of previous sections, we first convert
X_{n}
into a line environment. As in (
2.9
), define sequences of nondecreasing cadlag environments
\bar{X}_{n}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
by letting

\bar{X}_{n,i}(0)=0,\qquad\;\text{and}\;\qquad\bar{X}_{n,i}(y)-\bar{X}_{n,i}(x)=\sum_{p\in(x,y]\times\{i\}}X_{n}(p).

By the discussion following equation (
6.2
), the line last passage metric
d_{\bar{X}_{n}}
is simply the pullback via
f_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
of the lattice last passage metric
d_{X_{n}}
. We also define the continuous additive metric
d_{\bar{h}_{n}}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}\cup E_{v}
from the functions
\bar{h}_{n}:\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\begin{split}\bar{h}_{n}(m_{n}t/n+x,-t)&=\alpha_{n}t+\beta_{n}x,\qquad(m_{n}t/n+x,-t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}\quad\;\text{and}\;\\
\bar{h}_{n}(m_{n}t/n+x,-t)&=h_{n}(m_{n}t/n+x,-t-1/2),\qquad(m_{n}t/n+x,-t)\in\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}+1/2).\end{split}

(12.4)

Let
\mathcal{L}_{n}
be the pullback of
\chi_{n}^{-1}(d_{\bar{X}_{n}}-\bar{h}_{n})
under the map
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}}\circ L_{n}
.
By (
12.3
) and the fact that
d_{\bar{X}_{n}}
is the pullback of
d_{X_{n}}
, it suffices to prove the theorem for
\mathcal{L}_{n}
in place of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
. For this, we check the conditions of Theorem
10.3
. Before checking these conditions, we show why this theorem implies the results.

Step 2: The proof assuming Theorem
10.3
.
If Theorem
10.3
applies (or more precisely, Remark
10.4
), then for any countable union
S=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}S_{s_{i},t_{i}}
, we have that
(\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{L}_{n},\mathcal{L}_{n}|_{S})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}|_{S})
. By Skorokhod’s representation theorem, there exists a coupling of
\mathcal{L}_{n},\mathcal{L}
such that
(\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{L}_{n},\mathcal{L}_{n}|_{S})\to(\mathfrak{h}\mathcal{L},\mathcal{L}|_{S})
almost surely. This immediately implies multi-time convergence on
S
and hypograph convergence in this coupling.

Finally, geodesic convergence off holes holds by Theorem
8.5
and Theorem
8.7
. We just need to check that the conditions of those theorems hold almost surely for
\mathcal{L}_{n},\mathcal{L}
. The directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
is almost surely a spacetime metric by Proposition
6.7
and Definition
6.6
. The metrics
\mathcal{L}_{n}
satisfy conditions 1 and 2 of Theorem
8.7
since
d_{\bar{X}_{n}}(x,s;y,t)>-\infty
if and only if
x\leq y
and
{\left\lfloor s\right\rfloor}\geq{\left\lceil t\right\rceil}
. Geodesics in the pullback of
d_{\bar{X}_{n}}
under
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
are piecewise linear curves consisting of vertical and horizontal segments. Hence geodesics in
\mathcal{L}_{n}
are also piecewise linear curves since adding the additive metric
\bar{h}_{n}
does not affect the geodesics (see the discussion after Example
6.1
), and pulling back the metric under the invertible linear map
L_{n}
just pulls back all geodesics by Lemma
5.9
. Therefore Condition 3 of Theorem
8.7
also holds.

Step 3: Reducing Theorem
10.3
to Theorem
4.4
.
We now check the two conditions Theorem
10.3
. To do this, we will reduce to a statement that can be checked via Theorem
4.4
.

The mobile Tracy-Widom convergence and Airy sheet convergence required for Theorem
10.3
are both implied by the statement that for any
s_{n}\to s\in\mathbb{R}
and
r_{n}\to r>0
we have

\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{L}_{n}(\cdot,s_{n};\cdot,s_{n}+r_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{S}_{r^{1/3}}

(12.5)

Here the underlying topology is graph convergence in
\mathcal{E}_{*}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(see Remark
7.4
), and
\mathcal{S}_{r^{1/3}}
denotes an Airy sheet of scale
r^{1/3}
.
We first deal with the case when
ns_{n}\notin\mathbb{Z}
for all
n
; if
ns_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
for infinitely many
n
, there is an extra complication that we leave to Step 5.
Set

\tilde{r}_{n}=\frac{{\left\lfloor-ns_{n}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lceil-n(s_{n}+r_{n})\right\rceil}+1}{n}.

We will show that the convergence in (
12.5
) is equivalent to graph convergence of the functions

S_{n}(x,y)=\chi_{n}^{-1}\left(\bar{X}_{n}[(\tau_{n}x,{\left\lfloor\tilde{r}_{n}n\right\rfloor})\to({\left\lfloor\tilde{r}_{n}m_{n}\right\rfloor}+\tau_{n}y,1)]-(\alpha_{n}{\left\lfloor\tilde{r}_{n}n\right\rfloor}+\tau_{n}(y-x)\beta_{n})\right)

(12.6)

to
\mathcal{S}_{r^{1/3}}
. This we will be able to check with Theorem
4.4
. The deterministic second term in (
12.6
) is equal to

H_{n}(y-x):=\bar{h}_{n}(\tau_{n}(y-x)+\tilde{r}_{n}m_{n},-\tilde{r}_{n}n).

(12.7)

Letting
g_{n}=\bar{h}_{n}\circ f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}}\circ L_{n}
, we can write

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{n}(x,s_{n};y,s_{n}+r_{n})&=\chi_{n}^{-1}\bigg(\bar{X}_{n}[(\tau_{n}x+m_{n}s_{n},{\left\lfloor-ns_{n}\right\rfloor})\to[(\tau_{n}y+m_{n}(s_{n}+r_{n}),{\left\lceil-n(s_{n}+r_{n})\right\rceil})]\\
&-(g_{n}(y,s_{n}+r_{n})-g_{n}(x,s_{n}))\bigg).\end{split}

(12.8)

We have

\begin{split}g_{n}(y,s_{n}+r_{n})-g_{n}(x,s_{n})=\;&\bar{h}_{n}(\tau_{n}y+m_{n}(s_{n}+r_{n}),{\left\lfloor-n(s_{n}+r_{n})\right\rfloor})-\bar{h}_{n}(\tau_{n}x+m_{n}s_{n},{\left\lfloor-ns_{n}\right\rfloor})\\
=\;&\bar{h}_{n}(\tau_{n}(y-x)+m_{n}r_{n},-\tilde{r_{n}}n+O(1)).\end{split}

Here the first equality is by definition and the second inequality is by the linearity of
\bar{h}_{n}
. By (
12.3
) and the definition (
12.7
), this is equal to

H_{n}(y-x+\epsilon_{n})+o(\chi_{n}),\qquad\text{ where }\qquad\epsilon_{n}=\frac{m_{n}r_{n}-m_{n}\tilde{r}_{n}}{\tau_{n}}.

(12.9)

Next, by the translation invariance of
X_{n}
, the first term on the right hand side of (
12.8
) is equal in distribution, jointly in
x
and
y
, to

\begin{split}\bar{X}_{n}[(\tau_{n}(x+\delta_{n}),{\left\lfloor\tilde{r}_{n}n\right\rfloor})\to[(\tau_{n}(y+\delta_{n}+\epsilon_{n})+m_{n}\tilde{r}_{n},1)],\quad\text{where}\quad\delta_{n}=\frac{m_{n}s_{n}-{\left\lfloor m_{n}s_{n}\right\rfloor}}{\tau_{n}}.\end{split}

(12.10)

Combining (
12.10
) and (
12.9
), we get that

\mathcal{L}_{n}(x,s_{n};y,s_{n}+r_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}S_{n}(x+\delta_{n},y+\delta_{n}+\epsilon_{n})+o(1),

where the equality in distribution is joint in all
x
and
y
. Finally,
\delta_{n}=O(\tau_{n}^{-1})
and
\epsilon_{n}=O(m_{n}/n\tau_{n})
, so both terms converge to
0
by (
12.2
). Hence graph convergence of
\mathcal{L}_{n}(\cdot,s_{n};\cdot,s_{n}+r_{n})
is equivalent to graph convergence of
S_{n}
when
ns_{n}\notin\mathbb{Z}
.

Step 4: Checking Theorem
4.4
when
ns_{n}\notin\mathbb{Z}
.
Define the rescaled and linearly shifted line environment
Z^{n}
by

Z^{n}_{i}(y)=\chi_{n}^{-1}\left(\bar{X}_{n,i}(\tau_{n}y+{\left\lfloor\tilde{r}_{n}m_{n}\right\rfloor})+\beta_{n}\tau_{n}y\right).

By the fact that last passage values across cadlag paths commute with linear shifts, we have that

\begin{split}&S_{n}(x,y)=Z^{n}[(x+a_{n},{\left\lfloor\tilde{r}_{n}n\right\rfloor})\to(y,1)]+c_{n},\qquad\text{ where }\\
&a_{n}=-\frac{{\left\lfloor\tilde{r}_{n}m_{n}\right\rfloor}}{\tau_{n}}\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad c_{n}=-\frac{\alpha_{n}{\left\lfloor\tilde{r}_{n}n\right\rfloor}+\beta_{n}{\left\lfloor\tilde{r}_{n}m_{n}\right\rfloor}}{\chi_{n}}.\end{split}

(12.11)

We will check that
Z^{n}
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
4.4
. First note that
Z^{n}
is a cadlag line environment with positive jumps since
X_{n}
was supported on
[0,\infty)
. Next, letting
A^{\tilde{r}_{n}}_{n,k}
be as in (
12.1
), we have

\displaystyle A^{n,\tilde{r}_{n}}_{k}(y)=Z^{n}[(a_{n},{\left\lfloor\tilde{r}_{n}n\right\rfloor})\to_{\Delta_{k}}(y,1)]+c_{n}.

(12.12)

Here the
\to_{\Delta k}
notation for line models is as in Theorem
4.4
. This requires the fact that multi-point last passage values in a lattice environment are equivalent to multi-point last passage values in the corresponding line environment, Proposition
2.6
.

Moreover,
Z_{n}
inherits translation invariance from the i.i.d. environment
X_{n}
:

Z^{n}(\cdot)+\chi_{n}^{-1}\beta_{n}\tau_{n}z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}Z^{n}(\cdot+z)\qquad\text{ for any $z\in\tau_{n}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}$. }

Therefore letting
{\left\lceil x\right\rceil}_{n}
denote the largest element of
\tau_{n}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
which is greater than or equal to
x
, for any
z\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle Z^{n}[(a_{n}+z,{\left\lfloor\tilde{r}_{n}n\right\rfloor})\to_{\Delta_{k}}(z+y,1)]=\;

\displaystyle Z^{n}[({\left\lceil a_{n}+z\right\rceil}_{n},{\left\lfloor\tilde{r}_{n}n\right\rfloor})\to_{\Delta_{k}}(z+y,1)]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\;

\displaystyle Z^{n}[(a_{n},{\left\lfloor\tilde{r}_{n}n\right\rfloor})\to_{\Delta_{k}}(y+O(\tau_{n}^{-1}),1)]

where
O(\tau_{n}^{-1})
depends on
y
and
z
but is deterministically bounded in absolute value by
2|\tau_{n}^{-1}|
. Here the equality in distribution is as a function of both
y
and
k
.
Therefore by (
12.12
), the convergence assumption (
12.1
), and the fact that
\tau_{n}^{-1}=o(1)
by (
12.2
), for any
z\in\mathbb{R}
the sequence of functions

Z^{n}[(a_{n}+z,{\left\lfloor r_{n}n\right\rfloor})^{+}\to_{\Delta_{k}}(z+\cdot,1)]+c_{n}

converges in distribution in the product-of-Skorokhod topologies to an Airy line ensemble of scale
r^{1/3}
.
Moreover, again using that
X_{n}
is an i.i.d. environment,
we have that

Z^{n}[(a_{n}-x,{\left\lfloor r_{n}n\right\rfloor})\to_{\Delta_{k}}(y,1)]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}Z^{n}[(a_{n}-y,{\left\lfloor r_{n}n\right\rfloor})\to_{\Delta_{k}}(x,1)]

jointly in
x,y,
and
k
. Thus for every
z\in\mathbb{R}
, the sequence of functions

Z^{n}[(a_{n}+z-\cdot,{\left\lfloor r_{n}n\right\rfloor})\to_{\Delta_{k}}(z,1)]+c_{n}

also converges in the product-of-Skorokhod topologies to the Airy line ensemble of scale
r^{1/3}
.
Therefore by Theorem
4.4
and (
12.11
), the sequences
S_{n}
can be coupled to an Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}_{r}
of scale
r^{1/3}
so that
S_{n}\to\mathcal{S}_{r}
compactly a.s. Therefore
\mathfrak{g}S_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{g}S_{r}
, as desired.

Step 5: The extension on the lattice.
Finally, we extend (
12.5
) to the case when
ns_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
for some
n
. When

(\tau_{n}x+m_{n}s_{n},-ns_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, then the right hand side of (
12.8
) is equal to

\mathcal{L}_{n}(x,s_{n};y,s_{n}+r_{n})+\chi_{n}^{-1}X_{n}(\tau_{n}x+m_{n}s_{n},-ns_{n}).

Therefore since all entries in the environment
X_{n}
are nonnegative, the right hand side of (
12.8
) is always an upper bound for
\mathcal{L}_{n}(x,s_{n};y,s_{n}+r_{n})
and a lower bound for

\mathcal{L}_{n}(x-\tau_{n}^{-1},s_{n};y,s_{n}+r_{n})+\chi_{n}^{-1}\left(g_{n}(x,s_{n})-g_{n}(x-\tau_{n}^{-1},s_{n})\right)=\mathcal{L}_{n}(x-\tau_{n}^{-1},s_{n};y,s_{n}+r_{n})+\chi_{n}^{-1}\beta_{n}.

The equality above follows from writing
g_{n}
in terms of
\bar{h}_{n}
, and then evaluating
\bar{h}_{n}
. The term
\chi_{n}^{-1}\beta_{n}\to 0
and the shift
\tau_{n}^{-1}\to 0
by the first and third conditions in (
12.2
).
Therefore the sequence
\mathcal{L}(x,s_{n};y,s_{n}+r_{n})
is stochastically dominated above and below by sequences of random functions whose graphs converge to
\mathfrak{g}S_{r}
, and so
\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{L}(x,s_{n};y,s_{n}+r_{n})\to\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{S}_{r}
, as desired.
∎

Theorem
12.1
simplifies greatly if
X_{n}=X
for all
X
and
m_{n}={\left\lfloor\rho n\right\rfloor}
for all
n
for some fixed constant
\rho
. In this case, the sequences
\chi_{n},\tau_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}
can be given by
\chi n^{1/3},\tau n^{2/3},\alpha,\beta
for fixed numbers
\chi,\tau,\alpha,\beta>0
. The
n^{1/3},n^{2/3}
scaling for
\chi_{n}
and
\tau_{n}
is forced upon us by the
1-2-3
scale invariance of the directed landscape. Note that when the sequences
X_{n}
change with
n
and
m_{n}
is not proportional to
n
, this no longer needs to be the case. We will see an example of this in Section
13
for sequences of environments of i.i.d. geometric random variables. We also address graph convergence and geodesic convergence everywhere in this simpler setting.

Theorem 12.2
.

Let
X=\{X(u):u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}
be a collection of i.i.d. random variables in
[0,\infty)
and let
\rho>0
. Suppose that there exists
\alpha,\beta,\chi,\tau>0
such that

A^{n}_{k}(y)=\chi^{-1}n^{-1/3}\left(X[(0,n)\to_{\Delta k}({\left\lfloor\rho n\right\rfloor}+\lfloor\tau n^{2/3}y\rfloor,1)]-(\alpha n+\beta\tau n^{2/3}y)\right)

converge jointly in distribution as
n\to\infty
, in the product-of-Skorokhod topologies on functions from
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, to the Airy line ensemble.
Let
h
be the additive metric on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cup E
defined from the function
h:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
with

\displaystyle h(\rho t+x,-t)

\displaystyle=\alpha t+\beta x.

Set
h(e_{1},e_{2})=e_{1}
for all edges.
Let
\mathcal{K}_{n}
be the pullback onto
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of the metric

\chi^{-1}n^{-1/3}(d_{X}-h)

under the map

f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\circ L_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cup E,

where
L_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is the linear map with matrix

A=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\tau n^{2/3}&\rho n\\
0&n\end{array}\right].

Then for any finite or countable union
S=\bigcup S_{s_{i},t_{i}}
, there is a coupling of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}
satisfying multi-time convergence on
S
, hypograph convergence, and geodesic convergence off holes.
If, in addition, for some
\delta>0
we have that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{y\in[-3,3]}\mathbb{E}[(A^{n}_{1}(y))^{-}]^{5+\delta}<\infty,

(12.13)

then there is a coupling of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}
satisfying graph convergence and geodesic convergence everywhere.

The fifth moment condition is essentially optimal given our chaining method, see Example
13.13
. Note that the constant
3
in (
12.13
) is arbitrary; a variant of the same proof would work with
3
replaced by some
\epsilon>0
. However, in practice we do not expect that there is any difference between the verifiability of the two conditions.

We expect that for general i.i.d. random variables whose support is all of
\mathbb{R}
, rather than
[0,\infty)
, that it is equivalent to the condition that
\mathbb{E}[X(0,0)^{-}]^{5+\delta}>-\infty
. It is not difficult to check that if
\mathbb{E}[X(0,0)^{-}]^{5}=-\infty
then graph convergence to any continuous limit will fail.

Just as with the proof of Theorem
12.1
, the proof of Theorem
12.2
is a straightforward but tedious check on the assumptions of convergence theorems in previous sections.

Proof.

It is straightforward to check that
X
satisfies the Airy line ensemble convergence condition in Theorem
12.1
. This follows since we are using the same environment for all
n
, and since the scaling factors satisfy the
1-2-3
KPZ scaling. Moreover, all of the scaling conditions in (
12.2
) are immediate, so Theorem
12.1
applies.

Therefore we just need to check graph convergence and geodesic convergence everywhere. For this, it suffices to show that we can couple
\mathcal{K}_{n},\mathcal{L}
so that
\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{K}_{n}\to\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{L}
.
If this is the case, then for any sequence
(u_{n},v_{n})\to(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, we have
\mathcal{K}_{n}(u_{n},v_{n})\to\mathcal{L}(u,v)
. Since all geodesics in
\mathcal{K}_{n}
are connected, we can apply Theorem
8.5
to conclude that geodesic convergence everywhere holds in this coupling.

Rather than showing graph convergence of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
to
\mathcal{L}
, we will show graph convergence of
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
to
\mathcal{L}
, where
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
is the pullback of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
under a map
g_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

•

g_{n}(u)=u+\xi_{n}
for a sequence
\xi_{n}\to 0
,

•

L_{n}\circ g_{n}\mathbb{Q}^{2}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{2}=\emptyset
for all
n
.

The first condition above guarantees that the conclusion of Theorem
12.1
holds for
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
, and that graph convergence of
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
is equivalent to graph convergence of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
. The second condition will allow us to avoid lattice difficulties when applying the results of Section
11
.

By Corollary
11.5
, it is enough to check the conditions of Proposition
11.3
for
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
for any fixed compact set
A\times B\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
.

Let
k(n)={\left\lceil 3\log_{2}(n)/4\right\rceil}
. By the bounds in (
11.5
) and (
11.6
) relating
p
and
p_{k(n)}^{+}
, we can check that

L_{n}(p_{k(n)}^{+})-L_{n}(p)=(\Theta(n^{1/4})+O(n^{1/6}),\Theta(n^{1/4}))

for all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Therefore for large enough
n
,
\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,p_{k(n)}^{+})
is finite for all
p
. Moreover, since
X(u)
is supported on
[0,\infty)
,
\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,p_{k(n)}^{+})
is bounded below by

-\chi n^{-1/3}\left(h_{n}\circ f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\circ L_{n}(p_{k(n)}^{+})-h_{n}\circ f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\circ L_{n}(p)\right)=-\chi n^{-1/3}\Theta(n^{1/4}).

(12.14)

A similar deterministic lower bound holds on
\mathcal{K}_{n}(p_{k(n)}^{-},p)
. The right hand side of (
12.14
) tends to
0
with
n
, giving the first condition of Proposition
11.3
.

Now, for all
i\leq k(n)-1
and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, since
L_{n}\circ g_{n}\mathbb{Q}^{2}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{2}=\emptyset
, we can explicitly write out
\mathcal{K}_{n}(p_{i+1}^{+},p_{i}^{+})
as in (
12.8
):

\begin{split}&\mathcal{K}_{n}(p_{i+1}^{+},p_{i}^{+})\\
=\;&\chi n^{-1/3}\bigg(X_{n}[({\left\lceil\tau n^{2/3}p_{i+1,1}^{+}+\rho np_{i+1,2}^{+}\right\rceil},{\left\lfloor-np_{i+1,2}^{+}\right\rfloor})\to({\left\lfloor\tau n^{2/3}p_{i,1}^{+}+\rho np_{i,2}^{+}\right\rfloor},{\left\lceil-np_{i,2}^{+}\right\rceil})]\\
-\;&\left(h_{n}\circ f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\circ L_{n}(p_{i}^{+})-h_{n}\circ f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\circ L_{n}(p_{i+1}^{+})\right)\bigg).\end{split}

(12.15)

Now let

p_{i,n}=\frac{1}{n}\left({\left\lfloor-np_{i+1,2}^{+}\right\rfloor}-{\left\lceil-np_{i,2}^{+}\right\rceil}+1\right)\qquad\;\text{and}\;\qquad q_{i,n}=\frac{p_{i,1}^{+}-p_{i+1}^{+}}{p_{i,n}^{2/3}}.

(12.16)

By translation invariance of
X_{n}
, (
12.15
) is equal in distribution to

\begin{split}&\chi n^{-1/3}\bigg(X[(0,np_{i,n})\to({\left\lfloor\rho np_{i,n}\right\rfloor}+{\left\lfloor\tau(np_{i,n})^{2/3}(q_{i,n}+\epsilon_{n})\right\rfloor},1)]\\
-\;&\alpha np_{i,n}+\beta\tau(np_{i,n})^{2/3}(q_{i,n}+\epsilon_{n})+\delta_{n}\bigg),\end{split}

(12.17)

where
\epsilon_{n}
and
\delta_{n}
are error terms satisfying
|\epsilon_{n}(np_{i,n})^{2/3})|,|\delta_{n}|\leq c
for some constant
c
that is independent of the initial choice of
p
. We can then rewrite (
12.17
) in terms of
\mathfrak{A}^{np_{i,n}}_{1}
to get that

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}_{n}(p_{i+1}^{+},p_{i}^{+})&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}O(n^{-1/3})+p_{i,n}^{1/3}\mathfrak{A}_{1}^{np_{i,n}}\left(q_{i,n}+\epsilon_{n}\right).\end{split}

(12.18)

By (
11.6
) and the error bound on
\epsilon_{n}
, we have that
q_{i,n}+\epsilon_{n}\in[-3,3]
for large enough
n
and
i\leq k(n)
. Moreover, from the bound in (
11.5
) we have that
p_{i,2}^{+}-p_{i+1,2}^{+}\geq 2^{-i}
for all
i
. Therefore for any
n\in\mathbb{N},p\in\mathbb{R}^{2},i\leq k(n)
, we have that
np_{i,n}\geq cn^{1/4}-1
for some universal
c>0
. These two facts will allow us to bound (
12.18
) via (
12.13
) and Markov’s inequality.

More precisely, there exists
c^{\prime},d>0
and
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0},\epsilon\geq c^{\prime}n^{-1/3},p\in\mathbb{R}^{2},
and
i\leq k(n)
, we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{n}(p_{i+1}^{+},p_{i}^{+})\leq-\epsilon\right)&\leq\sup_{y\in[-3,3]}\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{A}^{np_{i,n}}_{1}(y)\leq-p_{i,n}^{-1/3}\epsilon/2)\\
&\leq d\epsilon^{-(5+\delta)}p_{i,n}^{(5+\delta)/3}\leq d\epsilon^{-(5+\delta)}{2}^{-(5+\delta)(i-1)/3}.\end{split}

(12.19)

The third inequality follows from the bound in (
11.5
), which ensures that
p_{i,2}^{+}-p_{i+1,2}^{+}\leq 2^{-i+1}
for all
i
. The same bound holds for
\mathcal{K}_{n}(q_{i}^{-},q_{i+1}^{-})
for all
q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Given (
12.19
), we can satisfy the second condition of Proposition
11.3
for all
\epsilon>0
by taking
\beta_{i,n}(\epsilon)=i^{-2}
for all
n\geq n_{0},i\geq i_{0}
for some
\epsilon
-dependent natural numbers
n_{0},i_{0}
.
∎

Remark 12.3
.

Theorem
1.17
can be extracted from the proof of Theorem
12.2
. Indeed, the assumption of multi-time convergence to
\mathcal{L}
used in that theorem is the only consequence of the Airy line ensemble convergence in Theorem
12.2
that we uses in the proof above.

All statements about convergence of last passage metrics to the directed landscape can be converted into statements that do not use the directed metric framework or the topological language of Theorems
12.1
,
12.2
. The statements become cleaner in this context, since we need not be concerned with preserving the metric structure of the prelimit when we embed. We demonstrate this here by translating the conclusion of Theorem
12.2
. We have simplified the conclusions slightly here. We also include a statement about joint convergence to the Airy line ensemble here that will be of use in Section
13
.

Corollary 12.4
.

Let
X
be an i.i.d. environment of nonnegative random variables and let
\rho,\chi,\tau,\alpha,\beta
be positive numbers. Let
(x,s)_{n}=({\left\lfloor\rho ns+\tau n^{2/3}x\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor-ns\right\rfloor})
, and define

\mathcal{L}_{n,\Delta k}(x,s;y,t)=\frac{X_{n}[(x,s)_{n}\to_{\Delta k}(y,t)_{n}]-\beta(t-s)n-\alpha\tau(y-x)n^{2/3}}{\chi n^{1/3}},

and write
\mathcal{L}_{n}:=\mathcal{L}_{n,\Delta 1}
.
Suppose that
\{\mathcal{L}_{n,\Delta k}(0,0;y,1):(y,k)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}\}
converges in distribution in the product-of-Skorokhod topologies to the Airy line ensemble. Then
\mathcal{L}_{n}
can be coupled with the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
so that on a set
\Omega
of probability
1
, the following hold:

\mathcal{L}_{n}(\cdot,s;\cdot,t)\to\mathcal{L}(\cdot,s;\cdot,t)

compactly for all rational pairs
s,t
, and for any
u_{n}\to u\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}_{n}(u_{n})\leq\mathcal{L}(u).

Now fix
(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, let
\pi_{n}
be any sequence of last passage geodesics from
(x,s)_{n}
to
(y,t)_{n}
, and let
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\Omega
be the set of probability one where
\mathcal{L}
has a unique geodesic
\pi
from
(x,s)
to
(y,t)
. Then on
\Omega^{\prime}
, the sequence of rescaled geodesics

\tilde{\pi}_{n}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\tau^{-1}n^{-2/3}&-\rho\tau^{-1}n^{-2/3}\\
0&-n^{-1}\end{array}\right]\pi_{n}

converges to
\pi
in the Hausdorff topology. For this convergence, we view the geodesics
\pi_{n}
as subsets of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, which are then in turn subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Moreover, we can additionally couple
A^{n}_{k}(\cdot)=\mathcal{L}_{n,\Delta k}(0,0;\cdot,1)
so that
A^{n}_{k}(\cdot)
converges to an Airy line ensemble
\mathfrak{A}
, and
\mathfrak{A}
and the Airy sheet
\mathcal{L}(\cdot,0;\cdot,1)
are coupled as in Remark
4.13
.
Finally, if in addition there exists
\delta>0
such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{y\in[-3,3]}\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{L}_{n}(0,0;y,1)^{-})^{5+\delta}<\infty,

(12.20)

then the coupling can be set up so that
\mathcal{L}_{n}\to\mathcal{L}
compactly on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
on
\Omega
.

Proof.

This is essentially just a rephrasing of Theorem
12.2
.
Note that there is only a slight difference between the definitions of
\mathcal{L}_{n,\Delta k}(0,0;y,1)
and
A^{n}_{k}(y)
in Theorem
12.2
, and between
\mathcal{L}_{n}
and
\mathcal{K}_{n}
from Theorem
12.2
. In particular, we have that

\mathfrak{A}^{n}_{k}(y)=\mathcal{L}_{n,k}(0,0;y+O(n^{-2/3}),1)+O(n^{-1/3}),

so convergence of
\mathcal{L}_{n,\Delta k}(0,0;y,1)
to the Airy line ensemble
\mathfrak{A}
implies convergence of
\mathfrak{A}^{n}_{k}(y)
to the Airy line ensemble and (
12.20
) implies (
12.13
). Also,

\mathcal{L}_{n}(u)=\mathcal{K}_{n}(u+O(n^{-2/3}))+O(n^{-1/3}),

(12.21)

so all convergence statements about
\mathcal{K}_{n}
pass over to convergence statements about
\mathcal{L}_{n,1}
. The fact that we can couple the limiting Airy line ensemble
\mathfrak{A}
with the Airy sheet
\mathcal{L}(0,0;\cdot,1)
follows from Remark
4.13
.
Finally, it is straightforward to check that each of the geodesics
\tilde{\pi}_{n}
is Hausdorff distance
O(n^{-2/3})
from a geodesic in
\mathcal{K}_{n}
from
(x,s)
to
(y,t)
. The geodesic convergence in the corollary then follows from geodesic convergence off holes for
\mathcal{K}_{n}
.
∎

Remark 12.5
.

The results of this section only apply to nonnegative random variables as written. This is due to the technical constraints on the combinatorics of cadlag last passage percolation from Section
2
. Though we do not pursue this here, the results of this section could be modified to included more general random variables whose support is potentially all of
\mathbb{R}
.

For example, for an i.i.d. environment of general random variables
X_{u}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying all the assumptions of Theorem
12.2
except nonnegativity, for each
n
we could replace the environment
X=\{X_{u}:u\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\}
with a sequence of nonnegative environments
X^{n}=\{(X_{u}+c_{n})^{+}:u\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\}
for a sequence of constants
c_{n}
satisfying
\mathbb{P}(X_{u}+c_{n}\leq 0)=o(n^{-2})
. On box of size
O(n)\times O(n)
, the two environments are equal with high probability up to a constant shift. Hence by shifting the second environment by shifted versions of original function
h
, we could still ensure the Airy line ensemble convergence in Theorem
12.1
.

12.1
Exponential tightness

If we strengthen certain tail bound assumptions in the setup of Theorem
12.2
and Corollary
12.4
, then we can move from distributional convergence to convergence of moments.
We give an example of how to do this in the setting of Theorem
12.2
with additional exponential control on one-point distributions.

Theorem 12.6

(Upper exponential tightness)
.

Consider the setup of Theorem
12.2
without the additional assumption (
12.13
). Suppose that there exists
a>1,\mathfrak{p}>0
such that for every compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{u\in K}\mathbb{E}a^{|\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)|^{\mathfrak{p}}}<\infty.

(12.22)

Then there exists
b>1
so that for every compact
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}

there exists
c>0
so that for all large enough
n

we have

\mathbb{E}b^{\sup_{K}(\mathcal{K}_{n}^{+})^{\mathfrak{p}}}<c.

Proof.

It suffices to prove this for compact product sets
A\times B\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
.
Fix points
p,q
with
\{p\}\times A,B\times\{q\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
. Let
m>0
. We apply Corollary
10.8
to
\mathcal{K}_{n}
with
c=m
,
\epsilon=2m/3
, and the given points
p,q
. This gives

\mathbb{P}\left(m<\sup_{u\in A\times B}\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)\right)\leq\mathbb{P}(K_{n}(p,q)>m/3)+\sup_{a\in A}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,a)<-m/3)+\sup_{b\in B}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{n}(b,q)<-m/3\right).

By Markov’s inequality and assumption (
12.22
), there exists
c>0
such that for all large enough
n
, the right hand side of this expression is bounded above by

ca^{-m^{\mathfrak{p}}/3^{\mathfrak{p}}},

yielding the result for every
1<b<a^{1/3^{\mathfrak{p}}}
.
∎

Theorem 12.7

(Lower exponential tightness)
.

In the setup of Theorem
12.2
, suppose additionally that there exists
a>1,\mathfrak{l}>0
, such that for every compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{u\in K}\mathbb{E}a^{(\mathcal{L}_{n}(u)^{-})^{\mathfrak{l}}}<\infty.

(12.23)

Then there exists
b>1
such that for every compact
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
there exists
c>0
so that for all large enough
n

we have

\mathbb{E}b^{\sup_{K}(\mathcal{L}_{n}^{-})^{\mathfrak{l}}}<c.

Proof.

As in the proof of Theorem
12.6
, we let our compact set
K
be of the form
A\times B\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
.
We will use the notation of Proposition
11.3
. Our aim is to appeal to Corollary
11.4
.

We follow the proof of Theorem
12.2
. With the choice
k(n)={\left\lfloor 3\log_{2}(n)/4\right\rfloor}
as in that proof, condition (
12.14
) shows that for large enough
n
,

\mathbb{P}(\eta_{k(n)}^{+}(\mathcal{K}_{n},A)<-1)+\mathbb{P}(\eta_{k(n)}^{-}(\mathcal{K}_{n},B)<-1)=0.

(12.24)

Next, recalling (
12.19
), there exists
c^{\prime}>0
and
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0},\epsilon\geq c^{\prime}n^{-1/3},p\in\mathbb{R}^{2},
and
i\leq k(n)
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{n}(p_{i+1}^{+},p_{i}^{+})\leq-\epsilon\right)\leq\sup_{y\in[-3,3]}\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{A}^{np_{i,n}}_{1}(y)\leq-p_{i,n}^{-1/3}\epsilon/2).

(12.25)

As in the proof of Theorem
12.2
, the quantity
np_{i,n}\to\infty
uniformly over
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
i\leq k(n)
, so by Markov’s inequality and the assumptions of the theorem, there exists
d,c_{\mathfrak{l}}>0
such that for all
n\geq n_{0},\epsilon\geq c^{\prime}n^{-1/3},p\in\mathbb{R}^{2},
and
i\leq k(n)
, the hand right side of (
12.25
) is bounded above by

d\exp(-\log(a)p_{i,n}^{-\mathfrak{l}/3}\epsilon^{\mathfrak{l}})\leq d\exp(-c_{\mathfrak{l}}2^{\mathfrak{l}i/3}\epsilon^{\mathfrak{l}}).

The same bound holds in the same parameter range for
\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{n}(p_{i}^{-},p_{i+1}^{-})\leq-\epsilon\right)
. Therefore the assumptions of Corollary
11.4
hold with

\beta_{i,n}(\epsilon)=d\exp(-c_{\mathfrak{l}}2^{\mathfrak{l}i/3}\epsilon^{\mathfrak{l}}i^{-2\mathfrak{l}})2^{5i/3}
for all
n\geq n_{0}
and
\epsilon\geq c^{\prime}n^{-1/3}k(n)^{2}
. In particular, as long as
n_{0}
is large enough we can use this value of
\beta_{i,n}(\epsilon)
for all
\epsilon>1
.

Next, let
m\geq 2
be the smallest integer so that
A_{m}^{+}\times B_{m}^{-}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
and let
b>2
. Then with notation as in Section
11
,

\mathbb{P}(\sup_{K}\mathcal{K}^{-}_{n}>b)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{A_{m}^{+}\times B_{m}^{-}}\mathcal{K}^{-}_{n}>b/2\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{K}\mathcal{K}_{n}-\inf_{A_{m}^{+}\times B_{m}^{-}}\mathcal{K}_{n}<-b/2\right)

(12.26)

By (
12.23
), a union bound, and Markov’s inequality, the first probability on the right side of (
12.26
) is bounded above by
c_{K}a^{-b^{\mathfrak{l}}/2^{\mathfrak{l}}}
, where
c_{K}>0
is a
K
-dependent constant. By Corollary
11.4
, the second term in (
12.26
) is bounded above by
c_{K}\sum_{i=m}^{k(n)}\beta_{i,n}(b/2)
. By the bounds on
\beta_{i,n}
, this is bounded above by
c_{K}\exp(-c_{\mathfrak{l}}b^{\mathfrak{l}})
, and so (
12.26
) is also bounded above by
c_{K}\exp(-c_{\mathfrak{l}}b^{\mathfrak{l}})
, yielding the result.
∎

13
Convergence of integrable last passage models

We now use the results of the previous sections to prove convergence of specific models of last passage percolation to the Airy sheet and the directed landscape. This section will cover the proofs of Theorems
1.7
and
1.8
. The language of convergence in our main theorems here is slightly different than in the introduction, we comment more on this in Remark
13.11
.

13.1
Integrable zoo

We first prove multi-time convergence, hypograph convergence, and geodesic convergence off holes for geometric last passage percolation in all feasible parameter directions by checking the conditions of Theorem
12.1
. We then appeal to coupling arguments to extend this to other integrable last passage models.

13.1.1
Geometric environment

Theorem 13.1
.

For each
\gamma\in(0,\infty)
, let
X_{\gamma}=\{X_{\gamma}(u):u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}
be an array of i.i.d. geometric random variables on
\{0,1,2,\dots\}
of mean
\gamma
. Let
n,m_{n},\gamma_{n}
be any sequences satisfying

n\to\infty,\qquad m_{n}\to\infty,\qquad nm_{n}\gamma_{n}\to\infty.

Then there exist parameters
\chi_{n},\tau_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}
such that with the sequence of environments
\mathcal{K}_{n}
defined as in Theorem
12.1
from the i.i.d. environments
X_{\gamma_{n}}
, the following holds: for any finite or countable union
S=\bigcup S_{s_{i},t_{i}}
, there is a coupling of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}
satisfying multi-time convergence on
S
, hypograph convergence, and geodesic convergence off holes. See the beginning of Section
12
for definitions.

Define
\chi_{n},\tau_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}
as follows. For each
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\gamma\in(0,\infty)
, define the arctic curve:

g_{n,\gamma}(m)=(m+n)\gamma+2\sqrt{mn\gamma(1+\gamma)},

(13.1)

which is the deterministic approximation of the last passage value
X_{\gamma}[(0,n)\to(m,1)]
.
We define all scaling parameters in terms of the value of the arctic curve
g=g_{n,\gamma_{n}}
and its derivatives
g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime}
evaluated at
m_{n}
:

n\alpha_{n}=g,\qquad\beta_{n}=g^{\prime},\qquad\tau_{n}^{3}=\frac{2g^{\prime}(1+g^{\prime})}{g^{\prime\prime 2}},\qquad\chi_{n}^{3}=\frac{(g^{\prime}(1+g^{\prime}))^{2}}{-2g^{\prime\prime}}.

(13.2)

In this and all remaining theorems in this section, we have matched our theorem presentation above with that of
Dauvergne et al. 2019
, so that the interested reader can easily refer back to the results we use in that paper. One can easily check that setting
\rho=m_{n}/n
in all theorems, that the scaling parameters match those in the table prior to Theorem
1.7
.

The scaling assumptions in Theorem
13.1
are optimal. The condition that
nm_{n}\gamma_{n}\to\infty
ensures that the mean total weight of the random variables in the box
[0,m_{n}]\times[1,n]
tends to infinity. If this is not the case, then
X_{n}[(0,n)\to(m_{n},1)]
could not rescale to a continuous random variable whose support is all of
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

We first check the four scaling conditions in (
12.2
). For this, we drop the subscripts
n
in order to avoid cluttered notation. Because the scaling relationships in (
13.2
) are expressed in terms of cubes, it will be easier to check that

\frac{\beta^{3}}{\chi^{3}}\to 0,\qquad\frac{|\beta m-\alpha n|^{3}}{(n\chi)^{3}}\to 0,\qquad\frac{1}{\tau^{3}}\to 0,\qquad\frac{m^{3}}{n^{3}\tau^{3}}\to 0.

(13.3)

Letting
\xi=\sqrt{\gamma(1+\gamma)n/m}
, we can calculate that

\frac{\beta_{n}^{3}}{\chi_{n}^{3}}=\frac{(\gamma+\xi)\xi}{(1+\gamma+\xi)^{2}m}\leq\frac{1}{m}.

This tends to zero since
m\to\infty
with
n
. Next, we calculate that

\displaystyle\frac{|\beta m-\alpha n|^{3}}{(n\chi)^{3}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(n\gamma+\xi m)^{3}\xi}{n^{3}(1+\gamma+\xi)^{2}(\gamma+\xi)^{2}m}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\gamma^{3}\xi}{(1+\gamma+\xi)^{2}(\gamma+\xi)^{2}m}+\frac{4\xi^{4}m^{2}}{n^{3}(1+\gamma+\xi)^{2}(\gamma+\xi)^{2}}

(13.4)

The first term in (
13.4
) is always bounded above by
4/m
, which tends to
0
as
n\to\infty
. The second term in (
13.4
) is bounded above by

\frac{4}{n}\frac{\xi^{4}m^{2}}{n^{2}\gamma^{2}(1+\gamma)^{2}}=\frac{4}{n},

which tends to
0
with
n
. Finally, we can write

\frac{1}{\tau^{3}}=\frac{\xi^{2}}{8(1+\gamma+\xi)(1+\xi)m^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{8m^{2}}

and

\frac{m^{3}}{n^{3}\tau^{3}}=\frac{\xi^{2}m}{8n^{3}(1+\gamma+\xi)(1+\xi)}\leq\frac{\xi^{2}m}{8\gamma(1+\gamma)n^{3}}=\frac{1}{8n^{2}},

both of which tend to
0
with
n
.
The assumption of Airy line ensemble convergence for each of the sequences (
12.1
) in Theorem
12.1
is a translation of Theorem 1.1 from
Dauvergne et al. 2019
. Note that the definition of last passage percolation in that paper follows lattice paths with edges in
\{(0,1),(1,0)\}
, rather than in
\{(0,-1),(1,0)\}
. Also, in that paper Airy line ensemble convergence is stated with a slightly different deterministic term subtracted from the main last passage term. The difference between the two deterministic terms is
O(\beta_{n}/\chi_{n})
, which tends to
0
with
n
by (
13.3
).
∎

Dauvergne et al. 2019
used the fact that geometric last passage percolation converges to the parabolic Airy line ensemble in any feasible direction in the parameter space to pass to other integrable last passage models via coupling. These couplings are strong enough that we can immediately get versions of Theorem
13.1
for these models.

13.1.2
Exponential environment

We can pass from a sequence i.i.d. geometric environments to an i.i.d. exponential environment via the usual geometric-to-exponential limiting transition. The details are given in
Dauvergne et al. 2019
, Corollary 6.1, and are applicable for the present convergence as well.

Corollary 13.2
.

Let
X
be an environment of independent exponential random variables of mean
1
. For any
m,n
define the arctic curve

g_{n}(m)=n+m+2\sqrt{nm},

which is the deterministic approximation of the last passage value
X[(0,n)\to(m,1)]
in this model.

Let
m_{n}\to\infty
be a sequence of natural numbers. Denoting by
g,g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime}
the value of
g_{n}
and its derivatives evaluated at
m_{n}
, we set the scaling parameters for this model:

\displaystyle n\alpha_{n}=g,\quad\beta_{n}=g^{\prime},\quad\tau_{n}^{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{2g^{\prime 2}}{g^{\prime\prime 2}}=\frac{8m_{n}^{2}(\sqrt{m_{n}}+\sqrt{n})^{2}}{n},\quad

\displaystyle\chi_{n}^{3}=\frac{g^{\prime 4}}{-2g^{\prime\prime}}=\frac{(\sqrt{m_{n}}+\sqrt{n})^{4}}{\sqrt{m_{n}n}}.

Then with the sequence of environments
\mathcal{K}_{n}
, defined as in Theorem
12.1
, the following holds: for any finite or countable union
S=\bigcup S_{s_{i},t_{i}}
, there is a coupling of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}
satisfying multi-time convergence on
S
, hypograph convergence, and geodesic convergence off holes.

13.1.3
Poisson lines environment

There are also two degenerations of the geometric environment to line last passage environments. The first is a Poisson transition which keeps the discrete nature of the weights. The details of this transition are given in
Dauvergne et al. 2019
, Corollary 6.3.

Corollary 13.3
.

Let
F=\{F_{i}:i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
be a collection of
n
independent rate-
1
Poisson processes. Define the Poisson lines arctic curve

g_{n}(t)=t+2\sqrt{tn}.

This is the deterministic approximation of the last passage value

F[(0,n)\to(t,1)]
. Let
t_{n}
be any sequence of nonnegative numbers such that
nt_{n}\to\infty
.
We define scaling parameters in terms of the arctic curve
g=g_{n}
and its derivatives
g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime}
taken in the variable
t
at the value
t_{n}
:

n\alpha_{n}=g,\qquad\beta_{n}=g^{\prime},\qquad\tau_{n}^{3}=\frac{2g^{\prime}}{g^{\prime\prime 2}},\qquad\chi_{n}^{3}=\frac{g^{\prime 2}}{-2g^{\prime\prime}}.

Define
h_{n}:\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)\to\mathbb{R}
by
h_{n}(m_{n}y+x,y)=\alpha_{n}y+\beta_{n}x
, and let
\mathcal{K}_{n}
be the pullback onto
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of the directed metric
\chi^{-1}(d_{F}-h_{n})
under the map
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}}\circ L_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
, where
L_{n}
is the linear map with matrix

A=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\tau_{n}&\rho_{n}\\
0&n\end{array}\right].

Then for any finite or countable union
S=\bigcup S_{s_{i},t_{i}}
, there is a coupling of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}
satisfying multi-time convergence on
S
, hypograph convergence, and geodesic convergence off holes.

13.1.4
Brownian last passage percolation

The second degeneration from a lattice to a line environment yields Brownian last passage percolation. Convergence of Brownian last passage percolation to the directed landscape was proven in
Dauvergne et al. 2018
. We include the result here for completeness.

Corollary 13.4
.

Let
\{B_{i}:i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
be a collection of independent copies of a two-sided Brownian motion of variance
1
. Define the Brownian arctic curve

g_{n}(t)=2\sqrt{tn},

which is the deterministic approximation of the last passage value
B[(0,n)\to(t,1)]
. We define scaling parameters in terms of the arctic curve
g=g_{n}
and its derivatives
g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime}
taken in the variable
t
at the value
1
:

n\alpha_{n}=g,\qquad\beta_{n}=g^{\prime},\qquad\tau_{n}^{3}=\frac{2}{g^{\prime\prime 2}},\qquad\chi_{n}^{3}=\frac{1}{-2g^{\prime\prime}}.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{n}
be defined from
d_{B}
and
\alpha_{n},\beta_{n},\tau_{n},\xi_{n}
as in Corollary
13.3
. Then for any for any finite or countable union
S=\bigcup S_{s_{i},t_{i}}
, there is a coupling of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}
satisfying multi-time convergence on
S
, hypograph convergence, and geodesic convergence off holes.

13.1.5
Poisson last passage percolation in the plane

Finally, geometric last passage percolation degenerates to a planar Poisson last passage percolation as we decrease the mean of the geometric random variables and make the grid
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
finer and finer. For this definition, we write
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\nearrow}=\{(a,c)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:a_{1}\leq c_{1},a_{2}\leq c_{2}\}
.

Definition 13.5
.

Let
P\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a collection of points such that
|P\cap K|
is finite for any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Let
d_{P}
be the directed metric of negative sign induced on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by the function
f:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\nearrow}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

f(a,c)=\begin{cases}1,\qquad P\cap\left([a_{1},c_{1}]\times[a_{2},c_{2}]\setminus\{a\}\right)\neq\emptyset\\
0,\qquad\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Observe that for
(a,c)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, that
d_{P}(a,c)+|P\cap\{a\}|
is a last passage value in the usual sense of Poisson last passage percolation. That is,

d_{P}(a,c)+|P\cap\{a\}|=\sup_{\pi}|P\cap\pi|,

where the supremum is over all continuous up-right paths from
a
to
c
. The details of the transition from discrete geometric environments to a Poisson environment are given in
Dauvergne et al. 2019
, Corollary 6.5.

Corollary 13.6
.

Let
P
be a Poisson process on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
To match with the main theorem, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
we define the arctic curve

g_{t}(r)=2\sqrt{rt},

which is the deterministic approximation of
d_{P}(0,0;r,t)
.
We now define scaling parameters in terms of the arctic curve
g=g_{t}
and its derivatives
g^{\prime},g^{\prime\prime}
taken in the variable
r
at the point
r=t
:

\tau_{t}^{3}=\frac{2g^{\prime}}{g^{\prime\prime 2}}=8t^{2},\qquad\chi_{t}^{3}=\frac{g^{\prime 2}}{-2g^{\prime\prime}}=t.

Using these definitions, let
h:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be given by
h(x+y,y)=2y+x
and let
\mathcal{K}_{t}
be the pullback of
\chi_{t}^{-1}(d_{P}-h)
under the linear map
L_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
with matrix

A_{t}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\tau_{t}&t\\
0&t\end{array}\right].

Then for any sequence
t_{n}\to\infty
and any finite or countable union
S=\bigcup S_{s_{i},t_{i}}
, there is a coupling of
\mathcal{K}_{t_{n}}
and
\mathcal{L}
satisfying multi-time convergence on
S
, hypograph convergence, and geodesic convergence off holes.

We have stated Corollary
13.6
for Poisson last passage percolation on square boxes of increasing area. Convergence results for more general rectangular boxes of increasing area follow from scale invariance of Poisson processes.

13.1.6
The Seppäläinen-Johansson model

The Seppäläinen-Johansson model is the only model we consider that is not a degeneration of geometric last passage percolation.
We recall the setup of Example
1.2
. Let
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be the lattice where
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\times\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is a directed edge if
y-x\in\{(1,0),(0,-1)\}
. Fix a parameter
p\in(0,1)
, and let
d_{p}
be the directed metric of positive sign on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
induced by the collection of independent weights
M^{p}
, where

•

M^{p}(x,y)=0
if
y-x=(-1,0)
,

•

M^{p}(x,y)
is a Bernoulli random variable of mean
p
if
y-x=(1,0)
.

The Seppäläinen-Johansson model
d_{p}
can be reinterpreted as a line last passage metric. Given the weight function
M^{p}
, define functions
M^{p}_{i}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z},i\in\mathbb{Z}
by setting

M^{p}_{i}(0)=0,\qquad M^{p}_{i}(n)-M^{p}_{i}(m)=n-m-\sum_{j=m}^{n-1}M^{p}((j,i),(j+1,i)).

Extend each
M^{p}_{i}
to a function from
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{Z}
by letting it be linear on every interval
[n,n+1],n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Note that each function
M_{i}
is an independent random walk with Bernoulli steps of mean
1-p
.
Let
D_{1-p}
be the line last passage metric associated to
M^{p}
.
By construction, for any
(x,n),(y,m)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
we have

d_{p}((x,n),(y,m))=(y-x)-D_{1-p}((x,n),(y,m)).

(13.5)

We can use this formula to extend
d_{p}
to a metric on all of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, the space where
D_{1-p}
is naturally defined.

Theorem 13.7
.

For
