p\in(0,1)
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
set
\lambda=(1-p)/p
and
\rho=m/n
.
Define the arctic curve

g_{n,\lambda}(m)=\frac{(\sqrt{m\lambda}-\sqrt{n})^{2}}{1+\lambda}\mathbf{1}(\rho>1/\lambda),

which is the deterministic approximation of the value
d_{p}((0,n),(m,1))
. Consider sequences
p_{n}\in(0,1),m_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\rho_{n}>1/\lambda_{n}
for all
n
.
We define scaling parameters in terms of
g=g_{n,\lambda_{n}}
and its derivatives
g^{\prime}
,
g^{\prime\prime}
evaluated at the point
m_{n}
:

n\alpha_{n}=g\qquad\beta_{n}=g^{\prime},\qquad\tau_{n}^{3}=\frac{2g^{\prime}(1-g^{\prime})}{(g^{\prime\prime})^{2}},\qquad\chi_{n}^{3}=\frac{n[g^{\prime}(1-g^{\prime})]^{2}}{-2g^{\prime\prime}}.

Define
h_{n}:\mathbb{R}\times(\mathbb{Z}/2)\to\mathbb{R}
by
h_{n}(m_{n}y+x,-y)=\alpha_{n}y+\beta_{n}x
, and let
\mathcal{K}_{n}
be the pullback onto
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of the directed metric
\chi_{n}^{-1}(d_{p_{n}}-h_{n})
under the map
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}}\circ L_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}
, where
L_{n}
is the linear map with matrix

A=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\tau_{n}&\rho_{n}\\
0&n\end{array}\right].

Note that unlike
d_{P_{n}}
,
\mathcal{K}_{n}
is a directed metric of
negative sign
since
\chi_{n}<0
.

Now suppose that
\chi_{n}\to-\infty
with
n
. Then for any finite or countable union
S=\bigcup S_{s_{i},t_{i}}
, there is a coupling of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}
satisfying multi-time convergence on
S
, hypograph convergence, and geodesic convergence off holes.

Note that the condition that
\chi_{n}\to-\infty
is always satisfied if
\rho,\lambda
are both fixed. The proof of Theorem
13.7
is similar to the proof of the main theorem for general models, Theorem
12.1
.

Proof.

We can equivalently work with the last passage metrics
D_{1-p_{n}}
defined by the formula (
13.5
). These fit better into the framework of the paper.

We can alternately think of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
as the pullback onto
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of the directed metric
|\chi_{n}|^{-1}(D_{1-p_{n}}-H_{n})
under the map
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}}\circ L_{n}
, where
H_{n}(a,b)=a-h_{n}(a,b)
. We will use this to check that
\mathcal{K}_{n}
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
10.3
/Remark
10.4
, which will imply multi-time convergence on
S
and hypograph convergence. Geodesic convergence off holes will then follow from Theorem
8.7
as in Step 2 of the proof of Theorem
12.1
.

In the line environments
M^{p_{n}}
, the multi-point last passage differences

A^{n}_{k}(y):=|\chi_{n}|^{-1}\Big(M^{p_{n}}[(0,n)\to_{\Delta k}(\rho_{n}n+\tau_{n}y,1)]-(\rho_{n}-\alpha_{n})n-(1-\beta_{n})\tau_{n}y\Big)

converge compactly to the Airy line ensemble
\mathfrak{A}
for any sequence of parameters
p_{n},\rho_{n}
such that the corresponding
\chi_{n}\to-\infty
. This is Corollary 6.6 in
Dauvergne et al. 2019
. Therefore by Theorem
4.4
, for any
t\in\mathbb{R},s>0
, the function
\mathcal{K}_{n}(\cdot,t;\cdot,t+s^{3})
converges in
\mathcal{E}_{*}(S_{t,t+s^{3}})
to an Airy sheet of scale
s
. Moreover, Corollary 6.6 in
Dauvergne et al. 2019
also implies that this same convergence holds if
t,s
are replaced by convergent sequences
t_{n}\to t,s_{n}\to s>0
. In particular, the sequence
\mathcal{K}_{n}
also satisfies the mobile Tracy-Widom convergence condition in Theorem
10.3
, so
\mathcal{K}_{n}
satisfies all the conditions of that theorem.
∎

13.2
Graph convergence, exponential tightness and geodesic convergence everywhere

The moment bounds necessary to prove graph convergence and geodesic convergence everywhere are not known for all of the models discussed above. However, they are available for geometric last passage percolation with a fixed height-to-width ratio and parameter, and for exponential, Brownian, and planar Poisson last passage percolation. In these models, stronger bounds also allow us to prove exponential tightness

Theorem 13.8
.

1.

(Geometric) Let the sequence of environments
\mathcal{K}_{n}
be either as in Theorem
13.1
where
\gamma=\gamma_{n}
and
m_{n}={\left\lfloor\rho n\right\rfloor}
for some fixed constants
\rho
and
\gamma
that do not depend on
n
.

2.

(Exponential) Let
\mathcal{K}_{n}
be as in Corollary
13.2
for some sequence
m_{n}={\left\lfloor\rho n\right\rfloor}
.

3.

(Poisson) Let
\mathcal{K}_{n}:=\mathcal{K}_{t_{n}}
be as in Corollary
13.6
.

4.

(Brownian) Let
\mathcal{K}_{n}
be as in Corollary
13.4
.

In all four cases, there is a coupling of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}
satisfying graph convergence and geodesic convergence everywhere. Moreover, in cases
1
and
2
we can couple
\mathcal{K}_{n},\mathcal{L}
so that for some
a>1
, for every compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}a^{[\sup_{K}(\mathcal{K}_{n}-\mathcal{L})^{-}]^{3}}+\mathbb{E}a^{\sup_{K}(\mathcal{K}_{n}-\mathcal{L})^{+}}=2.

(13.6)

In case
3
, we can couple
\mathcal{K}_{n},\mathcal{L}
so that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}a^{[\sup_{K}(\mathcal{K}_{n}-\mathcal{L})^{-}]^{3}}+\mathbb{E}a^{[\sup_{K}(\mathcal{K}_{n}-\mathcal{L})^{+}]^{3/2}}=2,

(13.7)

and in case
4
, we can couple
\mathcal{K}_{n},\mathcal{L}
so that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}a^{[\sup_{K}(\mathcal{K}_{n}-\mathcal{L})^{-}]^{3/4}}+\mathbb{E}a^{[\sup_{K}(\mathcal{K}_{n}-\mathcal{L})^{+}]^{3/2}}=2.

(13.8)

Proof of Theorem
13.8
.1.

When
X
is a geometric environment with fixed
\gamma,\rho
, by the proof of Theorem
13.1
, we are in the setting of Theorem
12.2
. Given this, to prove the uniform tail bounds in (
13.6
), it suffices to check the conditions of Theorems
12.6
and
12.7
with
\mathfrak{p}=1
and
\mathfrak{l}=3

and then appeal to those theorems. Indeed, if the condition in Theorem
12.7
holds then so does the condition (
12.20
), so in some coupling
\mathcal{K}_{n}\to\mathcal{L}
in the sense of graph convergence and geodesic convergence everywhere. Moreover, in this coupling

\sup_{K}(\mathcal{K}_{n}-\mathcal{L})^{+},\sup_{K}(\mathcal{K}_{n}-\mathcal{L})^{-}\to 0

almost surely. Theorems
12.6
and
12.7
imply the uniform integrability of
a^{[\sup_{K}(\mathcal{K}_{n}-\mathcal{L})^{+}]}
and
a^{[\sup_{K}(\mathcal{K}_{n}-\mathcal{L})^{-}]^{3}}
for small enough
a>1
,
so the right side of (
13.6
) converges to
2
for such
a
.

There exists
a>1
such that for any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, and all large enough
n\in\mathbb{N},
we have the upper tail bound

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)>m\right)\leq c_{K}a^{-m},

(13.9)

for all
m>0
. See, for example, Proposition 2.2 in
Ledoux 2007
for this bound.

By equation (1.21) in
Baik et al. 2001
, there exists
a>1,\delta>0
such that for any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, for large enough
n
and all
m\leq\delta n^{2/3}
and
u\in K
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)\leq-m\right)\leq c_{K}a^{-m^{3}}.

(13.10)

Since
\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)
is deterministically bounded below on
K
by
dn^{2/3}
for some constant
d
, (
13.10
) extends to all
m
, by possibly changing the constant
a>1
. Combining (
13.9
) and (
13.10
) shows that the condition in Theorem
12.7
holds with
\mathfrak{p}=1
. The inequality (
13.10
) shows that Theorem
12.7
holds with
\mathfrak{l}=3
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
13.8
.2.

The proof here is similar. Again, it suffices to check the conditions of Theorem
12.6
and
12.7
with
\mathfrak{p}=1
and
\mathfrak{l}=3
. By symmetry, we can assume
\rho\geq 1
, i.e.
m_{n}\geq n
for all
n
. The bound (
13.9
) also holds for the exponential environment. This is again shown in
Ledoux 2007
, see the discussion immediately following Proposition 2.2 therein.

Next, by Theorem 2 in
Ledoux and Rider 2010
, there are constants
c,c^{\prime}>0
such that for every
\epsilon\in(0,1]
and
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(X[(1,n)\to(m,1)]\leq(\sqrt{m}+\sqrt{n})^{2}(1-\epsilon)\right)\leq c\exp\left(-c^{\prime}\epsilon^{3}mn\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\wedge\frac{m^{1/2}}{n^{1/2}}\right)\right).

(13.11)

After a change of variables to
\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)
, this gives that there exists
a>1
such that for every compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, for all large enough
n
the bound in (
13.10
) holds.
∎

Proof of Theorem
13.8
.3.

While we cannot appeal directly to Theorems
12.2
,
12.6
, or
12.7
directly since the Poisson environment is not defined on a lattice, the proofs go through almost verbatim with the obvious changes. We sketch what is needed here.

Starting from Corollary
13.6
, we can conclude graph convergence and the lower tail in Theorem
12.7
via the chaining statements in Proposition
11.3
and Corollary
11.4
. Since the convergence of Poisson last passage percolation in Corollary
13.6
is following a standard
1-2-3
scaling, i.e.
\tau_{t}=8^{2/3}t^{2/3},\chi_{t}=t^{1/3}
, and Poisson last passage values are always nonnegative, we can again apply Proposition
11.3
and Corollary
11.4
with
k(n)={\left\lfloor 3\log_{2}(n)/4\right\rfloor}
. Just as in the lattice case, here the bound on the
\beta_{n,i}
functions follows from the fact that there exists
a>1
such that for all
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{u\in K}\mathbb{E}a^{(\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)^{-})^{3}}<\infty.

(13.12)

Equation (
13.12
) is an immediate corollary of Theorem 1.2 in
Löwe et al. 2002
.

For the upper tail, again, we cannot immediately appeal to the maximal inequality in Lemma
10.6
since
d_{P}
is not a line last passage metric. However,
d_{P}
can be obtained as a uniform limit of vertically compressed line last passage metrics. Indeed, starting with the Poisson process
P
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and a scaling parameter
s>0
, form a collection of Poisson processes
P_{s,i},i\in\mathbb{Z}
on
\mathbb{R}
, by setting

P_{s,i}[a,b]=P([-si,-s(i+1)]\times[a,b])

for all
i
. Let
d^{*}_{P_{s}}
be the pullback of the line last passage metric formed from
P_{s,i},i\in\mathbb{Z}
under the map
(x,y)\mapsto(x,-y/s)
. Then as
s\to 0
,
d^{*}_{P_{s}}\to d_{P}
compactly on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
a.s., and so by taking a limit, the maximal inequality in Lemma
10.6
holds for
d_{P}
. Therefore Corollary
10.8

holds for
\mathcal{K}_{n}
, and so we can pass through the proof of Theorem
12.6
. The required exponential tail condition follows from (
13.12
) and the corresponding upper tail bound from
Löwe and Merkl 2001
, Theorem 1.3, which shows that
there exists
a>1
such that for all
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{u\in K}\mathbb{E}a^{(\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)^{+})^{3/2}}<\infty.\qed

Proof of Theorem
13.8
.4.

The graph convergence in the Brownian case is the main result of
Dauvergne et al. 2018
, see Theorem 1.5 in that paper. Geodesic convergence everywhere is stronger than the type of geodesic convergence proven in Theorem 1.8 of
Dauvergne et al. 2018
. It follows deterministically from graph convergence by Theorem
8.5
, as in the proof of Theorem
12.2
.

The exponential tightness bound on
(\mathcal{K}_{n}-\mathcal{L})^{-}
also follows from Theorem 1.5 in
Dauvergne et al. 2018
. For the upper bound, as in the proof of Theorem
12.6
we can just appeal to Corollary
10.8
, since this corollary is stated in the generality of line last passage models. The required compressed exponential moment bound on
|\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)|
follows in this case from Theorem 1 in
Ledoux and Rider 2010
, which together with standard large deviation bounds on
|\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)|
(e.g. see display (1.4) in in
Ledoux and Rider 2010
), gives that there exists
a>1
such that for every compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}a^{\sup_{K}|\mathcal{K}_{n}-\mathcal{L}|^{3/2}}<\infty.\qed

Remark 13.9
.

All exponential tail estimates in Theorem
13.8
are optimal up to the value of
a
, except for the bound on
(\mathcal{K}_{n}-\mathcal{L})^{-}
in the Brownian case. Optimal tail estimates could be obtained by applying the ideas of Theorem
12.7
and tail bounds from
Ledoux and Rider 2010
. A few modifications would need to be made to resolve the fact that the Brownian landscapes
\mathcal{K}_{n}
are not deterministically bounded below at any scale. For brevity, we do not pursue this here.

Remark 13.10
.

Since the bound (
13.11
) has no restriction on the ratio of
m/n
, it is strong enough to give graph convergence and geodesic convergence everywhere for any sequence
\mathcal{K}_{n}
as in Corollary
13.2
with
m_{n}
tending to
\infty
. The proof of Theorem
12.2
can be modified to work in this setting.

The bound on
\mathcal{K}_{n}(p^{+}_{i+1},p^{+}_{i})
goes through in the same way. However, the deterministic bound on
\mathcal{K}_{n}(p,p_{k(n)}^{+})
fails when the ratio
m_{n}/n
tends to
0
or
\infty
quickly enough. To deal with this, we must replace this deterministic bound with a union bound over last passage values for all possible starting lattice points in a compact set. At a fine scale, the cost of taking the union bound is outweighed by the exponential tails in (
13.10
).

Remark 13.11

(Translation to Theorems
1.7
and
1.8
)
.

In order to stay in the world of directed metrics after rescaling, the setup in this section looks slightly different than the simpler setup in Theorems
1.7
and
1.8
. It is nonetheless easy to see that those statements are equivalent to the ones in this section. We give a detailed example of how to see this for the case of the geometric environment with fixed slope
\rho
and mean
\gamma
. Let
\sigma_{n}=n^{1/3}
, set
v=(\rho,-1),u=(\tau_{n},0)
, and let
\mathcal{K}_{n},\tau_{n},\chi_{n},\alpha,\beta
be as in Theorem
13.1
for
m_{n}:=\rho n
. Note that it is not important that
m_{n}
be an integer for the purposes of applying Theorem
13.1
, that
\alpha,\beta
do not change with
n
, and that
\chi_{n}=\chi n^{1/3},\tau_{n}=\tau n^{2/3}
for fixed
\chi,\tau
.

Let
d
denote the last passage metric on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cup E
coming from an array of i.i.d. geometric random variables
X_{\gamma}
, and let
h
be the additive metric on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cup E
satisfying
h(\rho t+x,-t)=\alpha t+\beta x
for
(\rho t+x,-t)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
h(e_{1},e_{2})=e_{1}
on edges. For
(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we rewrite the linear scaling map
L_{n}
as
L_{n}(x,s)=x\sigma_{n}^{2}u+s\sigma_{n}^{3}v
. Now let

\displaystyle(p)_{n}

\displaystyle=({\left\lfloor(L_{n}p)_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor(L_{n}p)_{2}\right\rfloor}),\quad{\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor}_{n}=L_{n}^{-1}({\left\lfloor(L_{n}p)_{1}\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor(L_{n}p)_{2}\right\rfloor}),\quad\;\text{and}\;

\displaystyle d_{n}(x,s;y,t)

\displaystyle=d((x,s)_{n},(y,t)_{n})-\left(\alpha_{n}\sigma_{n}^{3}(t-s)+\beta_{n}\tau_{n}\sigma_{n}^{2}(y-x)\right).

If we restrict
d_{n}
to
{\left\lfloor\mathbb{R}^{2}\right\rfloor}_{n}\times{\left\lfloor\mathbb{R}^{2}\right\rfloor}_{n}
, then
d_{n}
is exactly the pullback of the metric
d+h
under the map
f_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\circ L_{n}
. That is,
\chi_{n}^{-1}d_{n}=\mathcal{K}_{n}
on this set. Moreover, from the scaling it is easy to see that
d_{n}(p,q)-d_{n}({\left\lfloor p\right\rfloor}_{n},{\left\lfloor q\right\rfloor}_{n})=O(1)
, uniformly in
p,q,
and
n
. Combining these facts with Theorem
13.8
gives that
\chi_{n}^{-1}d_{n}\to\mathcal{L}
compactly in some coupling, and that rescaled geodesics converge everywhere, as is necessary for Theorem
1.8
.

We finish this subsection with a different way of rescaling planar Poisson last passage percolation where we first rotate the plane by
\pi/4
and then scale by a diagonal transformation. Rotated planar Poisson last passage percolation is precisely the polynuclear growth model of
Prähofer and Spohn 2002
. Note that
Prähofer and Spohn 2002
work with Poisson processes of intensity
2
, whereas we work with Poisson processes of intensity
1
.
The prelimiting models satisfies a natural reflection invariance that passes to
\mathcal{L}
, see Proposition
14.1
.

Corollary 13.12

(Convergence of polynuclear growth)
.

With all notation and assumptions as in Corollary
13.6
, let

\mathcal{L}_{t}
be the pullback of
\chi_{t}^{-1}(d_{P}-d_{h})
under the linear map
M_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
with matrix

B_{t}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1/\sqrt{2}&1/\sqrt{2}\\
-1/\sqrt{2}&1/\sqrt{2}\end{array}\right]\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\sqrt{2}\tau_{t}&0\\
0&\sqrt{2}t\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\tau_{t}&t\\
-\tau_{t}&t\end{array}\right].

Then for any sequence
t_{n}\to\infty
, there is a coupling of
\mathcal{L}_{t_{n}}
and
\mathcal{L}
satisfying graph convergence and geodesic convergence everywhere.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{K}_{t},A_{t}
be as in Corollary
13.6
. By Theorem
13.8
, we consider a coupling of
\mathcal{K}_{t_{n}},\mathcal{L}
satisfying graph convergence and geodesic convergence off holes. The environment
\mathcal{L}_{t}
is the pullback of
\mathcal{K}_{t}
under the map

A_{t}^{-1}B_{t}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
-\tau_{t}/t&1\end{array}\right].

Since
\tau_{t}/t\to 0
as
t\to\infty
,
A_{t}^{-1}B_{t}
converges to the identity as
t\to\infty
. Since
\mathcal{L}_{t_{n}}
is the pullback of
\mathcal{K}_{t_{n}}
under a sequence of maps converging to the identity, we have that
\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{L}_{t_{n}}\to\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{L}
in the specified coupling. Moreover, geodesics in
\mathcal{L}_{t_{n}}
are simply pullbacks of geodesics in
\mathcal{K}_{t_{n}}
by Lemma
5.9
, so
\mathcal{L}_{t_{n}}
inherits geodesic convergence everywhere from
\mathcal{K}_{t_{n}}
.
∎

13.3
A distinction between hypograph and graph convergence

We end this section by modifying exponential last passage percolation to give an example of a sequence of i.i.d. lattice environments
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
that can be coupled with
\mathcal{L}
to satisfy multi-time convergence, hypograph convergence, and geodesic convergence off holes, but not graph convergence.

Example 13.13
.

Let
X,\mathcal{K}_{n}
be as in Corollary
13.2
with
m_{n}=n
. Define a new sequence of i.i.d. environments
\tilde{X}_{n}
so that

\tilde{X}_{n}(u)=\begin{cases}X(u),\qquad&\text{ with probability }1-n^{-5/3}\\
-n^{1/3},\qquad&\text{ with probability }n^{-5/3},\end{cases}

and define
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
from
\tilde{X}_{n}
in the same way that
\mathcal{K}_{n}
was defined from
X
.
Then for any finite or countable union
S=\bigcup S_{s_{i},t_{i}}
, there is a coupling of
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}
satisfying multi-time convergence on
S
, hypograph convergence, and geodesic convergence off holes. However,
\mathfrak{g}\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
does not converge in distribution to
\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{L}
.

To see why Example
13.13
works, first observe that there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{(x,s;y,t)\in[0,1]^{2}\times\{0,2\}}\mathcal{K}_{n}(x,s;y,t)-\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}(x,s;y,t)\leq-2^{-4/3}\right)>c.

(13.13)

This is because there are
O(n^{5/3})
squares in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
that get sent to the box
[0,1]^{2}
after rescaling to get
\mathcal{K}_{n}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
, and so with nonvanishing probability, there is some box
u
in this set with
\tilde{X}_{n}(u)=-n^{1/3}<0\leq X(u)
. Since
X
dominates
\tilde{X}_{n}
everywhere the bound on
\mathcal{K}_{n}-\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
above follows since the scaling parameter
\chi_{n}=2^{4/3}n^{1/3}
for this model.

Since
\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{K}_{n}
converges in distribution to
\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{L}
by Theorem
13.8
and
\mathcal{K}_{n}\geq\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
, (
13.13
) implies that
\mathfrak{g}\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
cannot also converge in distribution to
\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{L}
. On the other hand, holes where
X(u)\neq\tilde{X}_{n}(u)
are rare. In particular,
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}(u)
and
\mathcal{K}_{n}(u)
will always be close (and typically will be equal) for all points
u=(u_{1};u_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
such that neither endpoint landed on a lattice point where
\mathcal{K}_{n}
disagreed with
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
. It is not difficult to make this argument quantitative, and therefore show that
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
satisfies all the conditions of Theorem
12.1
. Therefore there is a coupling of
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}
satisfying multi-time convergence on
S
, hypograph convergence, and geodesic convergence off holes.

Note that the environments
\tilde{\mathcal{K}}_{n}
satisfy the moment condition (
12.13
) when
\mathbb{E}\min(0,A^{n}_{1}(y))^{5+\delta}
is replaced by the fifth moment
\mathbb{E}\min(0,A^{n}_{1}(y))^{5}
.

14
Consequences

In this section, we prove consequences of the convergence statements in Section
13
.

14.1
Symmetries of
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{S}

The next proposition is a restatement of Proposition
1.23
.

Proposition 14.1
.

As a function of
x,s;y,
and
t
, the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
satisfies

\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(-x,s;-y,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(y,-t;x,-s),

(14.1)

and as a function of
x
and
y
, the Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}
satisfies

\mathcal{S}(x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}(-x,-y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}(y,x).

(14.2)

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{t}
be as in Corollary
13.12
. We have the symmetries

\mathcal{L}_{t}(x,s;y,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}_{t}(-x,s;-y,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}_{t}(y,-t;x,-s).

These follow from reflection invariance of a planar rate-
1
Poisson process across the horizontal and vertical axes. The distributional equalities in (
14.1
) follow immediately from these by Corollary
13.12
. The distributional equalities in (
14.2
) are equivalent to the distributional equalities in (
14.1
) when we fix
s=0,t=1
.
∎

The next theorem is a restatement of Theorem
1.21
.

Theorem 14.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be an Airy line ensemble, and let
\mathfrak{A}^{*}(x)=\mathfrak{A}(-x).
For
(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}\times\mathbb{Q}^{2}
define

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(x,y,z)&=\mathfrak{A}[(-\sqrt{k/(2x)},k)\to(y,1)]-\mathfrak{A}[(-\sqrt{k/(2x)},k)\to(z,1)],\\
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(-x,y,z)&=\mathfrak{A}^{*}[(-\sqrt{k/(2x)},k)\to(-y,1)]-\mathfrak{A}^{*}[(-\sqrt{k/(2x)},k)\to(-z,1)],\end{split}

(14.3)

for all sufficiently large
k
. This is well-defined since the right hand sides above stabilize almost surely as
k\to\infty
. For
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Q}\setminus\{0\}\times\mathbb{Q}
, define

\mathcal{S}(x,y)=\mathbb{E}\mathfrak{A}_{1}(0)+\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{2n+1}\sum_{z=-n}^{n}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(x,y,z)-(z-x)^{2},

(14.4)

Finally, extend
\mathcal{S}
to all of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by continuity.
Then
\mathcal{S}
is an Airy sheet and
\mathcal{S}(0,\cdot)=\mathfrak{A}_{1}(\cdot)
.

Proof.

To prove Theorem
14.2
, we will first construct a coupling between an Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathfrak{A}
satisfying (
14.3
) with
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(x,y,z)
replaced by
\mathcal{S}(x,y)-\mathcal{S}(x,z)
.

We work in the context of Theorem
13.1
. Let
X
be an environment of i.i.d. geometric random variables of mean
\gamma_{n}=1
and
m_{n}=n
. With these choices, there exist
\alpha,\beta,\tau,\chi
such that
\alpha=\alpha_{n},\beta=\beta_{n},\tau_{n}=\tau n^{2/3}
and
\chi_{n}=\chi n^{1/3}
for all
n
. Rather than work with the directed metrics
\mathcal{K}_{n}
as in Theorem
13.1
, we will work with the prelimit
\mathcal{L}_{n,k}
as defined in Corollary
12.4
. That is, letting
(x,s)_{n}=({\left\lfloor ns+\tau n^{2/3}x\right\rfloor},{\left\lfloor-ns\right\rfloor})
, we set

\mathcal{L}_{n,\Delta k}(x,s;y,t)=\frac{X[(x,s)_{n}\to_{\Delta k}(y,t)_{n}]-\beta(t-s)n-\alpha\tau(y-x)n^{2/3}}{\chi n^{1/3}}.

(14.5)

Also let
\tilde{X}(a,b)=X(-b,-a)
, and let
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{n,k}
be defined from
\tilde{X}
in the same way that
\mathcal{L}_{n,k}
was defined from
X
. All the assumptions of Corollary
12.4
hold for the environments
X
and
\tilde{X}
.

By Corollary
12.4
,
\mathcal{L}_{n,1},\mathcal{L}_{n,\cdot}(0,0;\cdot,1)
converge jointly to
\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{A}
, where convergence to
\mathcal{L}
is in the topology of convergence of graphs, and convergence to
\mathfrak{A}
is in the product of Skorokhod topologies. Moreover,
\mathcal{L}(\cdot,0;\cdot,1)
is coupled to
\mathfrak{A}
as in Definition
3.3
. The same convergence holds for
\tilde{\mathcal{L}},\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\cdot}(0,0;\cdot,1)
, so we can find a joint subsequential limit

\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{A},\tilde{\mathcal{L}},\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}
of

\mathcal{L}_{n,1},\qquad\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{n,1},\qquad\mathcal{L}_{n,\cdot}(0,0;\cdot,1),\qquad\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\cdot}(0,0;\cdot,1).

The coupling between
\mathcal{L}_{n,1}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{n,1}
and the scaling in (
14.5
) guarantees that

\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)=\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(-x,s;-y,t).

(14.6)

Moreover, the coupling between
\mathcal{L},\mathfrak{A},
and
\tilde{\mathcal{L}},\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}
guarantees that with
\mathcal{S}(\cdot,\cdot)=\mathcal{L}(\cdot,0;,\cdot,1)
, for all
(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{Q}^{+}\times\mathbb{Q}^{2}
, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}(x,y)-\mathcal{S}(x,z)&=\mathfrak{A}[(-\sqrt{k/(2x)},k)\to(y,1)]-\mathfrak{A}[(-\sqrt{k/(2x)},k)\to(z,1)],\\
\mathcal{S}(-x,y)-\mathcal{S}(-x,z)&=\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}[(-\sqrt{k/(2x)},k)\to(-y,1)]-\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}[(-\sqrt{k/(2x)},k)\to(-z,1)],\end{split}

(14.7)

for all sufficiently large
k
. Next we show that
\mathfrak{A}^{*}(x):=\mathfrak{A}(-x)
is equal to
\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

By continuity of
\mathfrak{A}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}
, and the fact that
\mathfrak{A}(-x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}(x)
for all
x
, to prove this it is enough to show that for any fixed
x>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{i}(-x)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathfrak{A}_{i}(x)\qquad\;\text{and}\;\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{i}(x)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathfrak{A}_{i}(-x)\qquad\text{ almost surely}.

(14.8)

We will prove the first of these inequalities. The second follows by a symmetric argument.
In the prelimit, the right hand side of the first inequality in (
14.8
) is a rescaled and centered multi-point last passage value in
X
from

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}=((0,1-k),\dots,(0,0))

to

\displaystyle\mathbf{q}=((n+{\left\lfloor\tau n^{2/3}x\right\rfloor},-n),\dots,(n+{\left\lfloor\tau n^{2/3}x\right\rfloor},-n+k-1),

and the left hand side of the first inequality in (
14.8
) is a rescaled and centered multi-point last passage value in
X
from
\mathbf{p}
to

\mathbf{q}^{\prime}=((n,-n+{\left\lfloor\tau n^{2/3}x\right\rfloor}),\dots(n,-n+{\left\lfloor\tau n^{2/3}x\right\rfloor}+k-1)).

Also let
\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime}
denote the vector
\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
with all components shifted by
(1,0)
. Since
x>0
, we have the triangle inequality

X[\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{q}^{\prime}]+X[\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathbf{q}]\leq X[\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{q}],

(14.9)

as any
k
-tuples of disjoint paths from
\mathbf{p}\to\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{q}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathbf{q}^{\prime}
can be concatenated to give a
k
-tuple of disjoint paths from
\mathbf{p}
to
\mathbf{q}
. We can rewrite (
14.9
) in terms of
\mathcal{L}_{n,k},\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{n,k}
to get that

Y_{n,k}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\Delta k}(0,0;-x,1)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}_{n,\Delta k}(0,0;x,1)

(14.10)

for some error term
Y_{n,k}
given by

Y_{n,k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}_{n,\Delta k}(x+O(kn^{-2/3}),1-\tau n^{2/3}x+O(k/n);x,1).

The inequality (
14.10
) implies (
14.8
) if we can show that the error term
Y_{n,k}
converges to
0
. By translation invariance of
X
, we can write

Y_{n,k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}O((\ell_{n}/n)^{1/3})\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}_{\ell_{n},\Delta k}(0,0;z_{n},1),

where the sequence
z_{n}
is bounded and
\ell_{n}=\Theta(n^{2/3})
. Since
\mathcal{L}_{\ell_{n},k}(0,0;\cdot,1)
converges to the parabolic Airy line ensemble and
\ell_{n}/n\to 0
,
Y_{n,k}\to 0
in distribution. This yields (
14.8
), and hence
\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathfrak{A}^{*}
.

We can now use (
14.3
) to show that the construction in the theorem is well-defined and returns the Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}
. Each of the Airy processes
y\mapsto\mathcal{S}(x,t)=\mathfrak{A}_{1}(t)+t^{2}
is strong mixing, see equation (5.15) in
Prähofer and Spohn 2002
and
Corwin and Sun 2014
, Proposition 1.13. Therefore with
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
defined as in (
14.3
) from
\mathfrak{A}
, by (
14.7
) and the pointwise ergodic theorem, the right hand side of (
14.4
) returns the Airy sheet value
\mathcal{S}(x,y)
almost surely. Extending continuously to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
returns the entire Airy sheet
\mathcal{S}
. This Airy sheet satisfies
\mathcal{S}(0,\cdot)=\mathfrak{A}_{1}(\cdot)
by Definition
3.3
(ii).
∎

14.2
Directed geodesics and longest increasing subsequences

Recall from Remark
8.4
that there is almost surely a unique
\mathcal{L}
-geodesic
\Gamma
from
(0,0)
to
(0,1)
. We can represent
\Gamma
as
\{(\Pi(t),t):t\in[0,1]\}
for some random continuous function
\Pi:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
. We call
\Pi
a
directed geodesic
. The almost surely unique
\mathcal{L}
-geodesic between any other pair of points in the plane is equal in distribution to a rescaled, sheared, version of
\Pi
. In this section, we show that
\Pi
is the scaling limit of the longest increasing subsequence in a uniform permutation.

Let
\sigma
be an element of the symmetric group
S_{n}
. An
increasing subsequence
in
\sigma
is a subsequence
I=\{i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\}\subset\{1,\dots n\}
such that

\sigma(i_{1})<\sigma(i_{2})<\dots<\sigma(i_{k}).

We think of each subsequence
I
as a function from
\{1,\dots,k\}
to
\mathbb{N}
with
I(m)=i_{m}
.
A basic observation is that the length of the
longest increasing subsequence
can be related to a last passage value: if we let

P=\{(i,\sigma(i)):i\in\{1,\dots,n\}\},

(14.11)

and define
d_{P}
as in Definition
13.5
, then

d_{P}((0,0);(n,n))

is the largest length of any increasing subsequence in
\sigma
.

When
\sigma
is a uniformly chosen permutation, then the point process
P
is closely related to a Poisson process in the plane, and so we can use Corollary
13.6
and the connection above to show that the scaling limit of the longest increasing subsequence in a uniform permutation is the directed geodesic.

Theorem 14.3
.

Let
\sigma_{n}\in S_{n}
be a uniformly chosen permutation. Let
I_{n},J_{n}
be arbitrary maximal length increasing subsequences in
\sigma_{n}
. Then

\tilde{I}_{n}(x):=\frac{I_{n}(\lfloor 2x\sqrt{n}\rfloor)-nx}{2n^{5/6}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\Pi(x)\qquad\;\text{and}\;\qquad\max_{1\leq i\leq 2\sqrt{n}}\frac{|I_{n}(i)-J_{n}(i)|}{n^{5/6}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}0,

where
\Pi
is the directed geodesic, the underlying topology in the first convergence is the Skorokhod topology on cadlag functions from
[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
, and
I_{n}(i):=n
for
i\geq|I_{n}|
.

We will prove Theorem
14.3
in tandem with a closely related theorem. This can be thought of as a Kolmogorov-Smirnov test for longest increasing subsequences.

Theorem 14.4
.

Let
\{X(i):i\in\mathbb{N}\}
be a sequence of i.i.d. random variables with a continuous CDF
F
. For each
n
, let
I_{n}=\{I_{n,1}<\dots<I_{n,K_{n}}\}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
be a subsequence of maximal length satisfying

X(I_{n,1})<\dots<X(I_{n,K_{n}}).

Define the empirical distribution function
F_{n}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
by

F_{n}(x)=\frac{1}{K_{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{K_{n}}\mathbf{1}(X(I_{n,j})\leq x).

Then

\tilde{F}_{n}:=n^{1/6}\left(F_{n}-F\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\Pi\circ F

where
\Pi
is the directed geodesic. Here the underlying topology is the Skorokhod topology on cadlag functions from
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, if
\tilde{F}_{n}^{\prime}
is defined analogously from a different maximal length subsequence
J_{n}
, then

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\tilde{F}_{n}(x)-\tilde{F}_{n}^{\prime}(x)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}0\qquad\;\text{as}\;n\to\infty.

Note that the Skorokhod convergence in Theorems
14.3
and
14.4
is equivalent of the existence of couplings such that
\tilde{I}_{n}\to\Pi
uniformly, and
\tilde{F}_{n}\to\Pi\circ F
uniformly. The key step in proving Theorems
14.3
and
14.4
is the following convergence result for planar Poisson last passage percolation.

Proposition 14.5
.

Let
P
be a Poisson process on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and define

T_{n}=\inf\{t\geq 0:P_{n}[0,T_{n}]\times[0,\sqrt{n}]\geq n\}

Let
\Gamma_{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be any sequence of
d_{P}
-geodesics from
(0,0)
to
(T_{n},\sqrt{n})
such that

\Gamma_{n}=\{(E_{n}(x),x):x\in[0,\sqrt{n}]\}

for some continuous bijection
E_{n}:[0,\sqrt{n}]\to[0,T_{n}]
. Also define

G_{n}(x)=d_{P}((0,0),(E_{n}(x),x))\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad G_{n}^{-1}(m)=\inf\{x\in[0,\sqrt{n}]:G_{n}(x)=m\}.

Then

\tilde{G}_{n}(x):=\frac{G_{n}(\sqrt{n}x)-2\sqrt{n}x}{2n^{1/3}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\Pi(x),\;\;\text{and}\;\;\tilde{E}_{n}(x):=\frac{E_{n}\circ G_{n}^{-1}({\left\lfloor 2\sqrt{n}x\right\rfloor})-\sqrt{n}x}{2n^{1/3}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\Pi(x),

(14.12)

where
\Pi
is the directed geodesic. Here the underlying topology is the Skorokhod topology on cadlag functions from
[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
.
To ensure that
\tilde{E}_{n}
is well-defined for all
x
, we set
G_{n}^{-1}(y)=\sqrt{n}
for
y>d_{P}((0,0),(\sqrt{n},T_{n}))
.

Moreover, if
\Gamma_{n}^{\prime}
is a different sequence of geodesics from
(0,0)
to
(\sqrt{n},T_{n})
, and
\tilde{G}_{n}^{\prime},\tilde{E}_{n}^{\prime}
were formed from
\Gamma_{n}^{\prime}
in the same way that
\tilde{G}_{n},\tilde{E}_{n}
were formed from
\Gamma_{n}
, then

\sup_{x\in[0,1]}|\tilde{G}_{n}(x)-\tilde{G}_{n}^{\prime}(x)|+|\tilde{E}_{n}(x)-\tilde{E}_{n}^{\prime}(x)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\to}}0

(14.13)

as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

In the proof, we let
\mathcal{K}_{\sqrt{n}}
and all related notation be as in Corollary
13.6
. Consider a new coupling of the environments
\mathcal{K}_{\sqrt{n}}
where almost surely,

1.

\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{K}_{\sqrt{n}}\to\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{L}
in
\mathcal{E}_{*}
.

2.

There is a unique
\mathcal{L}
-geodesic
\Gamma
from
(0,0)\to(0,1)
. Moreover, for any
p_{n}\to(0,1)
, any sequence of
\mathcal{K}_{\sqrt{n}}
-geodesics
\Lambda_{n}
from
(0,0)\to p_{n}
converges in the Hausdorff metric to
\Gamma
.

3.

(T_{n}-\sqrt{n})/n^{1/3}\to 0
.

The fact that such a coupling exists follows from Theorem
13.8
.3 for the first two points. The third point follows from elementary tail estimates for Poisson random variables. Now, in this coupling, with the matrices
A_{t}
as in Corollary
13.6
, condition 3 above guarantees that

A_{\sqrt{n}}^{-1}(T_{n},\sqrt{n})\to(0,1).

This convergence, along with condition 2 above implies that

A_{\sqrt{n}}^{-1}\Gamma_{n}=\left\{\left(\frac{E_{n}(\sqrt{n}x)-\sqrt{n}x}{2n^{1/3}},x\right):x\in[0,1]\right\}\to\Gamma.

(14.14)

Moreover, Proposition
8.9
implies that there exists a random constant
C
such that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{u\in A_{\sqrt{n}}^{-1}\Gamma_{n}}|\mathcal{K}_{\sqrt{n}}(0,0;u)|\leq C.

Using that
\mathcal{K}_{\sqrt{n}}
is the pullback under
A_{\sqrt{n}}
of
n^{-1/6}(d_{P}-h)
, this gives that

\displaystyle G_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=h(0,0;E_{n}(x),x)+O(n^{1/6})=x+E_{n}(x)+O(n^{1/6}),

(14.15)

Here the
O(n^{1/6})
is bounded in absolute value by
Cn^{1/6}
for all
x\in[0,\sqrt{n}]
. Therefore

\tilde{G}_{n}(x)=\frac{E_{n}(\sqrt{n}x)-\sqrt{n}x}{2n^{1/3}}+O(n^{-1/6}).

Therefore by (
14.14
) and the equivalence of Hausdorff convergence and uniform convergence to continuous functions,
\tilde{G}_{n}\to\Pi
uniformly a.s. This yields the first convergence in (
14.12
). We turn to the second convergence statement in (
14.12
). In the chosen coupling, the first convergence in (
14.12
) yields

G_{n}(\sqrt{n}x)=2\sqrt{n}x+2n^{1/3}\Pi(x)+o(n^{1/3}),

where
o(n^{1/3})/n^{1/3}\to 0
uniformly a.s.
This implies that
G_{n}^{-1}({\left\lfloor 2\sqrt{n}x\right\rfloor})=\sqrt{n}y_{n}(x)
for some
y_{n}(x)
satisfying

2\sqrt{n}x=2\sqrt{n}y_{n}(x)+2\Pi(y_{n}(x))n^{1/3}+o(n^{1/3}).

(14.16)

Now, (
14.14
) also implies that

E_{n}(\sqrt{n}x)=2\Pi(x)n^{1/3}+\sqrt{n}x+o(n^{1/3}).

(14.17)

Plugging (
14.17
) and
G_{n}^{-1}({\left\lfloor 2\sqrt{n}x\right\rfloor})=\sqrt{n}y_{n}(x)
into the formula for
\tilde{E}_{n}
gives that

\tilde{E}_{n}(x)=\Pi(y_{n}(x))+o(1).

(14.18)

Finally, equation (
14.16
) implies that
|y_{n}(x)-x|=O(n^{-1/6})
, so (
14.18
) and the continuity of
\Pi
proves that
\tilde{E}_{n}\to\Pi
uniformly a.s.

Finally, in this coupling the same uniform a.s. convergences hold with
\tilde{G}_{n}^{\prime},\tilde{E}_{n}^{\prime}
in place of
\tilde{G}_{n},\tilde{E}_{n}
, yielding (
14.13
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
14.3
.

Let
P,T_{n}
be as in the statement of Proposition
14.5
. Let

p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
be the Poisson points in
[0,T_{n}]\times[0,\sqrt{n}]
, listed in order of increasing first coordinate, and let
\sigma\in S_{n}
be the permutation such that the points
p_{\sigma(1)},\dots,p_{\sigma(n)}
are listed in order of increasing second coordinate.

Then
\sigma
is a uniform random permutation in
S_{n}
. Now let
I_{n}\subset\sigma
be a subsequence of maximal length
L_{n}
, and define
\Gamma_{n}\subset[0,T_{n}]\times[0,\sqrt{n}]
by connecting the sequence of points

(0,0),p_{I_{n}(1)},\dots,p_{I_{n}(L_{n})},(T_{n},\sqrt{n})

with straight lines between adjacent points. Then
\Gamma_{n}
is a
d_{P}
-geodesic from
(0,0)
to
(T_{n},\sqrt{n})
. Let
\tilde{E}_{n}
be formed from
\Gamma_{n}
as in Proposition
14.5
. Then

\tilde{E}_{n}(x)=\frac{p_{I({\left\lfloor 2\sqrt{n}x\right\rfloor}),1}-\sqrt{n}x}{2n^{1/3}}.

(14.19)

Here
p_{i,1}
denotes the first coordinate of
p_{i}
.
Now,
\{p_{i,1}:i=1,\dots,n\}
is an exponential random walk, where each exponential random variable has mean
1/\sqrt{n}
. Therefore by Donsker’s theorem, the sequence of random variables

\max_{i\in[1,n]}|p_{i,1}-i/\sqrt{n}|

is tight as
n\to\infty
. Therefore by (
14.19
),

\tilde{E}_{n}(x)=\frac{I({\left\lfloor 2\sqrt{n}x\right\rfloor})-nx}{2n^{5/6}}+O(n^{-5/6}),

so the first convergence in Theorem
14.3
follows from the second convergence in (
14.12
). Similarly, the second convergence in Theorem
14.3
follows from (
14.13
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
14.4
.

We first deal with the case when the
X(i)
are uniformly distributed.
Again, let
P,T_{n}
be as in the statement of Proposition
14.5
, and let
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
be the Poisson points in
[0,T_{n}]\times[0,\sqrt{n}]
, listed in order of increasing first coordinate. Letting
X(i)=p_{i,2}/\sqrt{n}
, we have that
X(1),\dots,X(n)
is a sequence of i.i.d. uniform random variables on
[0,1].

As in the proof of Theorem
14.3
, if we let
I_{n}
be a subsequence of maximal length satisfying

p_{I_{n}(1),2}<\dots<p_{I_{n}(|I_{n}|),2},

then the set
\Gamma_{n}
formed as in the proof of Theorem
14.3
is a
d_{P}
-geodesic from
(0,0)
to
(T_{n},\sqrt{n})
. Moreover, letting
F_{n},K_{n}
be formed in Theorem
14.4
from
X(1),\dots,X(n)
, and
G_{n}
be as in Proposition
14.5
, we have that

G_{n}(\sqrt{n}x)=K_{n}F_{n}(x)

for all
x
. In particular,

\tilde{G}_{n}(x)-\tilde{F}_{n}(x)=G_{n}(\sqrt{n}x)\left(\frac{K_{n}-2\sqrt{n}}{2n^{1/3}K_{n}}\right)=\frac{G_{n}(\sqrt{n}x)}{K_{n}}O(n^{-1/6}),

where in the final equality we have used that the length
K_{n}
of the longest increasing subsequence is
2\sqrt{n}+O(n^{1/6})
. Since
G_{n}(x)\leq K_{n}
for all
x
, the above converges to
0
in probability with
n
, yielding the convergence of
\tilde{F}_{n}
for uniform random variables. The convergence of
|\tilde{F}_{n}-\tilde{F}_{n}^{\prime}|
in uniform norm follows from the analogous statement for
\tilde{G}_{n},\tilde{G}_{n}^{\prime}
in (
14.13
).

Now consider
X(i)
with a general continuous CDF
F
. Define
F^{-1}:(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}
by

F^{-1}(x)=\min\{y\in\mathbb{R}:F(y)=x\}.

Since
F
is continuous, this function is well-defined and satisfies
F\circ F^{-1}(x)=x
for all
x\in(0,1)
and
F^{-1}\circ F(y)\leq y
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
. We can write
X(i)=F^{-1}(U(i))
for a sequence of i.i.d. uniform random variables
U(i)
and for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
we have that

X(i)=F^{-1}(U(i))\leq x\qquad\text{ if and only if }\qquad U(i)\leq F(x).

(14.20)

Now, with notation as in the statement of Theorem
14.4
, let

H_{n}(x)=\frac{1}{K_{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{K_{n}}\mathbf{1}(U(I_{n,i})\leq x),\qquad\;\text{and}\;\qquad\tilde{H}_{n}(x)=n^{1/6}(H_{n}(x)-x).

By (
14.20
), we have that

\tilde{F}_{n}=\tilde{H}_{n}\circ F.

By Theorem
14.4
for uniform random variables on
[0,1]
, we can couple
G_{n}
and
\Pi
so that
\tilde{H}_{n}\to\Pi
uniformly a.s., and hence
\tilde{F}_{n}=\tilde{H}_{n}\circ F\to\Pi\circ F
uniformly a.s. as well, yielding the distributional convergence in the theorem. The convergence of
|\tilde{F}_{n}-\tilde{F}_{n}^{\prime}|
from the corresponding statement for uniform random variables follows similarly.
∎

15
Interfaces

The goal of the remainder of the paper is to move from convergence of last passage percolation to convergence of tasep. Tasep and exponential last passage percolation are related through a well-known transformation, which describes tasep in terms of interfaces in the corresponding last passage metric. Because of this, we first prove general convergence theorems for interfaces. Though interfaces can be defined abstractly, we restrict our attention to planar directed metrics of negative sign with an asymptotic spacetime structure.

First, for a directed metric
d
of negative sign on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, define the
backwards ball of radius
t
centered at a set
S
by

B_{d}(S,t)=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:d(y,x)>t\text{ for some }x\in S\}=\bigcup_{x\in S}B_{d}(x,t).

(15.1)

We write
B_{d}(x,t)=B_{d}(\{x\},t)
. Note that for
t\geq 0
,
x\notin B_{d}(x,t)
: in usual metric spaces, the analogous balls would have nonpositive radius, and so would be empty. Now, suppose that
d
satisfies the positivity condition

d(x,s;x,t)\geq 0\qquad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R},s\leq t.

(15.2)

For such a metric
d
, boundaries of backwards balls are graphs of functions. Indeed, letting
\bar{B}
denote the Euclidean closure, define

I_{d}(S,t)(x)=\sup\{y\in\mathbb{R}:(x,y)\in\bar{B}_{d}(S,t)\},

the
interface of radius
t
centered at
S
. Positivity (
15.2
) implies that
\bar{B}
is the closed hypograph (see Section
7
) of
I_{d}(S,t)
, with the infinities removed:

\bar{B}_{d}(S,t)={\mathfrak{h}}I_{d}(S,t)\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}.

We now move on to proving convergence theorems for interfaces. For the remaining theorems in this section, we will work with the following objects and assumptions.

Assumptions 15.1
.

We have

•

a sequence of directed metrics
d_{n}
of negative sign on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying
d_{n}(x,s;y,t)=-\infty
for
s>t
,

•

a limiting metric
d
which is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\circ}
such that
\mathfrak{g}d_{n}\to\mathfrak{g}d
in
\mathcal{E}_{*}
,

•

scaling parameters
b_{n}>0
and
a_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

b_{n}\to\infty,\quad a_{n}/b_{n}\to 0,

(15.3)

and an additive metric
\ell_{n}
given by

\ell_{n}(p,q)=\ell_{n}(p)-\ell_{n}(q),\qquad\text{ where }\qquad\ell_{n}(x,t)=a_{n}x+b_{n}t

such that
d_{n}+\ell_{n}
satisfies the positivity condition (
15.2
).

Theorem 15.2
.

With the Assumptions
15.1
, define a function
i_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}\to\mathbb{R}
by

i_{n}(y,t;x,s)=b_{n}I_{d_{n}+\ell_{n}}(x,s;b_{n}(s-t))(y)-b_{n}t-a_{n}(x-y).

Then
i_{n}\to d
compactly on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
.
Moreover, letting

\tilde{h}_{n}(y,t;x,g)=b_{n}I_{d_{n}+\ell_{n}}(x,(g-a_{n}x)/b_{n};b_{n}t)(y)+b_{n}t+a_{n}y,

we have that

\tilde{h}_{n}(y,t;x,g)\to d(y,-t;x,0)+g

compactly on
(y,t;x,g)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

It helps with understanding how the scaling works to check that if
d_{n}=0
then
i_{n}=0
. In Section
16
,
\tilde{h}_{n}
will correspond to a rescaled and shifted version of a tasep height function
h_{n}
.

Proof.

By continuity of
d
on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, it suffices to show that for any
u_{n}=(y_{n},t_{n};x_{n},s_{n})\to u=(y,t;x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, that
i_{n}(u_{n})\to d(u)
. To simplify notation in the proof, we let

w_{n}:=I_{d_{n}+\ell_{n}}(x_{n},s_{n};b_{n}(s_{n}-t_{n}))(y_{n}).

Graph convergence of
d_{n}\to d
and continuity of
d
implies that
d_{n}\to d
compactly on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, see Lemma
7.2
. Therefore

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sup_{r\in[t-1,(s+t)/2]}|d_{n}(y_{n},r;x_{n},s_{n})|<\infty.

By the asymptotics in (
15.3
), this implies that

\frac{(d_{n}+\ell_{n})(y_{n},r;x_{n},s_{n})}{b_{n}}\to s-r

uniformly for
r\in[t-1,(s+t)/2]
.
Therefore since
s_{n}\to s
and
t_{n}\to t
, for any
\epsilon>0
we have that

(d_{n}+\ell_{n})(y_{n},t+\epsilon;x_{n},s_{n})<b_{n}(s_{n}-t_{n})<(d_{n}+\ell_{n})(y_{n},t-\epsilon;x_{n},s_{n})

for large enough
n
. This implies that

w_{n}\to t\quad\;\text{as}\;\quad n\to\infty.

(15.4)

Now, for any positive sequence
\epsilon_{n}
, condition (
15.2
) implies that

(d_{n}+\ell_{n})(y_{n},w_{n}+\epsilon_{n};x_{n},s_{n})\leq b_{n}(s_{n}-t_{n})\leq(d_{n}+\ell_{n})(y_{n},w_{n}-\epsilon_{n};x_{n},s_{n}).

(15.5)

Let
\epsilon_{n}>0
be such that
b_{n}\epsilon_{n}\to 0
. Since
d_{n}\to d
compactly on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
and
d
is continuous we have

(d_{n}+\ell_{n})(y_{n},w_{n}+\epsilon_{n};x_{n},s_{n})-(d_{n}+\ell_{n})(y_{n},w_{n}-\epsilon_{n};x_{n},s_{n})\to 0.

Therefore (
15.5
) and the monotonicity of
(d_{n}+\ell_{n})(y_{n},\cdot\;;x_{n},s_{n})
implies that

(d_{n}+\ell_{n})(y_{n},w_{n};x_{n},s_{n})-b_{n}(s_{n}-t_{n})\to 0,

(15.6)

or equivalently,

d_{n}(y_{n},w_{n};x_{n},s_{n})+a_{n}(x_{n}-y_{n})+b_{n}(t_{n}-w_{n})\to 0.

(15.7)

Now, (
15.4
) implies that
(y_{n},w_{n};x_{n},s_{n})\to u=(y,t;x,s)
, so
d_{n}(y_{n},w_{n};x_{n},s_{n})\to d(u)
. Subtracting (
15.7
) from this we get the required convergence

i_{n}(u_{n})=b_{n}w_{n}-b_{n}t_{n}-a_{n}(x_{n}-y_{n})\to d(u)\quad\;\text{as}\;n\to\infty.

For the ‘Moreover’ part of the theorem, we can rewrite
\tilde{h}_{n}
in terms of
i_{n}
as

\tilde{h}_{n}(y,t;x,g)=i_{n}\left(y,-t+\frac{g-a_{n}x}{b_{n}};x,\frac{g-a_{n}x}{b_{n}}\right)+g.

(15.8)

Since
a_{n}/b_{n}\to 0
with
n
,
(g-a_{n}x)/b_{n}\to 0
compactly on
(x,g)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Therefore compact convergence of
i_{n}\to d
implies the compact convergence of
\tilde{h}_{n}
.
∎

When we apply Theorem
15.2
in Section
16
, it will give joint convergence of tasep from a field of narrow wedge initial conditions. To give convergence of tasep from general initial conditions, we will need a few variations.
The first handles initial conditions with compact support.

Theorem 15.3
.

With the Assumptions
15.1
, fix a compact interval
J\subset\mathbb{R}
, and let
f_{n}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
be a sequence of upper semicontinuous functions. Assume that
f_{n}=-\infty
outside
J
, and that
\mathfrak{h}f_{n}\to\mathfrak{h}f
for some upper semicontinuous limit
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
which is not equal to
-\infty
everywhere.
Define

\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;f_{n})=\sup_{x:f_{n}(x)\neq-\infty}\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;x,f_{n}(x)),\qquad h(t,y;f)=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}f(x)+d(y,-t;x,0).

Then

\tilde{h}_{n}\to h
, compactly on
(t,y)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
.

Here recall from Remark
7.4
what it means for
\mathfrak{h}f_{n}\to\mathfrak{h}f
.
Note that for the directed metrics we study in Section
16
, we will be able to relate
\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;f_{n})
to a set interface, using that for nicely behaved metrics
d
and sets
S
, we have

I_{d}(S,t)=\sup_{y\in S}I_{d}(y,t).

This formula does not hold in general.

Proof.

When all of the sets
f_{n}(\mathbb{R})\setminus\{-\infty\}
lie in a common compact set
A
, then the claim follows by the uniform convergence in the ‘Moreover’ part of Theorem
15.2
.

Otherwise, we need to apply a truncation argument. For this, first observe that since
\mathfrak{h}f_{n}\to\mathfrak{h}f
and
f
is upper semicontinuous and never takes on the value
\infty
, and equals
-\infty
off of
J
, that there exists some
a\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f_{n}(x)\leq a
for all
n\in\mathbb{N},x\in\mathbb{R}
.
Next, fix
b>0
, and for any function
f
define

f_{b}^{-}=\begin{cases}f,\qquad&f(x)\geq-b,\\
-\infty,\qquad&f_{n}(x)<-b,\end{cases}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad f_{b}^{+}=\begin{cases}f\vee(-b),\qquad&x\in J,\\
-\infty,\qquad&x\notin J.\end{cases}

With these definitions, all of the sets
(f_{n})_{b}^{-}(\mathbb{R})\setminus\{-\infty\}
and
(f_{n})_{b}^{+}(\mathbb{R})\setminus\{-\infty\}
lie in the compact set
[-b,a]
, so we can apply the theorem to the sequences
(f_{n})_{b}^{-},(f_{n})_{b}^{+}
.

Now, recall that Hausdorff convergence is continuous under finite unions: if
A_{n},B_{n}
are any sequences of closed sets such that
A_{n}\to A
and
B_{n}\to B
in the Hausdorff topology, then
A_{n}\cup B_{n}\to A\cup B
. Therefore as
n\to\infty
,

\mathfrak{h}(f_{n})_{b}^{+}=\mathfrak{h}f_{n}\cup J\times[-\infty,-b]\to\mathfrak{h}f\cup J\times[-\infty,-b]=\mathfrak{h}f_{b}^{+},

so

\tilde{h}_{n}(\;\cdot\;;(f_{n})_{b}^{+})\to h(\;\cdot\;;f_{b}^{+})

(15.9)

compactly on
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
. Handling the lower truncations
(f_{n})^{-}_{b}
is more subtle. Consider any function
g_{b,N}
such that
\mathfrak{h}g_{b,N}
is a subsequential limit of
\mathfrak{h}(f_{n})_{b}^{-}
along some subsequence
N
. Then

\tilde{h}_{n}(\;\cdot\;;(f_{n})_{b}^{-})\to h(\;\cdot\;;g_{b,N})

(15.10)

compactly on
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
along
N
. Next, observe that
f_{b-1}^{-}\leq g_{b,N}\leq f
. Now,

h(\;\cdot\;;f_{b-1}^{-})\leq h(\;\cdot\;;g_{b,N})\leq h(\;\cdot\;;f)\leq h(\;\cdot\;;f_{b}^{+}).

(15.11)

Moreover, for any compact set
K\subset(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, we claim that

h(p;f_{b}^{+})=h(p;f_{b-1}^{-})

(15.12)

on
K
for all large enough
b
. Indeed, (
15.12
) holds if there exists any
s\in\mathbb{R}
with
f(s)>-b+1
, such that for all
p\in K
, we have

f(s)+d(p;s,0)>\sup_{x\in J}-b+d(p;x,0).

This holds for all large enough
b
for any
s
such that
f(s)>-\infty
by the continuity of
d
. The existence of one such
s
follows since
f
is not identically
-\infty
.

Finally, (
15.2
) implies that for any
b
,

\tilde{h}_{n}(\;\cdot\;;(f_{n})_{b}^{-})\leq\tilde{h}_{n}(\;\cdot\;;f_{n})\leq\tilde{h}_{n}(\;\cdot\;;(f_{n})_{b}^{+}).

(15.13)

Combining (
15.9
), (
15.10
), (
15.11
), (
15.12
), and (
15.13
) implies that
\tilde{h}_{n}(\;\cdot\;;f_{n})\to h(\;\cdot\;;f)
compactly.
∎

To handle initial conditions
f_{n},f
with potentially noncompact support, we need an additional tightness condition on
d_{n}
.

Tightness condition 15.4
.

Fix
t>0
. For any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}
, slope and height
s^{\prime},h_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
there exists
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
g_{n}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with
g_{n}(x)\geq s^{\prime}|x|-a
for all
x
and
\sup_{y\in K}\tilde{h}_{n}(y,t;g_{n})\leq h_{*}
for all large enough
n
.

This tightness condition is tailored to be used later in the context of tasep.

Theorem 15.5
.

With the Assumptions
15.1
, suppose that the tightness condition
15.4
holds for some fixed
t>0
. Consider any sequence of initial conditions
f_{n}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
for which there exists
s,a\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f_{n}(x)<a+s|x|
for all large enough
n
. Suppose that
\mathfrak{h}f_{n}\to\mathfrak{h}f
for some upper semicontinuous function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
which is not equal to
-\infty
everywhere. Define

\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;f_{n})=\sup_{x:f_{n}(x)\neq-\infty}\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;x,f_{n}(x)),\qquad h(t,y;f)=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}f(x)+d(y,-t;x,0).

Assume that for every
s^{\prime}>0
and every compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}
,

\sup_{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times K}s^{\prime}|x|+d(y,-t;x,0)<\infty.

(15.14)

Then

\tilde{h}_{n}(t,\;\cdot\;;f_{n})\to h(t,\;\cdot\;;f_{n})
, compactly on
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Fix a compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}
.
First,
f(x)\leq a+s|x|
for all
x
since each
f_{n}
satisfies the same inequality. Therefore by (
15.14
) with
s^{\prime}=s+1
, there exists a compact set
D\subset\mathbb{R}
such that

h(t,y;f)=h(t,y,f_{D}),\qquad y\in K

(15.15)

where
f_{D}
is equal to
f
on
D
and
-\infty
elsewhere. Also, let
x_{0}
be such that
f(x_{0})>-\infty
, and consider a sequence of points
x_{n}\to x_{0}
such that
f_{n}(x_{n})\to f(x_{0})
.
We have

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\inf_{(t,y)\in K}\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;f_{n})\geq\lim_{n\to\infty}\inf_{(t,y)\in K}\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;x_{n},f_{n}(x_{n}))=f(x_{0})+\inf_{y\in K}d(y,-t,x_{0},0),

where the final equality follows from the ‘Moreover’ in Theorem
15.2
. Letting
b
denote the right hand side above, for large enough
n
we have

\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;f_{n})\geq b-1

on
K
. Now let
g_{n}
be as in the tightness condition with
K
as above,
s^{\prime}=s+1
, and
h^{*}=b-2
. Then

\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;f_{n})>\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;g_{n})

on
K
for all large enough
n
, and there exists a compact set
E
containing
D
in its interior such that
g_{n}>f_{n}
on
E^{c}
. Therefore by condition (
15.2
), on
K

\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;f_{n})=\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;f_{n,E}),

(15.16)

where
f_{n,E}
is equal to
f_{n}
on
E
and
-\infty
elsewhere.
Now let
f^{\prime}
be any subsequential limit in the hypograph topology of
f_{n,E}
. By Theorem
15.3
,
\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;f_{n,E})\to h(t,y;f^{\prime})
uniformly on
K
along this subsequence. Since
D
is contained in the interior of
E
, we have
f_{D}\leq f^{\prime}\leq f
, and so by (
15.15
),

h(t,y;f)=h(t,y;f^{\prime})=h(t,y,f_{D}),\qquad y\in K.

(15.17)

Combining (
15.16
) and (
15.17
) yields the theorem.
∎

16
Tasep and the KPZ fixed point

In this section we prove Theorem
1.20
, and a few other convergence results relating tasep and the directed landscape. Let
\mathcal{H}
be the set of functions
h:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
so that
h(0)
is even and
|h(i)-h(i-1)|=1
for all
i
.

Continuous time tasep
is a continuous-time Markov process with state space
\mathcal{H}
. We write
h(t,y;f)
for continuous time tasep at time
t\in[0,\infty)
and location
y\in\mathbb{R}
started from an initial condition
f\in\mathcal{H}
. A continuous time tasep
h
decreases by
2
at each of its local maxima at rate 1. The movements at each of the local maxima are independent.

Continuous time tasep is connected to exponential last passage percolation via the well-known coupling given in the introduction. This coupling can be expressed using the language of interfaces introduced in Section
15
. Let

E^{2}=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:x+y\in 2\mathbb{Z}\}
denote the even lattice points. Let
X
be an array of independent mean 1 exponential random variables indexed by
E^{2}
. Let
d_{X}
be the directed metric of negative sign on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
induced by the function
d_{0}(a,c)=X(c)
, where
d_{0}
is defined for all points
a\in\mathbb{R}^{2},c\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with

a\neq c,\quad|a_{1}-c_{1}|\leq a_{2}-c_{2}.

This directed metric corresponds to a rotated version of the exponential last passage metric in the plane, see Remark
6.5
.

Next, extend all functions in
\mathcal{H}
to functions from
\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
by making them linear on intervals
[i,i+1],i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Recall the definition of the interface
I_{d_{X}}(p,t)
from Section
15
.
By the definition, for any
p\in E^{2}
and
t\geq 0
, we have

I_{d_{X}}(p,t)\in\mathcal{H}
,
and the function

h(t,y;p):=I_{d_{X}}(p,t)(y)

(16.1)

evolves as continuous time tasep started from the
narrow wedge

N^{p}(x)=p_{2}-|x-p_{1}|,

so we may write
h(t,y;N^{p}):=h(t,y;p)
.
We call this collection of coupled processes
h(\cdot;p),p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
the
narrow wedge field
for tasep. For a general initial condition
f\in\mathcal{H}
, the standard variational formula implies that

h(\cdot;f)=\max_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}h(\cdot;(x,f(x))).

(16.2)

evolves as tasep started from
f
. This couples tasep from all initial conditions. In the proofs, it will be convenient to use the formula (
16.2
) with
\mathbb{R}
in place of
\mathbb{Z}
. This changes all values by at most
2
, so will not affect any convergence statements where space is rescaled. We can also use the set
M_{f}
of local maxima of
f
in place of
\mathbb{Z}
without changing the right side of (
16.2
).

16.1
The KPZ fixed point

The KPZ fixed point is a Markov process that describes the scaling limit of tasep from an arbitrary single initial condition. It was first constructed in
Matetski et al. 2016
as the scaling limit of tasep, and later shown in
Nica et al. 2020
to be a function of the directed landscape. The
KPZ fixed point
associated to a directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
is the random function given by

h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;f):=\max_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)+f(x).

The domain of
h_{\mathcal{L}}
is every
t>0,y\in\mathbb{R}
and any function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
that is not identically equal to
-\infty
, and satisfies

f(x)\leq s|x|+a

for some
s,a>0
and all
x
. While weaker conditions on
f
are possible, see
Sarkar and Virág 2020
, we restrict our attention here to functions satisfying the linear growth bound above. By the bound in (
6.8
),
h_{\mathcal{L}}
is almost surely continuous and finite for all valid choices of
t,y,f
.

The main theorem of
Matetski et al. 2016
shows that tasep started from a given initial condition converges after rescaling to the directed landscape, see Theorem
1.19
of Section
1.11
.

Their theorem concerns convergence of hypographs of random upper semicontinuous functions from
\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
. However, Theorem 3.9 in
Matetski et al. 2016
implies that the same distributional convergence holds with the stronger topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.

In the context of tasep, the theorems in Section
15
allow for variations and strengthenings of Theorem
1.19
. In particular, we can prove joint convergence to the KPZ fixed point from multiple initial conditions.

16.2
Convergence of tasep

We first note that rotated exponential last passage still converges to the directed landscape and specify the scaling constants. The following is an application of Corollary
13.2
.

Corollary 16.1
.

Let
d_{n}^{\prime}
be the pullback of
d_{X}
under the linear transformation
K_{n}(x,t)=(2n^{2/3}x,nt)
,
let

\ell_{n}(p,q)=\ell_{n}(q)-\ell_{n}(p)
,

\ell_{n}(x,t)=n^{2/3}t,

and let
d_{n}=n^{-1/3}d_{n}^{\prime}/2-\ell_{n}
. Then
\mathfrak{g}d_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{g}\mathcal{L}
in
\mathcal{E}_{*}
.

Proof.

This is a rotated version of the convergence of exponential last passage, Corollary
13.2
, with parameters
N=n/2
,
m_{N}=N
,
\alpha_{N}=4
,
\beta_{N}=2
,
\chi_{N}=2n^{1/3}
,
\tau_{N}=2n^{2/3}.

∎

Our goal is now to translate the three convergence theorems of Section
15
to tasep. We start with a lemma that checks that the assumptions of Section
15
can be guaranteed to hold on a particular coupling.

Lemma 16.2
.

Let
d_{n},\ell_{n}
be as in Corollary
16.1
, let
a_{n}=0
and
b_{n}=n^{2/3}
, and let
d=\hat{\mathcal{L}}
be a directed landscape.

(i)

With these choices, there of exists a coupling of the exponential environments
X_{n}
for different
n
where
\mathfrak{g}d_{n}\to\mathfrak{g}\hat{\mathcal{L}}
in
\mathcal{E}_{*}
almost surely. In this coupling, Assumptions
15.1
hold almost surely. Moreover, letting
h_{n}(t,y;f)
denote the collection of coupled taseps governed by
X_{n}
, see (
16.1
) and (
16.2
),
the functions
i_{n},\tilde{h}_{n}
defined in Theorem
15.2
are given by

\begin{split}i_{n}(y,t;x,s)&=n^{-1/3}h_{n}(2n(s-t),2n^{2/3}y;2n^{2/3}x,ns)-n^{2/3}t\\
\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;x,g)&=n^{-1/3}h_{n}(2nt,2n^{2/3}y;2n^{2/3}x,n^{1/3}g)+n^{2/3}t.\end{split}

(16.3)

(ii)

For any finite or countable set
T\subset(0,\infty)
and any subsequence
Y\subset\mathbb{N}
, we can find a further subsequence
Y^{\prime}\subset Y
and a coupling of the exponential environments for different
n\in Y^{\prime}
such that both the conclusion of (i) holds a.s. along
Y^{\prime}
and Tightness condition
15.4
holds a.s. along
Y^{\prime}
for all
t\in T
.

Proof.

For (i), by Corollary
16.1
and Skorokhod’s representation theorem there exists a coupling where
\mathfrak{g}d_{n}\to\mathfrak{g}\hat{\mathcal{L}}
in
\mathcal{E}_{*}
almost surely. Let
X_{n}
denote the exponential environment at stage
n
in this coupling. In this coupling,
d_{n}(x,s;y,t)=-\infty
for
s<t
since this property holds for
d_{X}
,
b_{n}\to\infty
and
a_{n}/b_{n}=0
for all
n
, and
d_{n}+\ell_{n}
satisfies (
15.2
) since it is a rescaled version of the
d_{X}
. We now check (
16.3
). With
d_{n}^{\prime}
as in Corollary
16.1
, a directed translation of the formula for
i_{n}
in Theorem
15.2
gives

\begin{split}i_{n}(y,t;x,s)=n^{2/3}I_{2^{-1}n^{-1/3}d_{n}^{\prime}}(x,s;n^{2/3}(s-t))(y)-n^{2/3}t.\end{split}

(16.4)

We move the scaling factor of
2^{-1}n^{-1/3}
in front of
d_{n}^{\prime}
into the
n^{2/3}(s-t)
to get that

i_{n}(y,t;x,s)=n^{2/3}I_{d_{n}^{\prime}}(x,s;2n(s-t))(y)-n^{2/3}t.

(16.5)

To relate this to an interface in
d_{X_{n}}
, we can observe the following simple fact about backwards balls and pullbacks: if
d
is a directed metric of negative sign on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
g_{*}d
is the pullback of
d
under a function
g:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
, then for
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

B_{g_{*}d}(p,t)=g^{-1}B_{d}(gp,t):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:gx\in B_{d}(gp,t)\}.

(16.6)

Using (
16.6
) and the fact that
d_{n}^{\prime}
is the pullback of
d_{X_{n}}
under the map
K_{n}(x,t)(2n^{2/3}x,nt)
, the right side of (
16.5
) equals

n^{2/3}n^{-1}I_{d_{X_{n}}}(2n^{2/3}x,ns;2n(s-t))(2n^{2/3}y)-n^{2/3}t.

By (
16.1
), this is equal to the right hand side of (
16.3
).
Moreover,
i_{n}
is related to
h_{n}
by the formula (
15.8
); the same relationship holds between the two right sides of (
16.3
).

We move to (ii). Fix
Y
. For every
s^{\prime},a\in\mathbb{N}
, we can find a sequence of functions
g^{s^{\prime},a}_{n}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

1.

\mathfrak{h}g^{s^{\prime},a}_{n}\to\mathfrak{h}g^{s^{\prime},a}
, where
g^{s^{\prime},a}(x)=(s^{\prime}+1)|x|-a+1.

2.

(s^{\prime}+2)|x|-a+2\geq g^{s^{\prime},a}_{n}(x)\geq s^{\prime}|x|-a
for all large enough
n
and all
x
, and
J_{n}g^{s^{\prime},a}_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
for all
n
, where
J_{n}f(x)=n^{1/3}f(x/(2n^{2/3}))
.

Fix
T
, and for each
n
, define a function
D_{n}:\mathbb{N}^{3}\times T\to\mathbb{R}
by

D_{n}(m,s^{\prime},a,t):=\sup_{y\in[-m,m]}\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;g^{s^{\prime},a}_{n}).

By the formulas (
16.2
) and (
16.3
),
\tilde{h}_{n}(\cdot;g^{s^{\prime},a}_{n})
is a rescaled and centered tasep from the initial condition
J_{n}g^{s^{\prime},a}_{n}
up to an error of size at most
2n^{-1/3}
(the error comes from going from a maximum over
\mathbb{Z}
to a maximum over
\mathbb{R}
, see the discussion after (
16.2
)). More precisely, for all
t>0,y\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\left|\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;g_{n})-n^{-1/3}(h_{n}(2nt,2n^{2/3}y;J_{n}g^{s^{\prime},a}_{n})-nt)\right|\leq 2n^{-1/3}.

(16.7)

By (
16.7
) and conditions 1 and 2 above, for any fixed
m,s^{\prime},a,t
, the random variables
D_{n}(m,s^{\prime},a,t)
converge in distribution with limits determined by Theorem
1.19
. Therefore
D_{n}
is tight as a function in the pointwise topology, so we can find a subsequence
Y^{\prime}\subset Y
where
D_{n},\mathfrak{g}d_{n}
converge jointly in distribution to
D,\mathfrak{g}\hat{\mathcal{L}}
, where
\hat{\mathcal{L}}
is a directed landscape. By Skorokhod’s representation theorem, we can realize this as almost sure convergence. Now we check Tightness condition
15.4
. Fix
t\in T
, a compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}
, and a slope and height
s^{\prime},h_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
as in the statement of the tightness condition. By possibly increasing
s^{\prime}
and the size of
K
and decreasing
h_{*}
, we may assume
s^{\prime},-h_{*}\in\mathbb{N}
and
K=[-m,m]
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
a\in\mathbb{N}
, by Theorem
1.19
we have

\mathbb{P}(D(m,s^{\prime},a,t)\geq h_{*})=\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{x\in\mathbb{R},y\in[-m,m]}\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)+(s^{\prime}+1)|x|-a+1\geq h_{*}\right).

By (
6.8
), the right hand side above converges to
0
as
a\to\infty
. Therefore there exists a random
A\in\mathbb{N}
such that
D(m,s^{\prime},A,t)<h_{*}
almost surely, and so almost surely,

\sup_{y\in[-m,m]}\tilde{h}_{n}(y,t;g^{s^{\prime},A}_{n})=D_{n}(m,s^{\prime},A,t)<h_{*}

for all large enough
n
. The functions
g^{s^{\prime},A}_{n}
satisfy the criteria of Tightness condition
15.4
, so the condition holds almost surely for the given
s^{\prime},-h_{*},m\in\mathbb{N},t\in T
. Since we only have countably many choices for these parameters, the tightness condition holds almost surely along
Y^{\prime}
for all
t\in T
.
∎

We can now translate the three interface convergence theorems of Section
15
.

Theorem 16.3

(Convergence of the narrow wedge field)
.

In the coupling in Lemma
16.2
(i),
i_{n}\to\hat{\mathcal{L}}
compactly on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
almost surely. Moreover, letting

\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)=\hat{\mathcal{L}}(y,-t;x,-s),

(16.8)

we have
h_{n}(t,y;x,g)\to\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)+g

compactly on
(y,t;x,g)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
almost surely, and
\mathcal{L}
is a directed landscape.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
15.2
and Lemma
16.2
(i). The fact that
\mathcal{L}
is also a directed landscape follows from Proposition
14.1
.
∎

For these next two theorems, we use the notation
\iota_{n}f(x)=n^{-1/3}f(2n^{2/3}x)
from the introduction. The first theorem gives a strong convergence which is uniform in time, but only deals with compactly supported initial conditions.

Theorem 16.4

(Compactly supported initial conditions)
.

Let
f_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
. Suppose that there exists a compact set
I
, such that for every
n
, the set of local maxima
M_{\iota_{n}f_{n}}
of
\iota_{n}f_{n}
is contained in
I
.
Assume that
\mathfrak{h}\iota_{n}f_{n}\to\mathfrak{h}f
for some upper semicontinuous limit
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
with
f_{n}\not\equiv-\infty
. Then in the coupling of Lemma
16.2
(i) with
\mathcal{L}
as in (
16.8
), almost surely we have

n^{-1/3}(h_{n}(2nt,2n^{2/3}y;f_{n})+nt)\to h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y,f)

(16.9)

compactly on
(t,y)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Using the notation
\tilde{h}_{n}
from Lemma
16.2
, since all the local maxima of
\iota_{n}f_{n}
are contained in
I
the left side of (
16.9
) equals

\max_{x\in M_{\iota_{n}f_{n}}}\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;x,\iota_{n}f_{n}(x))=\max_{x\in I}\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;x,\iota_{n}f_{n}(x))+O(n^{-1/3}).

Here the
O(n^{-1/3})
correction comes from replacing the maximum over points of local maxima with a larger set, see the discussion after (
16.2
). In the notation of Theorem
15.3
, the right side above equals
\tilde{h}_{n}(t,y;g_{n})+O(n^{-1/3})
, where
g_{n}
equals
\iota_{n}f_{n}
on
I
and
-\infty
elsewhere. Now,
\mathfrak{h}g_{n}\to\mathfrak{h}f
since
\mathfrak{h}\iota_{n}f_{n}\to\mathfrak{h}f
. Therefore applying Theorem
15.3
and Lemma
16.2
(i) yields the theorem.
∎

The next theorem is a restatement of Theorem
1.20
.

Theorem 16.5

(Noncompact initial conditions)
.

Fix a finite or countable set
T\subset(0,\infty)
. Then there is a coupling of a sequence
h_{n}
of coupled taseps and
\mathcal{L}
, and an event
\Omega
of probability
1
with the following property. Consider any collection of functions
f_{n}\in\mathcal{H}
satisfying

1.

\mathfrak{h}\iota_{n}f_{n}\to\mathfrak{h}f
for some
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
with
f\not\equiv-\infty
, and

2.

there exists
s,a\in\mathbb{R}
such that for all
x
,
f_{n}(x)<a+s|x|
for all large enough
n
.

Then for any
t\in T
, on
\Omega
we have

n^{-1/3}(h_{n}(2nt,2n^{2/3}y;f_{n})+nt)\to h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;f)

compactly over
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

First, for any subsequence
Y\subset\mathbb{N}
, there exists a further subsequence
Y^{\prime}\subset Y
and a coupling of
h_{n},n\in Y^{\prime},\mathcal{L}
such that the result holds along this subsequence. Indeed, this follows from Theorem
15.5
and Lemma
16.2
(ii). The fact that condition (
15.14
), which is required for Theorem
15.5
, holds almost surely for
\mathcal{L}
follows from (
6.8
).

Now, in the coupling in Lemma
16.2
(ii), the sequence of rescaled exponential last passage metrics
d_{n}
satisfies
\mathfrak{g}d_{n}\to\mathfrak{g}\hat{\mathcal{L}}
almost surely, where
\mathcal{L},\hat{\mathcal{L}}
are related via (
16.8
). Also, with
D_{n}
as in the proof of Lemma
16.2
, in this coupling we have that almost surely,

D_{n}(m,s^{\prime},a,t)\to D(m,s^{\prime},a,t):=\max_{x\in\mathbb{R},y\in[-m,m]}\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)+(s^{\prime}+1)|x|-a+1.

for all
m,s^{\prime},a\in\mathbb{N},t\in T
. Recall also that
d_{n}\to\hat{\mathcal{L}}
along this subsequence. Since the joint distribution of
D,\hat{\mathcal{L}}
is independent of
Y,Y^{\prime}
, this implies that the whole sequence
(D_{n},\mathfrak{g}d_{n}),n\in\mathbb{N}
converges in distribution to
(D,\mathfrak{g}\hat{\mathcal{L}})
. Therefore we can apply the argument of the proof of Lemma
16.2
(ii) without first passing to a subsequence, to get that Lemma
16.2
(ii) holds with
Y^{\prime}=Y=\mathbb{N}
, and hence so does Theorem
16.5
.
∎

Remark 16.6
.

Here we have focused on convergence of continuous-time tasep to the KPZ fixed point by coupling with exponential last passage percolation. We can also consider discrete-time tasep.

Discrete-time tasep
with parameter
p\in(0,1)
is a discrete time Markov chain
D(t,\cdot;h_{0})
defined on the same state space
\mathcal{H}
as continuous-time tasep. At each time,
D(t+1,\cdot;h_{0})
is obtained from
D(t,\cdot;h_{0})
by independently decreasing all local maxima by
2
with probability
p
. Discrete-time tasep can be coupled to last passage percolation with geometric weights in the same way that continuous-time tasep can be coupled to last passage percolation with exponential weights. The correct geometric weights have mean
p^{-1}
and take values in
\{1,2,\dots\}
. This corresponds to the
\gamma=p^{-1}-1
and
m_{n}=n
case of Theorem
13.1
; note that in that theorem we worked with geometric random variables on
\{0,1,2,\dots\}
.

We can then use Theorem
13.1
to conclude convergence of discrete-time tasep to the KPZ fixed point
h_{\mathcal{L}}
, just as in Theorems
16.3
,
16.4
, and
16.5
. The rescaled version that converges to
h_{\mathcal{L}}(t,y;f)(x)
is

D_{n}(t,y;f):=c_{1}n^{-1/3}D\left(c_{3}nt,c_{2}n^{2/3}y;J_{n}f\right)+c_{1}n^{2/3}t.

Here
J_{n}f(x):=c_{1}^{-1}n^{1/3}f(x/(c_{2}n^{2/3}))
, and
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
are
p
-dependent constants given by

c_{1}=\frac{2^{1/3}(1-\sqrt{q})^{3}}{p^{1/3}q^{1/6}},\qquad c_{2}=\frac{2^{1/3}(1+\sqrt{q})^{2/3}}{q^{1/6}},\qquad c_{3}=\frac{1+\sqrt{q}}{p},

where
q=1-p
.
The rescaled narrow wedge field that converges to
\mathcal{L}
(i.e. the analogue of
i_{n}
in (
16.3
)) is

i_{n}(y,t;x,s):=c_{1}n^{-1/3}D_{n}\left(c_{3}n(s-t),c_{2}n^{2/3}y;c_{2}n^{2/3}x,ns\right)-c_{1}n^{2/3}t.

Remark 16.7
.

We have only considered tasep with unsloped initial conditions (i.e. initial conditions
f_{n}(x)
that converge after rescaling without subtracting an
x
-dependent linear term). One can also consider both discrete and continuous-time tasep with initial conditions with a nonzero slope
\rho\in(-1,1)
. Tasep with such initial conditions also converges to the KPZ fixed point. The same methods apply for proving convergence, though one needs to be more careful about tracking certain linear transformations. In this context, the right last passage percolation convergence result to apply is Theorem
13.1
or Corollary
13.2
with
(1+\rho)m_{n}=(1-\rho)n
.
